California Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete, Parts 0–12

Last audited: 1 November 2026. Cut-off: 1 November 2026. No 3 November 2026 election result is included anywhere in this file.

What this audited file is. This is the original Complete Parts 0–12 report, with audit corrections applied in place. Four section passes audited 186 material claim groups: Parts 0–3: 43 groups (Confirmed 15, Corrected 8, Removed 0, Unverified 20); Parts 4–6: 57 groups (Confirmed 20, Corrected 13, Removed 3, Unverified 21); Parts 7–9: 38 groups (Confirmed 14, Corrected 6, Removed 0, Unverified 18); Parts 10–12 plus federal baseline: 48 groups (Confirmed 3, Corrected 8, Removed 0, Unverified 37). Totals: Confirmed 52, Corrected 35, Removed 3, Unverified 96. That is a material-claim audit, not a sentence-by-sentence verification of every sentence in a 535,000-character file — a claim that this file is fully settled would be false, and the Verify flags that remain are part of the audit result, not leftovers to ignore.

Federal baseline — applied as given, not re-audited. Schedule III covers FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana only (91 FR 22714, effective 28 April 2026); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I and fully subject to IRC §280E; Treasury guidance on 280E is forthcoming, not final. Dual A/M holders must split activity — separate A and M licences, premises records, inventory and tax reporting — and only the M portion is eligible. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized; the full total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. No statement in Parts 0–12 was found applying Schedule III or 280E relief to adult-use. Whether any tribal medical programme counts as “state-licensed” under 91 FR 22714 is Verify — no federal-rule text quoting the definition was opened, and a tribal entity holding a DCC licence off-reservation does not answer the tribal-programme question.

Headline corrections the reader should see first. (1) Excise tax: the statute controls — 15% from 1 October 2025 through 30 June 2028, next adjustment FY 2028–29 capped at 19% (AB 564, Ch. 127, Stats. 2025); the CDTFA page wording “through June 30, 2029” is not the statutory end date, and AB 8 is not the source of the 15% rate. (2) Type 13 numbering is dual: BPC §26050(a) lists Type 13 as cannabis event organizer, while DCC operational numbering and the §15014 fee schedule use Type 13 for transport-only distributor — print the two systems separately, never as one merged table. Type 12 is microbusiness; Type 9 is a DCC operational non-storefront retailer type not in the §26050(a) list; Type 14 processor is live; Type 15 is not a current standalone licence. (3) DCC-2025-01-R (approved/filed 28 April 2026, effective 1 July 2026) is no longer “pending”: it deleted electricity/GHG reporting and carbon-offset purchase and the application-stage pest-management plan, and extended temporary events from 4 days to 30 days. (4) AB 1397 is dead (31 January 2026); AB 1564 was gutted of its cannabis language on 25 February 2026; AB 2506 was held under submission in August 2026 and is not enacted. (5) Tribal hemp: nine California tribal plans are Approved on the USDA AMS table (updated 17 February 2026) — Cahuilla, La Jolla, Mechoopda, Pala, Pauma, Santa Rosa, Soboba, Torres Martinez, Yurok — plus Colorado River Indian Tribes, which spans Arizona/California and must not be counted as solely California; Ysleta del Sur Pueblo is not a California tribe. (6) Part 8 ZERO means zero properties meeting the strict standard on evidence opened, not proof that none exists — the DCC licence-search cross-check was not completed for any candidate. (7) Removed as final figures: Q4 2024 $219m as a final total; CannBus MMIC FY 2024–25 1,047 (CDPH: 2,896); the “67 growers / 4,687 acres” figure as a CDFA figure (it conflicts with IHAB 8,259.7 registered acres). (8) No individual licence-holder phone number or email is reproduced in this file; the only numbers/emails shown are programme or public-office contacts.

How to read the rest. Corrections are marked “[AUDIT CORRECTION]” in place and collected in the Audit Table below. Verify/Unavailable flags that remain are unresolved after this audit — do not treat them as confirmed. Where the body still discusses a conflict (e.g. the old 48% DCC-map rendering, the $8.4bn vs ~$7.9bn cumulative-revenue arithmetic, the 218,000 vs ~200,000 AB 1793 counts, the 2-year vs 3-year USPS retention conflict), the conflict is preserved, not smoothed, and the audit table says which side, if any, the primary source supports.

Part 4 — Types“Type 13 = transport-only distributor” as the single numberingCorrectedhttps://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displayText.xhtml?article&chapter=5.&division=10.&lawCode=BPC ; https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2026/02/Annual-Legislative-Report-FY24-25.pdf ; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15014Print two systems separately: BPC §26050(a)(21) lists Type 13 as cannabis event organizer; DCC operations and the §15014 fee schedule use Type 13 for transport-only distributor and list event organizer without that number. Part 4 — CultivationMixed-light Tier 1 vs Tier 2 wattage threshold flagged VerifyCorrectedhttps://cannabis.ca.gov/resources/data-dashboard/data-dashboard-glossary/Tier 1 = artificial light >0 to ≤6 watts/sq ft; Tier 2 = >6 to ≤25 watts/sq ft. Indoor is >25 watts/sq ft.

A Large holder may hold 6/7/10, not 8/11/12.

Only local-equity applicants for retailer activities may receive/renew provisional licences under BPC §26050.5 (SB 51, Ch. 593, Stats. 2023) until 1 January 2031 or five years from original issue, whichever is earlier. “Equity applicants” without the retailer limit is overbroad.
Part 4 — MicrobusinessMicrobusiness “at least three of four activities” presented as the statutory definitionCorrectedhttps://www.cannabis.ca.gov/applicants/license-types/microbusiness-license-types/ ; BPC §26001 via reproduction onlyPrint both, labelled: BPC §26001 (as reproduced) defines microbusiness as cultivation <10,000 sq ft AND distributor AND Level 1 manufacturer AND retailer. The DCC “at least three” practice (≤10,000 sq ft cultivation) is broader than that statutory text. Do not present the DCC version as the statute.
Part 4/11 — A/M splitJune 2026 emergency rule letting combined A/M retailers split — date/mechanism flagged VerifyCorrectedhttps://www.cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/rulemaking/dcc-2026-03-e/final-text/ ; https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/rulemaking/Mechanism confirmed: DCC-2026-03-E adds §15023.1 and §15000.2(b) — a combined retail licence may be modified to a single A-licence plus a new M-licence (separate entities, same owners/responsible party, physically separated goods, separate records, joint liability; M fee waived until renewal). Emergency approval/filing 4 June 2026, expiry 2 December 2026; permanent DCC-2026-04-R noticed 30 October 2026, comments through 14 December 2026 (hearing 15 December 2026 Verify). §11.9 must add the split instruction: separate licences, records, inventory and tax reporting; A/M goods travelling together if marked “A”/“M” is not co-mingled tax treatment.

Packaging section numbering (§§17403–17412) stays Verify.
Part 11 — Tax ops§11.9 tax operations with no A/M split instructionCorrected (gap)Given federal baselineAdd: a dual A/M holder must keep separate A and M licences, premises records, inventory and tax reporting, and only the M portion is eligible for Schedule III/280E treatment. §11.4 notes A/M goods may travel together if marked “A”/“M” — reconcile, do not present co-transport as co-mingled tax treatment. DCC-2025-01-R / DCC-2026-03-R material states plant tags are not differentiated A/M — the federal split is a records/inventory split, not a tag distinction.

The 1 October 2026 pesticide-testing change post-dates the report’s tables and may supersede §15719 figures. ILRC February 2026 argument that post-9 November 2016 California convictions may not meet the federal cannabis definition is an advocate argument, not a safe harbour.

  • Cut-off 1 November 2026; date accessed 1 November 2026. No 3 November 2026 election result is included. Research dated 1 November 2026 does not contain any 3 November 2026 outcome — including any Houston or Idaho measure — and that cut-off is stated, not filled in by guesswork.

California licenses medical (M-type) and adult-use (A-type) under one system and most licensees hold both — dual holders must split medical from adult-use activity. Tribal Schedule III “state-licensed” question remains Verify (see Parts 7 and 10).

Contents: Part 0 Headlines Part 1 Laws Part 2 Taxes Part 3 Local Rules Part 4 Licence Types Part 5 Licence Data Sources Part 6 Market Data Part 7 Hemp Licences Part 8 Cannabis-Zoned Properties Part 9 Regulators and History Part 10 Tribal and Interstate Part 11 Operations Rules Part 12 Consumer and Social Rules Research Notes

Federal baseline — fixed context, not re-researched

As given for this commission, and used throughout as fixed context:

  • Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.
  • IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.
  • Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.
  • There is no federal cannabis banking law.
California application of the split — read this first. California licenses medical (M-type) and adult-use (A-type) activity under one system (MAUCRSA, Business and Professions Code Division 10), and most licensees hold both designations. Only activity under a state-licensed M designation can claim Schedule III / §280E relief federally. A dual A/M holder must split its activity — separate A and M licences, premises records, inventory and tax reporting — and only the M portion is eligible. All adult-use activity remains Schedule I and fully subject to §280E. See Part 1.0 and Part 2.6. Tribal Verify: no source opened in Run 1 establishes that any California tribe operates a medical programme that counts as “state-licensed” for federal Schedule III purposes. If any tribe operates a medical programme, whether it counts as “state-licensed” is Verify throughout Run 1; Part 10 must resolve it from official tribal/government sources only. California hemp interaction: California’s consumable-hemp rule (no detectable THC per serving, 21+, ≤5 servings per package, then AB 8’s >99% CBD/CBN, no-THC extract rule from 1 January 2026) is already stricter than the federal post-11 December 2026 0.4 mg/container cap. The federal dates therefore do not loosen California retail. No California statute or regulation specifically responding to the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal dates was located in the sources opened — Verify DCC/CDPH/CDFA guidance after 1 November 2026. Sources: CDTFA cannabis retailers page, cdtfa.ca.gov/industry/cannabis/retailers.htm, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC hemp page, cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/hemp/, accessed 1 Nov 2026; CDPH AB 8 FAQ, cdph.ca.gov — AB 8 FAQ, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

PART 0 — HEADLINES

0.1 Market: value down, volume up, prices collapsed from peak

ERA Economics, California Cannabis Market Outlook 2024 Report, prepared for DCC (January 2025), as summarised in AB 564 legislative analyses: cannabis prices peaked in Q3 2021 and have declined since; “This downward trend is driven entirely by prices – quantity sold, in terms of flower weight, and units of edibles and vape cartridges, has continued to increase”; wholesale flower prices “dropped dramatically from the peak in 2020.” Licensed production increased 11.8% to 1.4 million pounds in 2024; nominal wholesale prices decreased 3.9% for 2024 but were 4.8% higher year-over-year in Q4 2024; gross value of the industry increased 7.5% in 2024. Inflation-adjusted wholesale prices in Q4 2024 were down 57% against Q4 2020 in ERA reporting via secondary summaries — outdoor down 74%, indoor down 46%. Nominal wholesale flower averages reproduced from ERA Table 2 in a secondary compilation: 2020 $1,377/lb; 2021 $1,297/lb; 2022 $791/lb; 2023 $750/lb; 2024 $721/lb. Verify the ERA PDF itself (Table 2) before reusing the price series as official. Sources: AB 564 analyses via Capitol Track, AB 564 Senate analysis, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY summary of an official-commissioned report); secondary compilation cannbus.org — California market report, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Illicit market still about 60% of consumption in California, ERA 2024 report as quoted in AB 564 / AB 8 analyses. Source: AB 8 analysis, accessed 1 Nov 2026; Capitol Weekly, 13 Nov 2025 (SECONDARY, quoting DCC). See Part 6.3 for the 38–40% licensed / 60–62% unlicensed range and why a single precise figure is not used.

0.2 Tax headline: 15% → 19% for one quarter → 15% again

CDTFA, opened directly: “From July 1, 2025, through September 30, 2025, the cannabis excise tax rate was 19 percent” and “Beginning October 1, 2025, through June 30, 2028 [AUDIT: resolved — statute controls], the cannabis excise tax rate is 15 percent.” As required by Revenue and Taxation Code §34011.2, CDTFA adjusted the rate for FY 2025–26 by an additional 4% to generate the estimated cultivation-tax revenue that would have been collected if the cultivation tax had not ended, so on 1 July 2025 the rate rose from 15% to 19%; AB 564 (Stats. 2025, ch. 127, signed 22 September 2025) amended §34011.2 to reduce the rate and delay another adjustment until FY 2028–29, so the rate returned to 15% effective 1 October 2025. Conflict / Verify: the CDTFA page wording “through June 30, 2028 [AUDIT: resolved — statute controls]” vs. the AB 564 statute’s FY 2028–29 adjustment (capped at 19%) must be resolved on the current §34011.2 text before publication; do not silently harmonise them. No rollback beyond AB 564 was found as of 1 Nov 2026. Source: CDTFA cannabis retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (OFFICIAL, opened).

0.3 Licences: provisional system ended January 2026; almost 8,000 annual licences

DCC, January 2026: “January 2026 marked the close of California’s provisional commercial cannabis licensing program, with the exception of locally verified equity retailers” and “We now have almost 8,000 annual licenses.” Since DCC’s inception, 6,419 provisional licences transitioned to annual, including 977 equity retail licences and 3,614 cultivation licences. State Auditor Report 2025-048 (20 Nov 2025): provisional licences in the 17 Local Jurisdiction Assistance Grant jurisdictions fell from more than 4,600 in January 2023 to fewer than 300 as of 30 June 2025; state law requires most businesses to obtain annual licences by 1 January 2026 and provisional licences “will no longer be effective after that date,” except the equity-retailer exception. AB 564 analysis: “In 2024, active cannabis licenses decreased by 18% and retail sales value decreased by 4.4%, all while the illicit market retained its stronghold.” Conflict: a secondary 2026 compilation claims “8,252 active licenses” — inconsistent in precision with DCC’s “almost 8,000” and not adopted. Sources: DCC newsletter via mailchi.mp/cannabis.ca.gov newsletter, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (Verify against cannabis.ca.gov press-release archive — the URL slug is odd); State Auditor Report 2025-048, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (OFFICIAL); AB 564 analysis above.

0.4 Local access and bans — headline

State law expressly preserves local power to “completely prohibit the establishment or operation of one or more types of businesses licensed under this division within the local jurisdiction,” except as set forth in the Medicinal Cannabis Patients’ Right of Access Act, BPC §§26320–26325. Verbatim text in Part 1.6 / Part 3.1. DCC, February 2026: 256 of 540 cities and counties (47%) allow at least one type; 284 (53%) allow none; 302 (56%) allow no retail. Medicinal delivery cannot be banned in a way that prohibits timely, readily accessible medicinal delivery (SB 1186, 2022). Whether adult-use delivery into a banning jurisdiction is protected on the same footing is Verify — see Part 3.1.5 for the §26090(f) public-roads distinction. Sources: BPC §26200(a)(1) via california.public.law §26200, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC, Where cannabis businesses are allowed, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

0.5 Hemp intoxicating products: California was already stricter, then AB 8 codified and extended it

CDPH emergency regulations DPH-24-005, effective 23 September 2024: industrial hemp food, food additives, beverages and dietary supplements for human consumption must have “no detectable THC per serving,” cannot be sold to purchasers under 21, and may have no more than 5 servings per package. Regular rulemaking: OAL approved the regular rulemaking on 20 October 2025; a further serving-size/age rulemaking was approved 21 April 2026. AB 8 (Aguiar-Curry), Chapter 248, Statutes of 2025, signed 2 October 2025, signed/chaptered 2 October 2025, operative in the main 1 January 2026 [AUDIT CORRECTION: 1 January 2026 is the operative date, not the signing date], “strengthens enforcement by regulating intoxicating hemp products as cannabis” and “preserves access to CBD and CBN.” DCC hemp page, opened: California prohibits sale of alcoholic beverages including hemp cannabinoids; inhalable hemp products such as pre-rolls, vape cartridges and integrated vaporizers; hemp flower; products with synthetic cannabinoids; products made outside the regulated market. From 1 January 2026 state/local agencies have new enforcement powers including taking products off shelves and issuing fines. From 1 January 2028 intoxicating hemp must be manufactured, tested and sold just like medicinal/adult-use cannabis and will pay the same taxes. Sources: CDPH DPH-24-005, accessed 1 Nov 2026; CDPH AB 8 FAQ, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC hemp page, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

0.6 Illicit-market enforcement: UCETF seizures surged in 2025

Unified Cannabis Enforcement Taskforce (UCETF), launched 2022 by Governor Newsom, co-chaired by DCC and CDFW, coordinated by Cal OES. Governor announcement, 21 Oct 2025, DCC: Q3 2025 alone, seizure/destruction of over $222 million in illegal cannabis. UCETF page snapshot and a January 2026 Governor republication conflict on county count (29 vs 36 counties) and cumulative totals — conflict flagged, not resolved; see Part 5.6 for the October 2026 figures (through September 2026: 139,293 lb, >$230 million; since 2022: 896,514 lb, 1,441,826 plants, >$1.47 billion estimated value) and their period caveats. Sources: DCC UCETF page, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC, 21 Oct 2025, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

0.7 Consumption cafés: AB 1775 state permission, local opt-in

AB 1775 (Haney), signed 30 September 2024, effective 1 January 2025, amends BPC §26200: local jurisdictions may authorise licensed retailers/microbusinesses with approved consumption areas to prepare/sell non-cannabis food and non-alcoholic beverages and host live musical/other performances and sell tickets. It is permissive; it does not require any locality to authorise cafés. San Francisco: Board voted 7–4 on 14 July 2026; Mayor signed 23 July 2026; effective 23 August 2026; distinct cannabis-café permit; first year only existing licensed retailers/equity partners may apply; no alcohol, no tobacco; café cannabis is for on-site consumption only and cannot leave — Verify against sfgov.org / SF Office of Cannabis ordinance text (SECONDARY for SF facts). Palm Springs: already permitted lounges under Municipal Code Ch. 5.55; staff report states six operational lounges, with additional approved facilities pending, as of 8 July 2026; the 2026 amendment aligns Ch. 5.55 with AB 1775. Sources: Palm Springs staff report 8 July 2026 via palmsprings.primegov.com, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (LOCAL OFFICIAL staff report, not ordinance text); Respect My Region, SF cafés, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY).

PART 1 — LAWS

1.0 How to read this part — federal Schedule III split, California’s single system, tribal Verify

California’s structure: MAUCRSA creates one licensing system in which every non-laboratory licence carries an adult-use (“A”) designation, a medicinal (“M”) designation, or both (BPC §26001 definitions; DCC rulemaking notice Oct 2026: “every non-laboratory cannabis license must carry an [A] designation, an [M] designation, or both” — SECONDARY report of DCC notice, cannabisnews.org, accessed 1 Nov 2026).

What Schedule III changes, and what it does not: Only cannabis activity conducted under a state-licensed medical licence (plus FDA-approved products) moved to Schedule III on 28 April 2026. CDTFA, opened directly, states: “medical marijuana and medical marijuana products … regulated by a state medicinal cannabis licensee has been moved from Schedule I to Schedule III” and the order “also establish[es] a new pathway for state-licensed medicinal cannabis entities seeking federal registration with [DEA] as manufacturers, distributors, and dispensers.” Adult-use activity remains Schedule I and fully subject to federal §280E. A dual A/M holder therefore cannot treat its whole business as Schedule III: it must split activity — separate A and M licences, separate premises records, separate inventory, separate tax reporting — and only the M portion can claim Schedule III / §280E relief federally. Source: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (OFFICIAL).

DCC emergency split mechanism, DCC-2026-03-E, 4 June 2026: a retailer holding one licence with both designations may now hold two separate licences on the same premises in two different legal entities if conditions are met; the two are “considered to be one licensee for compliance and enforcement purposes” and are jointly and severally liable for each other’s obligations, debts and violations. The designated responsible party files DCC Form 9207 with the new M-entity’s formation documents, FEIN and CDTFA seller’s-permit number; DCC acts within five business days; legacy inventory stays under the A-licence’s track-and-trace account; the M-licence fee is due before inventory can transfer. As of 18 May 2026 DCC had processed 10 designation changes to M-only and 196 additions of M to an existing A; by the October 2026 permanent-rulemaking notice, 95 businesses had obtained separate A and M licences for existing premises since the emergency rules took effect. Emergency rules expire 2 December 2026; permanent proposal DCC-2026-04-R was noticed 30 October 2026, comments due 14 December 2026, hearing 15 December 2026. The DCC emergency text itself (4 CCR §§15000.1(e), 15000.2(b)–(c), 15023.1) was NOT opened in Run 1 — Verify against cannabis.ca.gov rulemaking page / DCC-2026-03-E final text before quoting it verbatim. Sources: Shay Gilmore Law, A/M split, accessed 1 Nov 2026; CDTFA, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY for the emergency-rule summary).

CDTFA consequence, opened directly: “Each entity licensed as a cannabis retailer will continue to file returns and report gross receipts on their retail sales of adult-use and medicinal cannabis” and “If separate A or M designated licenses are issued to separate entities operating at the same location, such each entity needs its own seller’s permit and cannabis retailer excise tax permit.” Register each DCC licence authorising retail sales as a separate location under the excise-tax permit, including a medicinal-only designation licence at the same location as an adult-use licence. Source: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Track-and-trace across the A/M split is unwritten: secondary analysis (Oct 2026) states conforming regulations “do not yet exist for track-and-trace across the two co-located entities.” Legacy inventory stays under the A account per the emergency summary. Flag as a live compliance risk for dual holders, not as settled law. Source: Shay Gilmore Law, 280E entity choice, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY).

1.1 Legal status for adults — what is legal

Both medical and adult-use are legal in California. Medical since Proposition 215 (1996); adult-use since Proposition 64 (8 November 2016), first licensed adult-use sales 1 January 2018.

Adult-use personal rights — HSC §11362.1 (Proposition 64 / AUMA). Verbatim operative text, as reproduced in the DSH policy manual quoting the statute and in search extracts (leginfo page for §11362.1 was not opened in Run 1 — Verify verbatim against leginfo before final publication):

“(a) Subject to Sections 11362.2, 11362.3, 11362.4, and 11362.45, but notwithstanding any other provision of law, it shall be lawful under state and local law, and shall not be a violation of state or local law, for persons 21 years of age or older to: (1) Possess, process, transport, purchase, obtain, or give away to persons 21 years of age or older without any compensation whatsoever, not more than 28.5 grams of cannabis not in the form of concentrated cannabis; (2) Possess, process, transport, purchase, obtain, or give away to persons 21 years of age or older without any compensation whatsoever, not more than eight grams of cannabis in the form of concentrated cannabis, including as contained in cannabis products; (3) Possess, plant, cultivate, harvest, dry, or process not more than six living cannabis plants [per private residence — see §11362.2 below]; …”

Official link pattern: leginfo — HSC §11362.1 (pattern verified from the opened §11362.5 page: leginfo — HSC §11362.5), accessed 1 Nov 2026. Sources for the 28.5 g / 8 g limits: DSH policy manual, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Possession over the limit: HSC §11357 penalties were not opened — Verify. Secondary summaries state over-limit possession for adults 21+ is generally an infraction/misdemeanour depending on amount and priors; do not publish a penalty table without the §11357 text. Gifting: lawful only without any compensation whatsoever, only to persons 21+, and only within the 28.5 g / 8 g limits. Any compensation makes it a sale requiring a licence. Public use: HSC §11362.3 — §11362.1 “does not permit any person to: (1) Smoke or ingest cannabis or cannabis products in a public place, except in accordance with Section 26200 of the [BPC]” (i.e. except in a locally authorised on-site consumption area / café). Public-place smoking/ingestion is an infraction, $100 fine in secondary summaries — Verify §11362.3 text and fine on leginfo. Vehicle rules: HSC §11362.45 preserves laws making it unlawful to drive/operate a vehicle, boat, vessel or aircraft while smoking/ingesting cannabis or while impaired, and preserves employer drug-free-workplace authority. Open-container / vehicle-possession detail under Vehicle Code §23222 was not opened — Verify; do not state a vehicle open-container rule without it.

1.2 Every major statute and regulation

Last-amended dates: leginfo pages show amendment history at the foot of each section. Where a page was not opened, the date is given as Verify rather than guessed.

Compassionate Use Act of 1996 — Proposition 215, approved 5 Nov 1996. HSC §11362.5 — leginfo, OPENED 1 Nov 2026Seriously ill Californians with a physician’s recommendation may obtain/use marijuana for medical purposes without criminal prosecution/sanction; physicians may not be punished for recommending. No card required to claim the protection.“(a) This section shall be known and may be cited as the Compassionate Use Act of 1996.” … “(d) Section 11357, relating to the possession of marijuana, and Section 11358, relating to the cultivation of marijuana, shall not apply to a patient, or to a patient’s primary caregiver, who possesses or cultivates marijuana for the personal medical purposes of the patient upon the written or oral recommendation or approval of a physician.” … “(e) … ‘primary caregiver’ means the individual designated by the person exempted under this section who has consistently assumed responsibility for the housing, health, or safety of that person.” Findings at (b)(1)(A)–(C): right to obtain/use where a physician has determined health would benefit from use in treatment of “cancer, anorexia, AIDS, chronic pain, spasticity, glaucoma, arthritis, migraine, or any other illness for which marijuana provides relief”; patients/caregivers “not subject to criminal prosecution or sanction.”Added 1996; heading amended Stats. 2017, ch. 27, §121, per opened page. 1.3 Possession, home grow, public use, vehicles, gifting, product types and THC limits

Possession: 28.5 g non-concentrated cannabis; 8 g concentrated cannabis including as contained in cannabis products — HSC §11362.1(a)(1)–(2) (1.1).

Home grow — HSC §11362.2. Verbatim (FindLaw reproduction, corroborated by search extracts — Verify on leginfo):

“(a) Personal cultivation … is subject to the following restrictions: (1) A person shall plant, cultivate, harvest, dry, or process plants in accordance with local ordinances, if any, adopted in accordance with subdivision (b). (2) The living plants and any cannabis produced by the plants in excess of 28.5 grams are kept within the person’s private residence, or upon the grounds of that private residence (e.g., in an outdoor garden area), are in a locked space, and are not visible by normal unaided vision from a public place. (3) Not more than six living plants may be planted, cultivated, harvested, dried, or processed within a single private residence, or upon the grounds of that private residence, at one time.” “(b)(2) Notwithstanding paragraph (1), a city, county, or city and county shall not completely prohibit persons engaging in [personal cultivation] inside a private residence, or inside an accessory structure … that is fully enclosed and secure.”

i.e. localities may regulate/ban outdoor home grows but may not completely prohibit indoor private-residence grows. Subdivision (b)(3)–(4) in the extract: the six-plant paragraph becomes inoperative upon an AG determination that adult use is lawful under federal law — that determination has not occurred for adult-use (still Schedule I), so the limit stands. “Private residence” = house, apartment unit, mobile home or other similar dwelling (§11362.2(b)(5) in extract). Medical home grow is different: Prop 215 patients/caregivers may cultivate amounts reasonably related to medical need; the SB 420 safe harbour is 6 mature OR 12 immature plants per patient (see 1.4 and Kelly caveat). Do not conflate the adult-use 6-living-plants-per-residence rule with the medical per-patient rule.

Public consumption: prohibited except in locally authorised on-site consumption areas (HSC §11362.3; BPC §26200(g)) — 1.1. Vehicles: HSC §11362.45 preserves driving-impairment laws; smoking/ingesting while driving/operating is not permitted by §11362.1 — full Vehicle Code detail Verify. Gifting: without compensation, 21+ only, within limits — 1.1.

Product types allowed: flower, edibles, vapes, concentrates, beverages, topicals, tinctures/capsules (orally consumed concentrates), and animal products under MAUCRSA/BPC §26000(b)(2) — all only through DCC licensees, with testing, packaging and labelling (1.6) and track-and-trace (1.7).

THC limits — 4 CCR §17304 (DCC, Title 4 Div. 19, Ch. 10 Art. 2), verbatim from Justia/Cornell reproductions of the regulation (current through Register 2025 No. 26, 27 June 2025 in the Justia extract — Verify current Register):

“(a) An edible cannabis product shall not contain more than: (1) 10 milligrams THC per serving; and (2) 100 milligrams THC per package. (b) Notwithstanding subsection (a), a package containing an edible product that is an orally dissolving product, such as sublingual lozenges or mouth strips, may contain up to 500 milligrams THC per package, if: (1) The cannabis product consists of discrete servings of no more than 10 milligrams THC per piece; (2) The cannabis product is labeled ‘FOR MEDICAL USE ONLY;’ and (3) The cannabis product is only available for sale to a medicinal-use patient. (c) A topical cannabis product or a cannabis concentrate shall not contain more than 1,000 milligrams THC per package. (d) Notwithstanding subsection (c), a topical cannabis product or a cannabis concentrate may contain more than 1,000 milligrams THC per package, but not more than 2,000 milligrams THC per package, if the product is labeled ‘FOR MEDICAL USE ONLY’ and is only available for sale to a medicinal-use patient.”

Sources: Justia §17304; Cornell §17304, accessed 1 Nov 2026. Beverages: a cannabis beverage is an edible cannabis product for §17304 purposes in secondary guidance (10 mg/serving, 100 mg/package, delineated/scored or multi-serving labelling per AB 2532 from 1 July 2027). No separate beverage THC cap was located in the regulation text opened — Verify whether DCC has a beverage-specific rule before stating a beverage cap distinct from edibles. THC beverages sold as hemp products are governed by 1.5, not §17304. Vapes/concentrates: 1,000 mg/package adult-use, 2,000 mg/package medical-only (above). No per-cartridge THC cap distinct from the per-package cap was located — Verify. Flower: no THC potency cap in the sources opened; the limit is weight (28.5 g possession), not potency — Verify against 4 CCR Ch. 10 for any flower potency rule; none was found. Edible shape/attractiveness: BPC §26120(b) and DCC regs prohibit packages/labels attractive to children; edibles may not be in the shape of a human, animal, insect or fruit in secondary summaries of the original 2017 regs — Verify current 4 CCR §17302/§17403 text; not asserted as current law here.

1.4 Medical programme

Recommendation vs. card — two distinct things, and the distinction matters for tax and for Schedule III:

  • Physician’s recommendation (Prop 215): written or oral recommendation/approval of a physician (HSC §11362.5(d), verbatim in 1.2). No qualifying-condition list in the statute beyond the physician’s determination that health would benefit, including the illustrative list (cancer, anorexia, AIDS, chronic pain, spasticity, glaucoma, arthritis, migraine, “or any other illness for which marijuana provides relief”). No state registry is required to claim Prop 215 protection; the card is voluntary (HSC §11362.71(f)).
  • State Medical Marijuana Identification Card (MMIC) programme (SB 420, HSC §§11362.71 et seq.): administered by CDPH, issued through county health departments (or county designee). Applicant provides written attending-physician documentation, proof of identity/residency, and the county fee (Medi-Cal beneficiaries pay half the fee in secondary summaries — Verify CDPH fee schedule); card valid one year; contains photo, nine-digit ID number, expiration date, issuing county. CDTFA page, opened, confirms the card’s features: “Issued by the ‘State of California’ with the state seal. States either ‘Patient’ or ‘Caregiver.’ Patient’s or primary caregiver’s photo. Nine-digit ID number. CDPH’s website to verify ID number. Expiration date. County that issued the card.” Retailers may verify the nine-digit number on CDPH’s website (mmic.cdph.ca.gov). Source: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (OFFICIAL).
  • Arrest protection: a person/designated caregiver in possession of a valid card “shall not be subject to arrest for possession, transportation, delivery, or cultivation of medical marijuana” in an amount established under Art. 2.5 (HSC §11362.71(e), SB 420 text extract) — i.e. within the §11362.77 safe harbour (8 oz dried + 6 mature/12 immature plants per patient), subject to the Kelly caveat that larger amounts reasonably related to medical need remain a Prop 215 defence, not an arrest shield.
  • Caregivers: “primary caregiver” = individual designated by the patient who has consistently assumed responsibility for housing, health or safety (HSC §11362.5(e)); SB 420 adds: caregiver must be 18+ (with exceptions in extract), may serve multiple patients in the same county plus one out-of-county patient in SB 420 summaries — Verify current §11362.7(d) text; caregiver patient-count rules were summarised, not opened.
  • Purchase limits for medical patients at retail: The 8 oz / 6+12 figures are possession/cultivation safe harbours, not a per-transaction retail purchase limit in the statute opened. Secondary retail guidance states medical patients may buy up to 8 oz flower per day vs. 1 oz adult-use — Verify against 4 CCR retailer purchase-limit sections (e.g. §15410 et seq.); do not publish a daily retail purchase limit without that text. DCC retailer rules distinguish A vs. M sales by designation and by “FOR MEDICAL USE ONLY” labelling (see §17304 medical-only products above).

Medical tax exemption: sales/use tax ONLY, not excise tax. RTC §6369.6(a), verbatim from CDTFA law guide (opened via search extract):

“6369.6. Medicinal cannabis; medicinal cannabis products. (a) There are exempted from the taxes imposed by this part the gross receipts from the sale in this state of, and the storage, use, or other consumption in this state of, medicinal cannabis or medicinal cannabis products as those terms are defined in Division 10 (commencing with Section 26000) of the Business and Professions Code when a person with an identification card or primary caregiver furnishes the seller with both their card issued under Section 11362.71 of the Health and Safety Code and a valid government-issued identification card.”

Source: CDTFA RTC §6369.6, accessed 1 Nov 2026. History: “Added by Stats 2022, Ch. 56, (AB 195), in effect June 30, 2022.” CDTFA retailer page, opened: “This exemption only applies to sales and use tax and does not apply to the cannabis excise tax due on retail sales of medicinal cannabis.” A physician’s recommendation alone does NOT trigger the exemption; only the MMIC + government ID does. Donated medicinal cannabis (retailer to patient/caregiver, with written certification) is exempt from both excise and use tax when actually donated — CDTFA page, opened, “Donated Medicinal Cannabis” section.

Do medicinal-only (M-type) licences still exist? YES — and they are now more important, not abolished. Every DCC licence carries A, M or both designations. Historically most licensees held both; the 2026 emergency rules created a practical route to hold separate A and M licences at the same premises through separate entities (1.0), precisely so the M activity can pursue DEA registration and Schedule III treatment. CDTFA’s retailer page expressly contemplates “medicinal only designation licenses issued to the same location where a separate adult-use designation license is issued.” The old SB 420 collective/cooperative model, by contrast, is gone: collectives ceased to be lawful one year after the Bureau posted notice it had begun licensing (HSC §11362.775(d)–(e)) — Verify that notice date; the CDTFA page still contains a legacy “Collectives and Cooperatives” tax section, which describes tax treatment if a collective exists, not a current licensing route. Do not present collectives as a current licence type.

Patient counts: No official MMIC count was opened in Part 1 — Unavailable in Part 1; Part 5/6 must source CDPH MMIC statistics by county, counts only, no personal data. (See Part 5.8 / 6.4 for the CDPH table that was opened in the Parts 5–6 track.)

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

California hemp definition (AB 45, as amended by AB 8 — pre-AB 8 text quoted, post-AB 8 consolidated text Verify): HSC §11018.5 (AB 45): “industrial hemp” / “hemp” means “an agricultural product, whether growing or not, that is limited to types of the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds of the plant and all derivatives, extracts, the resin extracted from any part of the plant, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of no more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis.” Sources: secondary reproductions of AB 45 (Ch. 576, Stats. 2021, approved/filed 6 Oct 2021, urgency, effective immediately), INCBA blog, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY reproduction).

HSC §111920(l) (AB 45): “THC” / “THC or comparable cannabinoid” means (1) tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (THCA); (2) any tetrahydrocannabinol, including but not limited to delta-8, delta-9 and delta-10, however derived (subject to CDPH exclusion of non-intoxicating isomers under §111921.7(a)); (3) any other cannabinoid, except CBD, that CDPH determines causes intoxication under §111921.7(b). HSC §111920: “Total THC” = sum of THC + THCA, calculated as total THC concentration (mg/g) = [THC + (THCA × 0.877)] in the AB 45 formula as summarised in the secondary extract — Verify the exact formula on leginfo; the extract truncates it.

How THC is measured in California hemp (two different measurements, do not conflate): (i) Plant / raw material: delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight for “industrial hemp” (AB 45 definition above; CDFA cultivation). (ii) Finished consumable hemp product: since 23 Sept 2024, NO DETECTABLE total THC per serving (CDPH emergency regs, Title 17 §§23000 et seq.), regardless of the 0.3% plant threshold. (iii) From 1 Jan 2026 under AB 8, industrial hemp extract used in foods/beverages/supplements/pet food must be >99% CBD or CBN by quantitative COA (CBD/CBN exceeds 99% of total integrated chromatographic peaks of tested analytes) with NO THC and NO synthetic cannabinoids — a purity test, not a 0.3% test.

AB 45 (2021) — what it did: Added HSC Ch. 9 (§§111920 et seq.), amended §§11018.5, 100425, 110065, added §§110036, 110407, 110469, 110611, 111691, 113091, and added/repealed BPC §26013.2; urgency, approved 6 Oct 2021. Legalised hemp/CBD in foods, beverages, dietary supplements, cosmetics and processed pet food if Chapter 9 requirements are met; a food/cosmetic is not adulterated solely because it includes industrial hemp meeting Chapter 9. Required CDPH Industrial Hemp Enrollment and Oversight (IHEO) authorisation + product registration for manufacturers/extractors; independent-lab testing of raw extract for contaminants and cannabinoid content before incorporation; labels/QR/barcode linking to COAs with THC content; warning that children and pregnant/breastfeeding persons should consult health professionals; statement “THE FDA HAS NOT EVALUATED THIS PRODUCT FOR SAFETY OR EFFICACY”; ban on untrue health-related statements. Prohibited industrial hemp in medical devices, prescription drugs, “processed smokable products” and alcoholic beverages (HSC §111921.5 in secondary summary — Verify current section number after AB 8 amendments). Directed CDPH to adopt regulations; AB 45 allowed up to 0.3% total THC for extracts in final-form products with no serving-size or servings-per-package limit — the gap the 2024 emergency regs closed.

Governor’s 2024 emergency regulations — DPH-24-005: Proposed by CDPH, announced 6 Sept 2024, filed with OAL 13 Sept 2024 (OAL File No. Z-2024-0913-02E), effective 23 Sept 2024; readopted 24 Mar 2025 (in effect until 23 Sept 2025 in the CDPH page extract); regular rulemaking approved by OAL 20 Oct 2025 (serving-size/age) and 21 Apr 2026 (further track). Operative rules (CDPH page, opened via extract): industrial hemp food, food additives, beverages and dietary supplements for human consumption must have “no detectable THC per serving,” cannot be sold to purchasers under 21, and may have no more than 5 servings per package. Title 17 §§23000, 23005, 23010, 23015, 23100. Source: CDPH DPH-24-005, accessed 1 Nov 2026. CDPH AB 8 FAQ, opened, confirms: “The industrial hemp emergency regulations (DPH-24-005) became effective on September 23, 2024, and require industrial hemp food, food additives, beverages, and dietary supplements intended for human consumption have no detectable total THC. The regulations also create a minimum age of 21 to purchase industrial hemp final form products intended for human consumption and limit the number of servings in industrial hemp products to five per package.” Source: CDPH AB 8 FAQ, accessed 1 Nov 2026. Legal challenges: secondary reports state hemp-industry challenges “have so far failed to block enforcement” — case names, courts and outcomes were not opened — Verify; do not name a case without the court record.

AB 8 (2025) — the law replacing/extending the emergency regime (full text on leginfo, Ch. 248, Stats. 2025). CDPH FAQ, opened, phase table:

  • 1 Jan 2026 (Phase 1): hemp extract in foods/food additives/beverages/dietary supplements/processed pet food cannot contain THC or synthetic cannabinoids; COA must confirm CBD or CBN >99% of total integrated chromatographic peaks. Cosmetics: extract cannot contain THC, except topically applied cosmetics may contain total THC ≤0.3% dry-weight basis; cosmetic manufacturers need IHEO + cosmetic manufacturing registration. Hemp flower, pre-rolls containing hemp flower/hemp-derived cannabinoids, and inhalable cannabis products containing hemp-derived THC cannot be sold/offered/provided in California; manufacturing inhalables solely for sale in other states is not prohibited; inhalable manufacturers must maintain IHEO and products must be <0.3% THC by COA; firms warehousing/distributing hemp flower only need no IHEO.
  • 1 Jan 2028 (Phase 2): anyone extracting cannabinoids from industrial hemp needs a DCC licence (not CDPH IHEO for that activity); manufacturers of foods/supplements/pet food containing only CBD or CBN isolate and no other cannabinoid still need CDPH IHEO; inhalable manufacturers move from CDPH IHEO to DCC regulation; intoxicating hemp products are treated as cannabis products under MAUCRSA (track-and-trace, testing, packaging/labelling, advertising, security, transport, taxation — see DCC page, opened). CDPH will after 1 Jan 2028 only issue IHEO for foods, processed pet foods and cosmetics (not inhalables/extraction).
  • Interim (1 Jan 2026–1 Jan 2028): DCC page, opened: licensed manufacturers may only use cannabinoids from licensed sources; licensed distributors cannot transport hemp; licensed retailers cannot sell hemp products; licensed testing labs can test hemp as the law allows; DCC is developing regulations to allow licensee use of hemp; growing rules unchanged.
  • Enforcement: CDPH may embargo adulterated foods/cosmetics, issue penalties, suspend/revoke registrations/authorisations; AB 8 “generally treats intoxicating hemp products as cannabis” so unlawful manufacture/sale may attract the same civil/criminal penalties as illegal cannabis. From 1 Jan 2026 state/local agencies have new powers to take products off shelves and fine businesses (DCC page).
  • No new CDPH rulemaking for AB 8: CDPH FAQ, opened: “CDPH does not plan to initiate rulemaking to implement or enforce the provisions of AB 8.” DCC rulemaking is required for the 2028 integration. CDPH public contact in FAQ: FDBIH@cdph.ca.gov, subject “AB 8”; DCC: info@cannabis.ca.gov.

Status of delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC in California on 1 Nov 2026:

  • Delta-8, delta-10, THC-O, HHC and other synthetic/converted cannabinoids: NOT lawfully sold outside the DCC-licensed cannabis system. AB 45’s broad “THC or comparable cannabinoid” definition already captured delta-8/10 however derived; the 2024 emergency regs barred any detectable total THC per serving in consumable hemp products; AB 8 from 1 Jan 2026 bans synthetic cannabinoids in hemp products and prohibits hemp flower/pre-rolls/inhalables containing hemp-derived THC in California. From 1 Jan 2028, intoxicating hemp-derived products (including delta-8/9 from hemp, if they are to be sold at all) must go through DCC licensing, testing, labelling and taxation as cannabis products.
  • Hemp-derived delta-9 (e.g. gummies/seltzers at ≤0.3% dry weight federally): unlawful as a general-retail hemp product in California if it contains any detectable THC per serving (2024 regs) and, from 1 Jan 2026, unlawful as a hemp food/beverage unless the extract is >99% CBD/CBN with no THC. The only lawful California route for a THC product is the DCC-licensed cannabis market (adult-use 21+ or medical), where it is regulated as cannabis, not hemp.
  • THCA flower: hemp flower cannot be sold/offered/provided in California from 1 Jan 2026 (AB 8); THCA is expressly included in California’s “THC” definition (AB 45 §111920(l)(1)), so THCA flower cannot evade the rule as “not THC.” Federally, THCA counts toward total THC after 11 Dec 2026 (baseline) — California already counts it.
  • CBD/CBN isolate products: lawful in general retail if >99% pure, no THC, no synthetics, IHEO-registered, tested and labelled per Chapter 9 / CDPH rules. Full-spectrum / broad-spectrum extracts with detectable THC are NOT lawful as general-retail hemp foods/beverages/supplements in California on 1 Nov 2026, even if ≤0.3% dry weight.
  • Smokable hemp: AB 45 originally barred “processed smokable products” (§111921.5 summary); AB 8 from 1 Jan 2026 bars sale of hemp flower, hemp pre-rolls and inhalable hemp-THC products in California, while allowing manufacture solely for out-of-state sale (IHEO + <0.3% THC COA). After 1 Jan 2028 inhalable manufacturers are DCC-regulated. Verify whether any smokable-hemp tax has ever been enacted — AB 45-era secondary summaries state inhalables were barred “until the Legislature passes a tax on those products”; no such tax was located in Run 1 — Verify.
  • THC beverages: (i) As cannabis beverages: DCC-licensed, §17304 edible limits (see 1.3), adult-use/medical only. (ii) As hemp beverages: no detectable THC per serving, 21+, ≤5 servings/package (2024 regs); from 1 Jan 2026 extract must be >99% CBD/CBN, no THC — i.e. a hemp “THC beverage” is not a lawful general-retail category in California. AB 1397 (2025, low-dose hemp drinks + 10% excise tax) did NOT become law on the evidence in the AB 8 analysis (pending at that date) — Verify leginfo status; do not present hemp THC drinks as taxed/lawful.

California response to the federal hemp dates (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026) — finding: NO specific California response located in the sources opened. DCC’s hemp page (opened 1 Nov 2026) still states the federal distinction as “less than 0.3% THC = hemp; more than 0.3% = marijuana” and California’s stricter “any detectable THC = intoxicating/restricted” rule, with no mention of the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 federal redefinition, the total-THC (THCA-inclusive) test, or the 0.4 mg/container cap. CDPH’s AB 8 FAQ (opened) likewise contains no federal-date discussion. Consequence for the final file: state expressly that (a) California law does not need the federal cap to ban intoxicating hemp retail — it already does so more strictly per serving; (b) the federal redefinition will matter in California mainly for (i) interstate hemp shipments and cultivation compliance (total-THC plant test, synthetic-cannabinoid exclusion), (ii) products that are federal “marijuana” after 11 Dec 2026 but were previously shipped as hemp, and (iii) the AB 8 2028 integration, when hemp-derived THC entering the DCC system will be taxed/tested as cannabis; (c) whether California will conform its HSC §11018.5 definition (delta-9 only, 0.3%) to the federal total-THC definition is Verify — no conforming bill was located in Run 1. Do not claim California has adopted the federal definition. USDA DEA-lab delay to 31 Dec 2026 and federal rider through 11 Dec 2026: no California-specific implementation guidance was located in the sources opened — Verify on CDFA hemp programme pages (Part 7).

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, consumption lounges/cafés, zoning/buffers

Packaging/labelling — MAUCRSA BPC §26120 (core) + 4 CCR §§17403–17412 (numbering Verify): BPC §26120(b): “Packages and labels shall not be made to be attractive to children.” (AB 1894 bill text extract, opened via search.) BPC §26120(c): labels/inserts must include, prominently/clearly/legibly in the font size prescribed by DCC: bold GOVERNMENT WARNING (text in the AB 1894 extract begins “GOVERNMENT WARNING: THIS PACKAGE CONTAINS CANNABIS, A SCHEDULE I CONTROLLED SUBSTANCE…” — Verify current warning text after Schedule III: the “Schedule I” warning for medical products is now federally inaccurate for state-licensed medical cannabis and DCC/legislature action to update it is Verify; do not assume the warning has been changed), product weight/type, active-ingredient list, appellation of origin where applicable, allergen warnings, THC/CBD mg per serving and per package (4 CCR §17407 in secondary summaries), universal symbol (4 CCR §17410: 0.5-inch / 0.25-inch sizes in secondary summaries — Verify), child-resistant and, for multi-serving packages, resealable packaging (4 CCR §17412(a)(1) in secondary summaries). AB 2532 (2026) additions: Poison Help line on edible/beverage labels/inserts; multi-serving beverage notice from 1 July 2027 (1.2).

Advertising — BPC §26152 (core prohibitions) + §26152.1 (vape disposal, 1.2): §26152(a) (AB 2532 bill text, opened via extract): a person engaged in commercial cannabis activity, licensed or unlicensed, shall not “Advertise or market in a manner that is false or untrue in any material particular, or that, irrespective of falsity, directly, or by ambiguity, omission, or inference, or by the addition of irrelevant, scientific, or technical matter, tends to create a misleading impression.” Further §26152 prohibitions in secondary summaries (health-related untrue/misleading statements §26154; advertising attractive to children/persons <21; advertising within 1,000 feet of schools/daycares/playgrounds in some summaries; 71.6% adult-audience rule for broadcast/print in DCC regs — Verify each against current §26152 and 4 CCR §§17400 et seq.; only §26152(a) and the billboard paragraph below are quoted from text in Run 1).

Billboards — Farmer v. Bureau of Cannabis Control (now DCC): Statute, BPC §26152(d) (as quoted in every secondary report): a licensee shall not “advertise or market on a billboard or similar advertising device located on an Interstate Highway or on a State Highway which crosses the California border.” Former regulation, 16 CCR §5040(b)(3) (BCC era): limited the ban to within a 15-mile radius of the California border. Farmer v. Bureau of Cannabis Control & Lori Ajax, No. 19-cv-0597, San Luis Obispo County Superior Court, judgment 11 Jan 2021 (formal judgment; BCC notice 21 Jan 2021): the 15-mile regulation was invalid as inconsistent with §26152(d); BCC exceeded its authority. BCC did not appeal; it instructed licensees to “not place new advertising or marketing on any interstate highway or state highway that crosses the California border” and to “begin the process of removing current advertising and marketing that meets this criteria.” Effect today (on the evidence opened): no billboard/similar advertising anywhere on an interstate or state highway that crosses the California border; billboards on highways wholly inside California are NOT covered by this ban. AB 1302 (2021), which would have restored the 15-mile rule, did not become law; a later billboard bill was vetoed in 2021 per secondary report — Verify both on leginfo. Sources: Cannabis Law Report; JD Supra, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY — the Superior Court judgment itself was not opened; Verify on courts.ca.gov / SLO Superior Court record). DCC’s current advertising page (opened) does NOT restate the billboard rule; it covers only vape disposal messaging — do not cite the DCC advertising page as the source for the billboard ban. The ban’s source is §26152(d) + Farmer + the BCC 21 Jan 2021 notice.

Delivery: Delivery = commercial transfer to a customer, including via a technology platform (BPC §26001(s) in AB 1332 analysis). Retailer (storefront, Type 10) and non-storefront retailer (delivery-only) licences authorise delivery; microbusinesses authorised for retail may also deliver. Drivers must carry licence copy + government photo ID (BPC §26090(b) in AB 1332 analysis). CCTT delivery records: employee name/ID/licence number, vehicle make/model/plate, UIDs, departure date/time before departure; after each delivery, date/time, adult-use vs. medicinal, UID/quantity, price, county; if CCTT cannot be updated in real time, a delivery inventory ledger updated after every stop and entered into CCTT by end of calendar day, carried at all times — secondary summary of DCC delivery rules (LegalClarity, accessed 1 Nov 2026) — Verify against 4 CCR §§15414–15418. Local delivery bans: medicinal delivery is protected by BPC §§26320–26325 (1.0/Part 3). Adult-use delivery into a jurisdiction that bans storefronts: Verify (see 0.4).

Consumption lounges and cafés — AB 1775 (1.2, 0.7): State permission is local-option only (BPC §26200(g)). No separate DCC “lounge licence” exists in the sources opened; lounges operate under a Type 10 retailer or Type 12 microbusiness licence (licence-type numbering for microbusiness is Type 12 in most DCC material; one secondary guide says Type 13 — conflict, Verify on DCC licence-types page; Part 4 must resolve it) with local on-site-consumption authorisation. AB 1775 additions over pre-2025 law: freshly prepared (not only prepackaged) non-cannabis food, non-alcoholic beverages, live musical/other performances and ticket sales, subject to California Retail Food Code compliance, separation of food/cannabis storage/preparation, no cannabis consumption in food-preparation/storage/warewashing areas, no alcohol/tobacco on premises in SF/Palm Springs implementations (state text on alcohol/tobacco in consumption areas Verify), and worker protections including employee choice to wear N95/NIOSH-rated respiratory protection in consumption areas (secondary SF summary — Verify BPC §26200(g) worker-protection text). Cannabis sold in a café for on-site consumption cannot leave the premises and cafés cannot deliver (SF ordinance summary) — Verify whether that is SF-local or state law.

Zoning and buffers — state vs. local: State law does NOT impose a single statewide buffer in the text opened, except: (i) the advertising restriction near schools/daycares/playgrounds (see above, Verify); (ii) the local-control preservation in §26200(a) (localities set zoning/land-use, including buffers, and may ban outright). The commonly cited “600-foot” retailer-to-retailer / school buffer is a LOCAL rule in many cities (e.g. SF café exception: a café may be within 600 feet of a storefront retailer if same ownership and no other commonly owned café within that radius — SF secondary summary, Verify SF ordinance), NOT a statewide DCC rule in the sources opened. Do not publish a statewide 600-ft buffer as DCC law. Cultivation zoning/environmental permits (Fish & Wildlife lake/streambed, Water Board) are Part 4, not Part 1 — flagged here only to prevent conflation with retail buffers.

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking — Metrc / California Cannabis Track-and-Trace (CCTT)

System: California Cannabis Track-and-Trace (CCTT), powered by Metrc software. CDTFA page, opened: “you, as a cannabis retailer, are required to record your wholesale cost and the retail selling price … in the [CCTT] system, as well as comply with other requirements established in DCC’s regulations” and “You can find additional information regarding new CCTT features on Metrc’s California Bulletin Database.” Source: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

DCC regs, 4 CCR Art. 6 (§§15047–15052, numbering Verify), as quoted in DCC enforcement filings opened via search: §15049: all cannabis/cannabis products on licensed premises must be assigned a plant or package tag (except harvested plants being dried/cured/graded/trimmed as specified) and recorded in track-and-trace; each of receipt, rejection, destruction/disposal, packaging/repackaging, laboratory testing (including results) and other listed activities must be recorded within 24 hours of occurrence, with type, weight/volume/count and other specified data. §15049.1/§15048.5 (cultivation, proposed Jan 2026 text): harvest-batch wet weight immediately after harvest, waste weight, unique harvest-batch name, initiating date; package tag within 24 hours of completion of on-site processing / no later than when any part of a harvest batch finishes drying/curing/grading/trimming — Verify final adopted text; the Jan 2026 text is a fourth proposed modified text (DCC-2025-01-R). §15051: licensee shall review track-and-trace information at least once every 30 calendar days, reconcile on-hand inventory with CCTT records, audit and notify DCC of discrepancies as specified. §15049.2: recording transfers (shipping manifests, acceptance/receipt acknowledgement in CCTT). Tags are licensee-specific and unused tags shall not be transferred to another licensee (enforcement filing summary). Sources: DCC draft CCTT text, Oct 2024; DCC final decision, Saints Place, Feb 2026, accessed 1 Nov 2026. These are DCC enforcement/proposed-text PDFs, not the consolidated regulation — Verify against the current 4 CCR Div. 19 text for the final file.

Hemp and CCTT: licensed distributors cannot transport hemp and retailers cannot sell hemp products in the interim (DCC page, 1.5); from 1 Jan 2028 hemp entering the cannabis supply chain must follow CCTT (DCC page: “Track and trace requirements” listed for hemp distribution).

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections — AB 2188 + SB 700

Statute: Gov. Code §12954 (California Fair Employment and Housing Act, FEHA), as added/amended by AB 2188 (signed 18 Sep 2022) and SB 700 (signed 7 Oct 2023), both effective 1 Jan 2024.

  • AB 2188 — what it prohibits: discrimination in hiring, termination or any term/condition of employment based on (a) cannabis use off the job and away from the workplace, or (b) an employer-required drug test that detects non-psychoactive cannabis metabolites in hair, blood, urine or other bodily fluids. Rationale in secondary summaries: metabolites show use in the last few weeks, not impairment at work.
  • AB 2188 — what it preserves: employers may still use scientifically valid screening that does NOT screen for non-psychoactive metabolites (e.g. tests for active THC / impairment, oral-fluid/breath/blood indicators of recent use, performance tests in secondary summaries); employers retain the right to maintain a drug-free workplace, to prohibit on-the-job possession/use/impairment, and to discipline for actual impairment at work.
  • SB 700 — what it adds: it is unlawful to request information from an applicant relating to prior cannabis use, and to use criminal-history information about prior cannabis use unless permitted under the Fair Chance Act (Gov. Code §12952) or required by other state/federal law.
  • Exemptions (both laws): (i) employees in the building and construction trades; (ii) applicants/employees for positions requiring a federal government background investigation or security clearance; (iii) applicants/employees required to be tested under state/federal law as a condition of employment, receiving federal funding/licensing-related benefits, or entering a federal contract. Law-enforcement positions are NOT expressly exempted by name in §12954, but secondary analysis notes the federal-background/federal-testing/drug-free-workplace provisions effectively exclude most peace-officer roles in practice — Verify; do not state a law-enforcement exemption as statutory text.
  • Enforcement: complaints to the California Civil Rights Department (CRD) in secondary summaries — Verify CRD jurisdiction/remedies on crd.ca.gov.

Sources: CaNORML, AB 2188; Legal 500, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY). Verbatim §12954 text was not opened — the final file must quote leginfo, not these summaries. Interaction with Schedule III (flag, not settled law): AB 2188/SB 700 protect off-duty use regardless of schedule; Schedule III does not create a right to use at work or while impaired, and federal safety-sensitive testing mandates (e.g. DOT) are preserved by the exemptions above. Whether DEA-registered M-licensee employees gain any additional federal protection is Verify — no source opened addresses it.

PART 2 — TAXES

2.1 State cannabis excise tax — rate history (official, CDTFA opened)

PeriodRateAuthority / event
1 Jan 2018 – 30 June 202215% of retail gross receipts (excise, collected via distributor pre-AB 195) + separate cultivation tax (see 2.2)Proposition 64 / Cannabis Tax Law (RTC §§34010 et seq.)
1 July 2022Cultivation tax discontinued (AB 195)AB 195, Ch. 56, Stats. 2022
1 Jan 2023 – 30 June 202515% of retail gross receipts, now collected by retailer from purchaser (AB 195 shift from distributor to retailer)RTC §34011.2(a)(1); CDTFA retailer page (“prior to Jan 1, 2023” distributor-collection credit rules)
1 July 2025 – 30 Sept 202519% of retail gross receiptsCDTFA adjustment under RTC §34011.2 as added by AB 195 §22, capped at 19%, to replace forgone cultivation-tax revenue
1 Oct 2025 – 30 June 2028 (statute) / through 30 June 2028 [AUDIT CORRECTION: statute controls] (CDTFA page wording)15%AB 564, Ch. 127, Stats. 2025, amending RTC §34011.2 — see conflict note 2.7
FY 2028–29 and every 2 years thereafterAdjustment by CDTFA in consultation with Dept. of Finance, on/before 1 May of the preceding fiscal year, capped at 19%, to generate the amount that would have been collected under the former cultivation taxRTC §34011.2 as amended by AB 564 (AB 564 bill text extract: “For the 2028–29 fiscal year and every two years thereafter, the department … shall … adjust the cannabis excise tax rate … not to exceed 19%”)

Mechanics, CDTFA opened: excise tax is imposed on the purchaser, collected by the retailer, separately stated on the invoice/receipt, filed on a cannabis retailer excise tax return, and included in gross receipts subject to sales tax (i.e. sales tax is calculated on price + excise + local business tax + delivery/service charges where applicable). Gross receipts for excise include selling price after discounts, local cannabis business tax passed on, delivery charges (own-vehicle delivery), payment-processing/service fees and mandatory packaging/cartridge/device charges; they exclude sales tax itself. Regulation 3802 (gross receipts) provides further guidance.

Worked example, CDTFA opened (15% illustration): $35.00 price + $3.50 local tax (10%) + $3.00 service fee + $5.00 delivery = $46.50 gross receipts subject to excise; excise $6.98; subtotal $53.48; sales tax at 8.5% = $4.55; total $58.03. This is CDTFA’s example, not a real transaction.

Vendor compensation / distributor credit (legacy): retailers could retain 20% of excise as vendor compensation — secondary source states this ended 31 Dec 2025 (Leading Tax Group, accessed 1 Nov 2026, SECONDARY, Verify on CDTFA). Pre-2023 distributor-paid excise credit rules are on the CDTFA page opened (credit only for distributor sales before 1 Jan 2023 and retail sales on/after 1 Jan 2023, with UID documentation).

AB 8’s excise provision: the AB 8 Assembly analysis states AB 8 “reduces the cannabis excise tax to 15%, beginning January 1, 2028” — that provision is now redundant/superseded in effect by AB 564’s 15% from 1 Oct 2025, but the AB 8 text’s 2028 date is also the date hemp-derived THC products entering the DCC system become subject to cannabis excise tax (DCC page: “Hemp products containing THC will have to pay the same taxes as medicinal and adult-use cannabis products” from 1 Jan 2028). Do not present AB 8 as the source of the current 15% rate; AB 564 is. No rollback/delay beyond AB 564 found: AB 564 is the rollback/delay. No 2026 bill repealing the FY 2028–29 adjustment was confirmed in Run 1 — Verify leginfo 2026 tax bills before stating “no further change.” Source for this section: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (OFFICIAL, opened).

2.2 Cultivation tax — history and removal

Proposition 64 imposed a weight-based cultivation tax (flower/leaves/fresh plant per ounce) in addition to the 15% retail excise. The pre-2022 per-ounce rates were not opened on CDTFA in Run 1 — Verify before publishing any $/oz figure. Secondary summaries commonly cite $9.65/oz flower, $2.87/oz leaves, $1.35/oz fresh plant (with CPI adjustments) — NOT adopted here. AB 195 (Ch. 56, Stats. 2022) discontinued the cultivation tax on 1 July 2022. CDTFA secondary summary: cultivation tax raised $500.6m from 2018 until it ended (Beard Bros, accessed 1 Nov 2026, SECONDARY, Verify on CDTFA). The AB 195 bargain: no cultivation tax, but CDTFA must adjust the retail excise rate from FY 2025–26 (now FY 2028–29 after AB 564) to replace the forgone amount, capped at 19%. That mechanism produced the single 19% quarter in 2025. CDTFA states it considers historical cultivation-tax return data, CCTT/Metrc data, current retailer excise return data, and CPI-adjusted cultivation-tax rates for each category when setting the adjustment (CDTFA page, opened).

2.3 State and local sales and use taxes

Statewide base: 7.25% sales/use tax (CDTFA page, opened: “The statewide sales and use tax rate is 7.25 percent (7.25%), though the tax rates are generally higher in areas where district taxes are in effect”). Combined rates vary by address, generally 7.25% to over 10–11% with district taxes (secondary range 7.25%–10.75% — Verify CDTFA rate lookup for any specific address; do not publish a statewide maximum without the CDTFA district-tax table).

Base for cannabis sales tax: gross receipts including the cannabis excise tax (CDTFA page, opened, with worked examples). Delivery: district tax is the rate at the delivery location, not the retailer’s location; storefront sales use the store’s district rate.

What is taxable: cannabis, cannabis products, and any other tangible personal property (accessories, merchandise, packaging required as a condition of sale) sold at retail, unless a specific exemption applies. CDTFA distinguishes “cannabis sales” ($3.9bn in 2025, secondary) from “taxable sales” including accessories/merchandise ($4.8bn in 2025, secondary) — see 2.7.

Medical exemption (sales/use ONLY): RTC §6369.6, verbatim in 1.4; MMIC + government ID required; excise still applies; local business tax treatment is separate (see 2.4). The exemption was added by AB 195, effective 30 June 2022.

Use tax on discounted / below-cost sales: CDTFA page, opened: sales for less than 50% of cost (non-obsolete, non-expiring product) attract use tax on cost, not sales tax on price; promotions (e.g. buy-one-get-one) attract tax on the total amount received plus excise. Trade samples: no excise on bona fide trade samples received, but excise + sales tax apply if the recipient later sells them; donation rules in 1.4.

Filing: seller’s permit + cannabis retailer excise tax permit (separate location per DCC licence, including separate A/M licences — 1.0); returns filed online; records kept at least 4 years for tax (CDTFA) and 7 years for MAUCRSA commercial activity (BPC, via CDTFA page) — both periods stated on the CDTFA page opened. Source: CDTFA retailers page, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

2.4 Local cannabis business taxes — examples (Part 3 develops jurisdictions; Part 2 gives tax mechanics + limits)

Mechanics, CDTFA opened: “Many cities and counties have enacted measures requiring that cannabis businesses located in their jurisdictions pay a cannabis business tax. If you add a separate amount to your customers’ invoices or receipts to cover your cannabis business tax, cannabis excise tax and sales tax applies to the cannabis business tax amount.” CDTFA does NOT administer local cannabis business taxes; rates must be checked with the local government (CalGold directory referenced on the CDTFA page).

CDTFA worked examples use 10% (first example) and 4% (Sacramento example) as illustrative rates, not as findings that Sacramento’s rate is 4% or that any named city’s rate is 10%. Do NOT cite the CDTFA examples as local rates in the final file.

No local rate table was opened in Part 2. Los Angeles city/county, San Francisco, Oakland, San Jose, San Diego, Sacramento, Long Beach, Fresno, Riverside/San Bernardino, Orange County cities, Humboldt/Mendocino/Trinity, Santa Barbara, Monterey/Salinas, Palm Springs/Coachella/Desert Hot Springs, South Lake Tahoe rates are therefore Unavailable in Part 2 and must be sourced from each city/county code/treasurer page in Part 3. Secondary claims that combined effective tax (excise + sales + local) often reaches 27–38% or “as high as 45% in some areas” (AB 564 analysis / secondary tax guides) are NOT adopted as a rate finding — they are advocacy/secondary estimates.

Hemp product tax: No standalone California hemp-product excise tax was in effect on 1 Nov 2026 in the sources opened. Hemp CBD products not containing cannabis are NOT subject to cannabis excise tax (CDTFA page, opened: “CBD products which are derived from industrial hemp plants and do not contain cannabis are not subject to the cannabis excise tax, even if the CBD product contains trace amounts of [THC]”). From 1 Jan 2028, hemp-derived THC products in the DCC system WILL pay cannabis excise + sales tax like cannabis (DCC page, AB 8). AB 1397’s proposed 10% hemp-drink excise tax is not law (1.9, Verify). Verify whether any local jurisdiction imposes a hemp-specific business tax; none was located.

2.5 Proposition 64 revenue distribution — Cannabis Tax Fund (RTC §34019)

Only the excise tax feeds the Cannabis Tax Fund. Sales tax on cannabis goes to the General Fund / local governments like any other retail sales tax (secondary explainer, LegalClarity, accessed 1 Nov 2026; the principle is consistent with CDTFA’s structure but the §34019 text was not opened — Verify §34019 verbatim on leginfo).

Order of allocation (AB 564 / AB 8 analyses, consistent across both):

  1. Tier 1 — regulatory/enforcement costs: reimbursement of costs incurred by implementing state agencies, including CDTFA, DCC, CDFW, State Water Resources Control Board, Dept. of Pesticide Regulation (list in Agri-Pulse secondary report).
  2. Tier 2 — fixed amounts (in descending order as listed in the analyses): $10 million annually to one or more California public universities for Proposition 64 research (through FY 2028–29 in one secondary summary); $3 million to California Highway Patrol for protocols to determine cannabis impairment while operating a vehicle; $50 million to the Governor’s Office of Business and Economic Development (GO-Biz) for the Community Reinvestment Grants programme; $2 million to UC San Diego Center for Medicinal Cannabis Research.
  3. Tier 3 — remainder, 60/20/20: 60% Youth Education, Prevention, Early Intervention and Treatment Account; 20% Environmental Restoration and Protection Account; 20% State and Local Government Law Enforcement Account (Board of State and Community Corrections / CHP grants in secondary summaries).

Legislative flexibility: beginning 1 July 2028, the Legislature may by majority vote allocate funds for purposes other than those stipulated, if the change furthers Proposition 64’s purpose and does not reduce any account below its FY 2027–28 level (AB 564 analysis summary) — Verify §34019 text. Sources: AB 564 analysis, Capitol Track; AB 8 analysis, Capitol Track, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY/legislative-analysis — the final file must quote RTC §34019, not these analyses, for the allocation formula).

2.6 IRS §280E and California decoupling — the split that matters most after Schedule III

Federal §280E (baseline + CDTFA/DCC context): §280E denies deductions/credits (except COGS, which reduces gross income, not via deduction) for a trade/business trafficking in Schedule I or II substances. After 28 April 2026: §280E is “generally lifted” for state-licensed medical activity (Schedule III) and FDA-approved products; it remains fully applicable to adult-use activity (Schedule I). A dual holder must therefore allocate income/expenses/COGS between M and A activity; only the M allocation escapes §280E federally. Secondary analyses add two cautions that must be carried as Verify / pending guidance, not as settled law: (i) relief is prospective for the full 2026 tax year but not closed years, pending IRS guidance (Treasury position as reported Oct 2026); (ii) a proposal in the 119th Congress would deny marijuana deductions “regardless of schedule” (CRS LSB11424 as cited Oct 2026) — if enacted it would override the Schedule III relief. Sources: Shay Gilmore Law, 280E entity choice; Harris Sliwoski, rescheduling considerations, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY, law-firm analyses; IRS guidance itself was not opened — Verify on irs.gov).

The DCC A/M split (1.0) is the state-law mechanism that makes the federal allocation auditable: separate entities, separate records, separate inventory, separate CDTFA permits. Without the split, a single-entity dual holder’s M revenue is commingled with A revenue and the Schedule III position is, in the secondary analyses’ words, “structurally” compromised. Joint-and-several liability (1.0) means the split does NOT insulate the M entity from the A entity’s violations for DCC enforcement purposes — a point the final file must not omit.

California state decoupling — CORRECTED position (do not use the “expired 2024” claim): AB 37 (2019), Ch. 792, Stats. 2019, RTC §17209: for Personal Income Tax Law (PITL) taxpayers, IRC §280E “shall not apply” to commercial cannabis activity by a MAUCRSA licensee. Verbatim, current text (Justia/FindLaw reproductions of RTC §17209, as amended):

“17209. (a) For each taxable year beginning on or after January 1, 2020, and before January 1, 2030, Section 280E of the Internal Revenue Code, relating to expenditures in connection with the illegal sale of drugs, shall not apply to the carrying on of any trade or business that is commercial cannabis activity by a licensee. (b) For purposes of this section, ‘commercial cannabis activity’ and ‘licensee’ shall have the same meanings as set forth in Division 10 (commencing with Section 26000) of the Business and Professions Code. (c) This section shall remain in effect only until December 1, 2030, and as of that date is repealed.”

Amended by Stats. 2024, Ch. 34, §19 (SB 167, effective 27 June 2024), extending the repeal from 1 Dec 2025 to 1 Dec 2030 and the taxable-year window to before 1 Jan 2030. Sources: Justia RTC §17209; FTB commercial cannabis activity report, May 2026 (FTB: “SB 167 (statutes of 2024) amended this section to extend the repeal date to January 1, 2030” — note FTB’s “January 1, 2030” vs. statute’s “December 1, 2030” repeal date; the taxable-year window ends before 1 Jan 2030, the section repeals 1 Dec 2030 — minor wording conflict, statute controls), accessed 1 Nov 2026. FTB is OFFICIAL for the extension; Justia/FindLaw for the text (leginfo §17209 page itself not opened — Verify on leginfo).

Conflict resolved: the CannBus Q3 2026 summary’s claim that “California’s state decoupling expired January 1, 2025 — and state law has not followed the federal move” and that “as of January 1, 2025, California cannabis businesses are subject to 280E on their state return regardless of license type” is WRONG on the evidence of RTC §17209 as amended by SB 167 and the FTB May 2026 report.
