Do NOT repeat it. The correct position is: PITL decoupling runs through taxable years before 1 Jan 2030 (repeal 1 Dec 2030).

Corporation Tax Law: California corporation tax law never conformed to §280E; corporate cannabis businesses could always deduct ordinary/necessary expenses + COGS for state purposes (FTB/TaxBuzz summaries, TaxBuzz, accessed 1 Nov 2026, SECONDARY, Verify on ftb.ca.gov). AB 37 was needed only to align PITL (sole proprietorships, partnerships, S-corporation PIT investors, LLCs taxed as partnerships) with corporation law.

Scope limits: §17209 applies only to MAUCRSA licensees in compliance with state/local licensing; unlicensed activity remains subject to §280E for state PITL purposes in the secondary summaries — Verify §17209 conditions on leginfo. FTB Form 4197 (“Information on Tax Expenditure Items”) reporting applies to cannabis PITL returns (FTB report).

Interaction with federal Schedule III for state tax: because California PITL already decoupled from §280E through 2030 for licensed activity (both A and M), the federal Schedule III move does NOT change California PITL treatment for licensed businesses — it changes only the federal return, and only for the M allocation. For corporation taxpayers, state treatment was already decoupled. This is the single most important tax point for dual holders and must be stated plainly; the CannBus error, if repeated, would invert it. High Road Cannabis Tax Credit: mentioned in FTB-related secondary material for Type 10 retailer / Type 12 microbusiness licensees — Verify on FTB/CDTFA; not developed here, no rate/eligibility stated.

2.7 Revenue figures — official first, conflicts listed, nothing smoothed

Official CDTFA quarterly figures established in Run 1 (via secondary reporting of CDTFA releases — CDTFA’s own tax-revenue dashboard was NOT opened, so each figure below is marked for direct CDTFA verification): Q2 2025: $259.7m total ($147.3m excise at 15% + $112.4m sales/use), vs. Q2 2024 $272.8m ($160.9m excise + $111.9m sales/use); H1 2025 $502.8m (Q1 2025 revised $243.1m = $142.4m excise + $100.7m sales). Q2 2025 excise down ~8% year-over-year. Q2 2026 (preliminary, CDTFA 27 Aug 2026): $261.7m total ($152.0m excise + $109.7m sales tax); Q1 2026 revised up to $249.8m. Sources: Highly Capitalized, Q2 2025 CDTFA reporting, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY); CannBus Q3 2026 report, citing CDTFA 27 Aug 2026, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY compilation of an official release).

Annual sales / tax totals — conflicts, do not pick one silently:

  • Series A (MJBizDaily, 26 Feb 2026, reporting CDTFA “cannabis sales”): 2023 $4.4bn, 2024 $4.2bn, 2025 $3.9bn; total taxable sales incl. accessories/merchandise 2023 $5.2bn, 2024 $4.9bn, 2025 $4.8bn; Q4 2025 cannabis sales $979m vs. $1.0bn+ Q4 2024 (−$46m); total cannabis tax revenue 2025 $1.05bn vs. 2024 $1.07bn. Source: MJBizDaily, 26 Feb 2026, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY, trade press reporting CDTFA).
  • Series B (Beard Bros, reporting CDTFA with quarterly table): 2023 $4.45bn, 2024 $4.21bn, 2025 $3.93bn (−6.5% in 2025); Q3 2025 (19% quarter) $960.4m, −6.9% YoY, lowest quarterly total since CDTFA began reporting the figure in 2023; Q3 2025 excise $180.7m vs. Q2 2025 $149.9m; full-year excise 2025 $623.2m vs. 2024 $627.5m. Source: Beard Bros, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY).
  • Series C (CannBus modelled from CDTFA excise ÷ rate): 2025 implied retail receipts ~$3.85bn (−5.7% vs. ~$4.08bn in 2024); H1 2026 ~$1.98bn (Q1 ~$965m + Q2 ~$1,013m, preliminary); DCC track-and-trace sales 2025 $4.41bn (−5.2% YoY; 2024 $4.66bn) as reported by Cannabis Business Times. Source: CannBus above (MODELLED, not official).

Why the series differ: CDTFA “cannabis sales” vs. “taxable sales” (accessories included) vs. excise-implied receipts vs. Metrc track-and-trace sales are different measures; quarterly revisions (1–3% upward in CannBus note) and the 19% quarter’s effect on excise-implied sales further diverge them. The final file must state which measure each figure uses and must NOT present a single “California sales = $X” without the measure. Recommended follow-up: pull the CDTFA cannabis tax revenue by quarter table directly (cdtfa.ca.gov, Tax Revenue / Cannabis Tax Revenue pages) and the DCC track-and-trace sales series, and publish both with their definitions.

Cumulative: Governor’s office “nearly $8.4bn since 2018” (see 0.9) vs. component sum ~$7.9bn ($4.5bn excise + $3.4bn sales) in the same report — arithmetic conflict flagged; Verify on governor.ca.gov / CDTFA cumulative table. An earlier secondary total (“more than $7.3bn, excise $3.9bn + sales $2.9bn” as of Q2 2025 reporting) is consistent with growth to ~$8bn by late 2026 but is not a substitute for the CDTFA cumulative figure.

Hemp market figures: No official California hemp product-market revenue figure was opened in Run 1 — Unavailable in Run 1; Part 6 must source CDFA/CDPH hemp registration and market data, not industry estimates. CDPH SRIA (Apr 2025) estimated ~115 licensed California hemp firms, ~100 eliminated by the permanent serving-size/age rule (secondary DOF comment letter, DOF comment letter, accessed 1 Nov 2026) — Verify against the CDPH SRIA itself; do not use as a current hemp-firm count.

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 The California local-control system

Dual licensing — state + local. California does not have a state-only licence. A business needs: (1) local authorisation — a city or county licence, permit, or other authorisation, plus zoning / land-use compliance, in the jurisdiction where the premises sit; and (2) a DCC state licence for the specific activity. DCC states on its “Where cannabis businesses are allowed” page:

“Cannabis businesses are regulated in two ways: By the state, and By their city or county.” “Each city or county can decide whether to license cannabis businesses in their area. They can: License all cannabis businesses / License some types and prohibit others / Prohibit all cannabis businesses.” “A city may have different rules than its county. Counties can decide which types of cannabis businesses they license or prohibit. But county rules only apply to the unincorporated parts of the county. They do not apply to incorporated cities that are within the county. Incorporated cities can decide which types they license or prohibit within their city. Their rules do not need to align with the rules of the county where they’re located.”

Source opened 1 Nov 2026: DCC — Where cannabis businesses are allowed. DCC licence-types hub: DCC licence types, opened 1 Nov 2026: “You must have a valid DCC license before performing any commercial cannabis activity, including: Growing cannabis (cultivation) / Transporting cannabis (distribution) / Making cannabis products (manufacturing) / Testing cannabis or cannabis products (testing laboratory) / Selling cannabis (retail) / Holding an event where cannabis will be sold (event organizers).”

The statute: Business & Professions Code §26200. §26200(a)(1) — verbatim operative text, as returned in search/opened secondary reproduction of the code (leginfo text to be re-opened verbatim in the final dossier — see Verify note at 3.7):

“Except as set forth in the Medicinal Cannabis Patients’ Right of Access Act (Chapter 26 (commencing with Section 26320)), this division shall not be interpreted to supersede or limit the authority of a local jurisdiction to adopt and enforce local ordinances to regulate businesses licensed under this division, including, but not limited to, local zoning and land use requirements, business license requirements, and requirements related to reducing exposure to secondhand smoke, or to completely prohibit the establishment or operation of one or more types of businesses licensed under this division within the local jurisdiction.”

§26200(a)(2): the division does not supersede or limit existing local authority for law enforcement, enforcement of local zoning, or enforcement of local licence/permit/authorisation requirements. §26200(b): the division shall not be interpreted to require the Department to undertake local law enforcement responsibilities, enforce local zoning, or enforce local licensing/permitting/authorisation requirements. §26200(c): a local jurisdiction shall notify the Department upon revocation of any local licence, permit, or authorisation. Within 60 days of being so informed, the Department shall begin the process to determine whether the state licence should be suspended or revoked under Chapter 3 (commencing with §26030). §26200(g)(1): only if the conditions in (g)(2) are met, a local jurisdiction may allow (A) smoking, vaporizing, and ingesting cannabis on the premises of a retailer or microbusiness granted local authority for onsite consumption, and (B) preparation/sale of non-cannabis food/beverage in compliance with the California Retail Food Code. This is the state-law gateway for consumption lounges/cafés — local opt-in is still required. Official code link to cite (not opened as a full leginfo page in Part 3 — Verify the current leginfo text before quoting in print): leginfo — BPC §26200.

The state-licence gate: BPC §26055. Secondary summaries consistently cite §26055 for the rule that DCC must deny a licence application if the local jurisdiction has told DCC the proposed activity is prohibited there. This is the operative “local veto” at application stage. Verify: open the current §26055 text on leginfo before final publication; it was not opened as a full page in Part 3.

Local equity programmes — structure. California equity works at two levels, and they are not the same thing. State level — DCC equity fee relief: DCC’s equity fee relief programme provides fee waivers (if funding is available) and fee deferrals. “If you were negatively affected by the criminalization of cannabis, you can apply to have your license fee waived or deferred.” Verified local-equity participants can prove eligibility by attestation on the Equity Fee Relief Request Form; non-participants must submit documents. “Equity eligibility is valid for 12 months, and licensees must reapply each year for eligibility.” Renewal applicants must show tax documentation verifying gross revenue under $5 million and email the form + documentation to equity@cannabis.ca.gov within 60 days of licence expiration. Sources opened 1 Nov 2026: DCC equity fee relief; DCC state and local equity programmes; DCC how California supports equity businesses. State tax credits linked to DCC fee relief (for Part 2 cross-reference, not local tax): CDTFA vendor compensation: licensed retailers/microbusinesses with retail activity approved for a DCC fee waiver could retain 20% of cannabis excise tax due; programme ends 31 December 2025 per DCC page as crawled. FTB cannabis equity tax credit: $10,000, taxable years through 31 December 2027, for businesses approved for DCC equity fee relief.

Local level — GO-Biz grants: The Governor’s Office of Business and Economic Development (GO-Biz) administers the Cannabis Equity Grants Program for Local Jurisdictions. Local programmes offer: priority processing, reduced/waived local fees, low/no-interest loans or grants, technical assistance, business partnerships, interagency advocacy. An individual cannot be certified as an equity applicant directly by the state in the usual route — certification runs through the city/county programme where the business is located; that local certification then unlocks DCC fee relief and local grant funds. This is stated in secondary synthesis of the programme; Verify against the current GO-Biz solicitation before final publication. Cumulative programme to Dec 2024 (GO-Biz, 4 Mar 2025 release, updated 8 Jun 2026): $123.4 million granted to 34 local jurisdictions; 1,446 Californians licensed who faced barriers. Latest round cited in that release: $18.4 million to 18 jurisdictions, including City and County of San Francisco $3,324,052.50. FY 2026-27 round announced 1 Oct 2026 (secondary report of GO-Biz solicitation): $15 million total; portal opened 8 Oct 2026; applications due 1 Dec 2026, 11:59 p.m.; up to $75,000 for assessment/programme development, up to $3.5 million for existing programmes; awards over $750,000 generally require 1-to-1 local match on the portion above $750,000. Verify against business.ca.gov/CEG before final publication. Sources: GO-Biz, $18.4m awards, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC equity page above.

3.1.5 Limits on local bans of delivery — three different rules, do not conflate

A. Adult-use delivery — general DCC rule, and the 2019 litigation. Former BCC Regulation 5416(d) provided a delivery employee “may deliver to any jurisdiction within the State of California.” In April 2019, 25 local governments (County of Santa Cruz; Cities of Agoura Hills, Angels Camp, Arcadia, Atwater, Beverly Hills, Ceres, Clovis, Covina, Dixon, Downey, McFarland, Newman, Oakdale, Palmdale, Patterson, Riverbank, Riverside, San Pablo, Sonora, Tehachapi, Temecula, Tracy, Turlock, Vacaville) sued in Fresno Superior Court to invalidate it as violating Prop 64’s local-control promise in §§26090 and 26200. Source (League of California Cities report, secondary): League of California Cities, Apr 2019, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

B. The statute that now governs the road: §26090(f). Current §26090 (amended Stats. 2022, Ch. 56, Sec. 6, AB 195, eff. 30 June 2022):

“(f) A local jurisdiction shall not prevent delivery of cannabis or cannabis products on public roads by a licensee acting in compliance with this division and local law as adopted under Section 26200.”

That is narrower than “a city cannot ban delivery.” It protects transit on public roads; it does not, on its face, compel a jurisdiction to allow a delivery business to be based in, or to make retail delivery sales into, its territory for adult-use. DCC’s current delivery regulation is Title 4, §15418 (cited in DCC’s consolidated-regulations announcement as “Supports expanded access to safe and tested products through delivery into regions of the state with limited access to legal cannabis”). Verify the current §15418 text before quoting it. Source for §26090: Justia §26090 — Verify against leginfo before final publication.

C. Medicinal delivery — the hard exception: SB 1186 (2022), the Medicinal Cannabis Patients’ Right of Access Act, §§26320–26325. Enacted Stats. 2022, Ch. 395 (SB 1186, Wiener), effective 1 Jan 2023; operative prohibition from 1 Jan 2024. §26322(a): “A local jurisdiction shall not adopt or enforce any regulation that prohibits the retail sale by delivery within the local jurisdiction of medicinal cannabis to medicinal cannabis patients or their primary caregivers, or that otherwise has the effect of prohibiting [that] retail sale by delivery … in a timely and readily accessible manner and in types and quantities sufficient to meet demand.” §26324: “Nothing in this chapter shall be construed to limit or otherwise affect the ability or right of a local jurisdiction to regulate adult-use cannabis pursuant to Section 26200.” — i.e. full local control is maintained for adult-use delivery. §26325: the chapter addresses a matter of statewide concern, not a municipal affair (Cal. Const. Art. XI, §5). §26323: enforcement by mandamus-type action (Code Civ. Proc. §§1084 et seq.) by a medicinal patient, caregiver, medicinal business, or other beneficially interested party. League of California Cities FAQ (secondary, but summarising the statute): prohibited local rules include limiting the number of businesses authorised to deliver medicinal cannabis, limiting operating hours, limiting number/frequency of delivery sales, and requiring physical premises in the city. Reasonable regulations on time/place/manner consistent with state law/DCC rules are not barred. Sources: League SB 1186 fact sheet, accessed 1 Nov 2026. Verify §§26320–26325 on leginfo before final publication.

Practical consequence for Part 3 tables: a jurisdiction shown on the DCC map as “Prohibited” for retail (storefront) or retail (delivery) is not thereby entitled to block medicinal delivery into it after 1 Jan 2024, and is not entitled to block transit on public roads under §26090(f). Conversely, a jurisdiction that “allows delivery” on the DCC map is recording that it licenses a delivery business type — a different question from whether outside licensees may deliver in.

San Bernardino County’s own FAQ illustrates the tension and must be quoted with care: the County states it “prohibits all commercial cannabis activity, including delivery, within the unincorporated areas” but “cannot prohibit someone from driving through the county on public roads for purposes of delivering cannabis to and from other jurisdictions that allow commercial cannabis activity.” That second sentence is §26090(f); the first sentence, insofar as it purports to ban medicinal delivery sales into the county, is in tension with SB 1186 — flag as Verify / unresolved in the final dossier, do not present the County FAQ as settling SB 1186 compliance. Sources: San Bernardino County FAQ; San Bernardino County enforcement programme, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Local authorisation mechanics. DCC contacts the city/county to confirm local compliance during state review (DCC application guidance, via secondary summary — Verify against current DCC “How to apply” page). If local authorisation is revoked, §26200(c) requires local notice to DCC and DCC to begin suspension/revocation process within 60 days. Local rules commonly add, beyond the state licence: caps on number of businesses, zoning districts, buffer distances from schools/parks/youth facilities (state law sets a 600-foot default that localities may vary — see Part 1), hours (state retail hours 6 a.m.–10 p.m. under Title 4, §15403, cited in the League FAQ), security, signage, labour peace agreements (state threshold 10+ employees for applications/renewals on or after 1 July 2024, BPC §26051.5(a)(5) [AUDIT CORRECTION]; 20+ was the original threshold; some cities lower it — e.g. Palm Springs 5 employees, per a 2022 Cathedral City staff report comparing neighbours — Verify current Palm Springs code), and local business tax.

3.2 Which jurisdictions allow / ban each licence type

3.2.1 What the official DCC map actually says — and what it does NOT say

Opened 1 Nov 2026, DCC “Where cannabis businesses are allowed”: 256 out of 540 cities and counties (47%) allow at least one type of cannabis business. 284 out of 540 (53%) do not allow any type. 302 out of 540 (56%) do not allow any retail cannabis business. 4.27 retail licences per 100,000 people in California (as of Feb 2026). “The Department of Cannabis Control (DCC) updated the data on this page in February 2026. The DCC reviewed city and county laws and websites. The DCC also contacted cities and counties for this information. If you’re a city or county official and think your data is not correct, email locals@cannabis.ca.gov.” Filters offered on the page: Retail (storefront) | Retail (delivery) | Distribution | Testing | Manufacturing | Cultivation — note: consumption is not a filter on this map, and microbusiness/event-organiser are not separate filters. Do not present the map as covering consumption, microbusiness, or event licences.

Conflict to list in research notes: an earlier search-result rendering of the same DCC page showed “Allow: 48%” and a county table beginning “Alameda County | Allowed | Allowed | Allowed | Allowed | Allowed | Allowed” and “Alpine County | Prohibited” on all six columns, with the same February 2026 update note. The page as opened 1 Nov 2026 shows 47% / 256 of 540, not 48%. The opened page is the more recent crawl and is adopted here; the 48% snippet is listed as a conflicting rendering, not discarded silently. The full per-jurisdiction table is rendered inside the interactive map and was not retrievable as text in Part 3 — therefore no complete 540-row allow/ban table is reproduced here. Any final dossier that needs that table must extract it from the interactive map / DCC data download, jurisdiction by jurisdiction, and date-stamp it. Source: DCC map page, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

3.2.2 Jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction findings grounded in official city/county pages opened or returned in Part 3

  • City and County of San Francisco (consolidated — do not split into “city” vs “county”): Allows retail (storefront + delivery), cultivation, manufacturing, distribution, testing — subject to Office of Cannabis permitting. Specific allow/ban by type for SF beyond retail/equity was not extracted as a code table in Part 3 — Verify against SF Office of Cannabis permit types before final publication.
  • Los Angeles — CITY: Allows all main types under LAMC Chapter X, Article 4 (§104 et seq.), phased licensing via Department of Cannabis Regulation (DCR). Adult-use retail taxed at 10% (see §3.4). No citywide ban. Caps/phasing and Social Equity phasing apply — see §3.4. Exact current caps by type — Verify against LAMC §104 and DCR “phases” page.
  • Los Angeles — COUNTY (unincorporated areas only): All commercial cannabis activities remain prohibited in unincorporated LA County until the County’s permitting programme launches. Stated verbatim in substance by both the County (Aug 2022) and DCBA (Oct 2022): “Until LA County fully develops and launches its cannabis licensing program, all commercial cannabis activities, including retail, delivery, manufacturing, and cultivation, remain prohibited in unincorporated LA County.” This is not changed by Measure C (see §3.4) — Measure C is a tax authorisation only; it does not legalise sales. The Board still had to adopt a permitting ordinance. Current launch status in late 2026 — Verify against cannabis.lacounty.gov before final publication; do not assume the 2023 launch date in older County material was met.
  • San Bernardino County (unincorporated areas only): Prohibits all commercial cannabis activity — “including cultivation, production, storage, processing, manufacturing, dispensing, delivery, distribution, testing, transportation, provision and sale — for medical or other purposes in the unincorporated areas,” regardless of profit. Limited exception: a primary caregiver who is owner/operator of a state-licensed health facility, registered with Land Use Services; outdoor cultivation prohibited even for those facilities. Ordinance No. 4309, adopted 23 Aug 2016, effective 22 Sep 2016, adding County Code Chapter 84.34; §84.34.030 prohibition text upheld in County of San Bernardino v. Mancini (2022) (Court of Appeal, injunction against a church dispensary affirmed). Does not apply inside the County’s 24 incorporated cities/towns — each sets its own rules. Sources: San Bernardino County pages above.
  • Fresno — CITY: Allows commercial cannabis, but only as specifically authorised by Fresno Municipal Code Chapter 9, Articles 33 (medicinal) and 34 (adult-use/commercial). §9-3402: “Except as specifically authorized in this Article, the commercial cultivation, manufacture, processing, storing, laboratory testing, labeling, sale, delivery, distribution or transportation (other than as provided under Bus. & Prof. Code section 26090(e)) … is expressly prohibited in the city.” Retail hours in the ordinance text returned: 6 a.m.–10 p.m.; delivery vehicles unmarked. Voters approved Measure A (Cannabis Business License Tax) in Nov 2018, before the retail/commercial ordinance (Bill B-66, Dec 2018, adding Art. 33). Verify current permit caps and whether delivery-only, cultivation, and testing are all presently licensed in the city, against the current Fresno code — the ordinance text returned is the framework, not a live permit count. Sources: Fresno agendas, Art. 34 and Art. 33, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Fresno — COUNTY (unincorporated areas): Commercial cultivation/manufacture/retail prohibited in unincorporated areas by County Ordinance No. 17-001 (secondary summary; Verify the current Fresno County Code text). Incorporated cities in Fresno County set their own rules; secondary summary states only Fresno City allows commercial operations among the County’s 15 incorporated cities — Verify against the DCC map, do not present as settled.
  • Riverside County (unincorporated areas): Secondary summary cites Ordinance No. 925 as prohibiting commercial cultivation in unincorporated Riverside County. Verify — Riverside County has debated and amended its commercial cannabis ordinance since 2019, and Part 3 did not open the current Riverside County Code. Do not publish a Riverside County ban as current law on the basis of the secondary summary alone. The DCC map filter for Riverside County must be checked jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction.
  • Orange County — general: County unincorporated rules are distinct from city rules. The cities that allow storefront retail in the Feb 2026 OC Register / Public Health Institute report-card summary are a handful: Santa Ana, Costa Mesa, Stanton, La Habra (delivery only, no storefronts), Laguna Woods, plus Irvine/Garden Grove delivery-permit rules. Most OC cities prohibit storefronts. That report-card summary is secondary (PHI, Feb 2026) — use it only to direct checks of each city code, not as the legal source.
  • Santa Ana (Orange County): Allows retail, cultivation, distribution, manufacturing, testing laboratories/facilities under Santa Ana Municipal Code Chapter 40, by Regulatory Safety Permit (RSP), 1-year validity, co-location permitted (§40-9.3.1). Retail hours 7 a.m.–11 p.m. daily per code excerpt returned. Earlier city comparative exhibit (undated in search, c. 2021): retail cap 30 (27 active at that time), supply licences uncapped (37 active); adult-use retail tax 8% or $25/sq ft, medicinal retail 6%, cultivation 6% or $10/sq ft, distribution 6% or $4/sq ft, testing 1% or $1.50/sq ft — those comparative figures are stale; current tax rates must be re-checked against the current Chapter 40 / tax code (see §3.4). 600-foot separation from schools; separation between retailers; health warnings; signage restrictions (per PHI report card). Source: Santa Ana Municipal Code Ch. 40; Exhibit 5 comparison, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Costa Mesa (Orange County): Allows distributing, manufacturing, processing, R&D, retail, testing, transporting under Municipal Code Chapter VI (§§9-481 et seq.), by city cannabis business permit + conditional use permit; CEQA review triggered because the permit is discretionary. 1,000-foot separation from schools/parks/youth areas; 600-foot figure in one comparative exhibit conflicts — Verify current §9-486 setbacks. Tax (code §9-29.5 as returned): distributor/manufacturer/R&D lab 1% of gross receipts; storefront retailer 7%; non-storefront (delivery) retailer 7%. Earlier comparative exhibit: retail 7%, cultivation 1%, distribution 1%, manufacturing 1%, testing 1%; “no cap, 0 active, 6–8 approved, 15 in pipeline, 49 applications” at that exhibit’s date — stale, do not publish as current counts. Source: Costa Mesa council attachment, 4 May 2021, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Coachella Valley — Riverside County cities: Cathedral City, Desert Hot Springs, Palm Springs, Palm Desert: allow dispensaries and other businesses (2017 guide, updated by 2022 Cathedral City staff comparison — see §3.4 for taxes). Indian Wells, Indio, La Quinta, Rancho Mirage, and unincorporated county land: did not allow dispensaries/businesses in the 2017 guide. Verify all seven against current codes/DCC map before final publication — Indio in particular has since considered/adopted retail rules, and the 2017 position must not be carried forward unchecked. Coachella (city): allows medical cultivation, manufacturing, testing under Municipal Code Chapters 5.68 & 5.69 by Commercial Cannabis Activity Regulatory Permit; historically banned retail/delivery, then considered downtown delivery/dispensary proposals (2017). Current retail status — Verify against current Coachella code. Desert Hot Springs: allows cultivation, manufacturing, testing, dispensing; also a specific “Cannabis Hotel” regulatory permit + conditional use permit, in commercial districts (excluding Downtown Commercial), Mixed-Use Corridor, or Visitor-Serving Mixed-Use districts. Sources: Coachella document; City of Desert Hot Springs Q&A, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • South Lake Tahoe (El Dorado County): City allows retail subject to city permits; County (unincorporated, including the South Lake Tahoe area outside city limits) allows retail storefront/delivery under County Code Title 130, Chapters 130.41/130.42, by Commercial Cannabis Use Permit + Annual Operating Permit + business licence, then state licence. Type 10 storefronts in CC, CR, CG, IL, MAP-1 zones, 8 a.m.–8 p.m. daily per secondary summary of Chapter 130.41; cultivation setback 1,500 feet from schools/bus stops/places in the county application form. A specific South Lake Tahoe-area retail storefront use permit (CCUP19-0008, Eldo Lake Investment, LLC, APN 033-050-023, west side of US 50, ~430 ft SW of Jewell Rd) was approved by the Planning Commission 8 Sep 2022. Sources: El Dorado County pre-application; El Dorado Legistar, CCUP19-0008, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Humboldt / Mendocino / Trinity — general allow position: All three allow commercial cultivation (the Emerald Triangle permitting counties) under county cannabis divisions/ordinances, with local cultivation permits that are the local-authorisation layer for DCC licences. Specific caps, zoning, and permit types are county-code matters — see §3.4 for taxes/equity. Do not generalise from “allows cultivation” to “allows retail/manufacturing/testing” without checking each county’s ordinance — Mendocino’s programme, for example, is cultivation-permit-led (CCBL), and Trinity’s is a Cannabis Division permit programme.

3.2.3 Local election results that ground allow/ban changes (official or official-adjacent)

  • Los Angeles County, Measure C, 8 Nov 2022: cannabis business tax for unincorporated areas — approved (secondary reports: ~60% in favour at first tallies). It authorises tax only; it does not authorise businesses. See §3.4 for rates.
  • City of Fresno, Measure A, 6 Nov 2018: Cannabis Business License Tax — approved, enabling the city’s tax ordinance (Fresno Legistar file ID18-0750, 14 June 2018 council report calling the election).
  • City of San Jose, Measure U, 2 Nov 2010: marijuana business tax up to 10% — approved (secondary history; Verify against Santa Clara County Registrar / City Clerk).
  • City of Long Beach, Measure MA, 8 Nov 2016: gross-receipts tax on medical and non-medical cannabis — approved (Long Beach Finance page).
  • City of San Diego, cannabis tax, 2016: voter-approved programme starting at 5% (see §3.4).
  • Humboldt County, Measure S, 8 Nov 2016: commercial marijuana cultivation tax — approved; Board later set rate to zero (see §3.4).
  • Monterey County, Measure Y, 8 Nov 2016: commercial cannabis tax — approved; implementation of the County’s medical ordinance was tied to its passage. Salinas, Measure L (2016, in the same election cycle): cannabis business tax — approved per secondary summary; Verify both against County/City election results.
  • Santa Barbara County, Ordinance 5026 (2018): tax on cannabis operations, Chapter 50A — Board-adopted under Rev. & Tax. Code §34021.5, subject to electorate approval as a general tax.

Gap: No comprehensive official list of local cannabis ballot measures (city-by-city, with Yes/No percentages) was retrieved in Part 3. The Secretary of State’s ballot-measure database and each county Registrar-Recorder are the follow-up sources for a complete election table. Do not fill this gap from news roundups.

3.3 Official DCC / other list or map of local cannabis rules

Retrieved — this is the official list/map:

  • Title: “Where cannabis businesses are allowed” — Department of Cannabis Control
  • URL: cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/where-cannabis-businesses-are-allowed
  • Format: Interactive map + filter by business type (storefront retail, delivery retail, distribution, testing, manufacturing, cultivation) + county-detail maps + city/county search box. No CSV/XLSX download button was visible on the page as opened. The underlying per-jurisdiction table is rendered inside the map widget and was not extractable as text in Part 3.
  • Update stated on page: February 2026. Method stated: DCC reviewed city/county laws and websites and contacted cities/counties. Corrections contact: locals@cannabis.ca.gov.
  • Headline figures on page as opened: 47% (256/540) allow ≥1 type; 53% (284/540) allow none; 56% (302/540) allow no retail; 4.27 retail licences per 100,000 people (Feb 2026).
  • Limitations: does not filter by consumption, microbusiness, or event organiser; does not show caps, zoning, fees, or tax rates; lags ordinance changes (DCC’s own caveat in secondary guidance); a city and its county are separate rows.

No other official statewide list/map was found. In particular: there is no official DCC downloadable “local ordinances” spreadsheet linked from that page as opened. County/city codes (Municode / Qcode / CivicPlus / city clerk sites) are the authoritative source for each jurisdiction’s ordinance text — the DCC map is a finding aid, not the ordinance. Third-party maps that repackage DCC data (e.g. Schwass Law county map, claiming 20,432 active DCC licences, DCC data Mar/Apr 2026) are not official sources and must not be cited as DCC figures in the final dossier, even where they claim DCC sourcing.

3.4 Local business taxes, fees, and equity programmes — jurisdiction by jurisdiction

How to read this section: “Tax” here means the local cannabis business tax / gross-receipts tax / per-square-foot cultivation tax imposed by the city/county, in addition to state excise tax and state/local sales tax. Where a rate changed, both the old and new rates and effective dates are given. Where only a secondary source was available, it is flagged.

Los Angeles — CITY

Local tax (LAMC, Measure M framework): adult-use retail 10% of gross receipts; medical retail 5%; manufacturing and cultivation 2%; transportation, testing, research 1%. Stated in multiple city sources: LA Office of Finance report (2 Oct 2025, via Council File 25-0600-S125) — “Tax rates vary from 1 percent to 10 percent of gross receipts depending on the specific goods and services provided, with retail sales of cannabis for adult use, the largest category, taxed at the highest rate”; and Cannabis Business Times summary of the same structure (Mar 2026). Verify the current LAMC §21.XX tax table before final publication — the 10/5/2/1 split is consistently reported but was not opened as a current code page in Part 3.

Scale / delinquency (city official, Oct 2025): 738 licensed, registered cannabis business accounts (Office of Finance, 2 Oct 2025); taxes filed monthly on prior month’s gross receipts; over 500 businesses delinquent, collectively owing ~$400 million (~$35m interest, ~$100m penalties; 329 owe <$200,000, 48 owe >$2m each — city figures via Council File 25-0600-S125 and press summary).

Amnesty (2026): Council instructed staff Mar 2026 (13-0) and adopted 1 July 2026 (14-0, CF 25-0600-S125) a business-tax amnesty: full waiver of penalties/interest, instalment agreements up to 36 months (up to 5 years for small businesses with large debts in the July item summary); receipts programmed 20% General Fund/Office of Finance, 40% LAPD/City Attorney illegal-enforcement, 40% Community Investment / EWDD Social Equity grants. Office of Finance to report within 45 days after application deadline. Verify whether the amnesty ordinance itself (not just the instruction) has been adopted and its application deadline before final publication.

DCR fees: a 2024 DCR fee study discussed in public comments proposed annual DCR licensing fees $8,486–$11,806 per activity (excluding LADBS, Health permits). That figure comes from a public comment on Council File 25-0418, not from a DCR fee schedule opened in Part 3 — flag as Verify / non-official for fee purposes.

Equity — City of Los Angeles Social Equity Program (Department of Cannabis Regulation, DCR): phased licensing with Social Equity Applicants prioritised; the Cannabis Regulation Commission’s July 2025 Urgent Policy Recommendations (cited in Feb 2026 council material) warned the licensed market would collapse without tax/enforcement action and proposed, among other things, tiered rates (max 6% adult-use / 3% medical for highest-revenue businesses; 0.5% / 0.25% under $500,000) — that is a recommendation, not adopted law; do not present it as the current rate. State GO-Biz equity grants to LA: largest award in the FY 2026-27 reporting round cited was $3.5 million to Los Angeles (secondary report of GO-Biz round; Verify against GO-Biz award list). Local authorisation: DCR licence required for a Business Tax Registration Certificate (BTRC). Sources: LA Council File 25-0600-S125; LA City Clerk, CF 25-0600-S125, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Los Angeles — COUNTY (unincorporated areas)

Status: commercial cannabis prohibited until the County permitting programme launches (see §3.2.2). Tax therefore applies only once businesses are permitted.

Measure C, 8 Nov 2022 — initial rates (1 July 2023–30 June 2026, per County/DCBA): Retail 4% of gross receipts; Manufacturing 3%; Distribution 3%; Testing 1%; Cultivation $7/sq ft canopy (indoor artificial light); $4/sq ft (mixed light); $4/sq ft (outdoor) — with a County footnote that outdoor cultivation would not be permitted in the initial launch; Any other cannabis business 4%; Expected revenue: $10.36m–$15.17m annually (County ballot summary).

Maximum rates after 1 July 2026 (Board may decrease or increase up to these maxima, and from July 2027 cultivation maxima adjust for inflation): Retail 6%; Manufacturing 4%; Distribution 3%; Testing 2%; Cultivation $10/sq ft indoor, $7 mixed-light, $4 outdoor (as stated in the Aug 2022 County release; the $4 outdoor maximum vs $4 initial rate should be re-checked — see conflict note); Any other 4%.

Equity — LA County Office of Cannabis Management (OCM), Department of Consumer and Business Affairs: directed Feb 2022 to develop an equitable licensing programme for unincorporated areas, including a Cannabis Equity Program with priority licensing, business development/technical assistance, pro bono legal assistance, access to capital, and workforce pathways. Initial permit concept reported in secondary coverage: up to 25 storefront retail, 25 delivery retail, 10 indoor/mixed-light cultivation, 10 manufacturing, 10 distribution, 10 testing — Verify against the adopted County ordinance; do not publish as adopted caps. Sources: LA County, Measure C, Aug 2022; LA County DCBA, Measure C, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

San Francisco (City and County — consolidated)

Local tax — authorised 2018, implementation repeatedly deferred/suspended: Voter-authorised 2018: 1–5% gross-receipts levy (Budget & Finance Committee presentation, Dec 2025, via secondary summary). Structure as adopted before deferral: retail sales up to $1m at 2.5%, above $1m at 5% (2020 ordinance summary); other cannabis revenue 1% up to $1m / 1.5% above (same summary — Verify against SF Business & Tax Regulations Code, Art. 28). Dec 2025 development — Verify before final publication: Budget & Finance Committee 3 Dec 2025 amended an ordinance to suspend the local cannabis business tax through 31 Dec 2035 (retroactive to 1 Jan 2026) and referred it to the full Board without recommendation (3-0). Controller/BLA fiscal note cited: ~$3.7m General Fund loss next fiscal year, ~$8.7m the following full year, if suspended. Whether the full Board adopted suspension is NOT established in Part 3 — do not state SF tax as “suspended” or “in effect” for 2026 without checking the Board’s final action. Market context cited in that committee debate (secondary): SF sales down ~12% locally over three years vs ~6% statewide; illicit share estimated 50–60% of local sales in the presentation.

Equity — San Francisco Office of Cannabis (OOC), Cannabis Equity Program: Verified Equity Applicants (VEAs) — verification criteria include income, cannabis-related criminal-justice involvement, family conviction history, housing insecurity, SFUSD attendance, or residence in eligible census tracts (OOC briefing summary). Early programme design (2017–18): 1-to-1 ratio of grandfathered dispensaries to equity-owned shops; priority permitting for equity applicants. GO-Biz grants: OOC “has dispersed $12,000,000 in direct grants” to date (OOC Director, July 2025 committee); Round 6 award $3,324,052.50 (GO-Biz, Mar 2025), term 1 Apr 2025–31 Oct 2026, awards anticipated $5,000–$69,000 per grantee, with ~$1.8m local match (staffing + $500,000 tax waivers already approved). Technical assistance: OOC RFP Jan 2025 for VEA technical assistance (permitting, business development, security, legal), $150,000 GO-Biz-funded, to be used by Oct 2025. Sources: GO-Biz awards page above; SF committee summary via Citizen Portal summary, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY — Verify against SF Board file).

Oakland (City)

Local tax — 2026 Cannabis Business Tax Renewal (City Finance, 10 Dec 2025 notice, opened via search): rates unchanged from 2022 for 2026; declaration/payment postmarked by 2 Mar 2026; payment required to maintain local authorisation/permit.

CategoryEquity: $0–$1.5mEquity / General: $1.5m–$5mGeneral: $0–$750k / $750k–$1.5mOver $5m
Retail storefront & delivery, manufacturing, indoor/outdoor cultivation, packaging & distribution/storage0.12% (all categories)Retail 5.00%, Manufacturing 5.00%, Indoor Cultivation 4.50%, Outdoor Cultivation 4.50%, Packaging/Distribution/Storage 3.00%$0–$750k: 0.12%; $750k–$1.5m: Retail/Manufacturing 4.00%, Indoor/Outdoor Cultivation 3.50%, Packaging/Distribution 2.50%5.00% flat (non-marginal) on retail/indoor/manufacturing; 4.00% on distribution (as rendered in the notice — re-check the notice PDF table alignment before final publication; the search rendering interleaves columns)

Rebates (2026): four programmes adopted 10 Dec 2019, nine conditions; e.g. workforce equity-employee thresholds (30%/25%/20% = 0.50% rebate tiers), delivery-by-equity-business value thresholds; floor: no rebate may reduce the overall rate below 2.5% for 2026. Incubation rebate by 2025 gross receipts: <$2m = 1.5%, up to $7.5m = 1%, $7.5m–$12.5m = 0.5%, >$12.5m = none. Code: Oakland Municipal Code §§5.04.240, 5.04.245 (payment/renewal), 5.04.267 (rebates), 5.80.050 & 5.81.060 (Equity Business definition).

Equity — Oakland Cannabis Equity Program (first in the nation, Spring 2017): Equity Applicant = Oakland resident, income ≤80% of Oakland AMI (household-size adjusted), living in designated police beats (21 beats in early summaries; 10 of past 20 years) and/or convicted of a cannabis crime in Oakland since 5 Nov 1996. 50% rule: at any point in the Initial Permitting Phase, a minimum of 50% of all permits collectively issued under OMC Chapters 5.80/5.81 shall be issued to Equity Applicants; a General Applicant serving as an Equity Incubator receives the next available General local authorisation. Incubator = providing free rent/real estate or equivalent support. GO-Biz #6: City received $2,074,369.75 in Spring 2025, period ending 31 Oct 2026, first-come-first-served grants for start-up/ongoing costs (rent, fees/taxes, legal, compliance, testing, capital improvements, workforce). Eligibility: valid annual state licence + valid Oakland Annual/Conditional Operating/Non-Operating Permit. No new loan applications being processed (lack of funding) as of the City page crawled. Earlier GO-Biz #5 FAQ (June 2024): max $50,000 per equity applicant regardless of locations/permits. Sources: Oakland 2026 tax renewal PDF; Oakland 2026 rebate information PDF; Oakland grant/loan opportunities, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

San Jose (City)

Local tax: 10% of gross receipts on dispensary sales (Measure U, 2010, up to 10%). Consistently reported in City budget coverage: 16 dispensaries (2023), $16.6m tax revenue, projected $15.8m (2024-25 budget, ~13% decrease from 2022-23); regulatory fees described as “highest of any city at roughly $140,000” (San José Spotlight, Feb 2024, citing city documents — Verify the fee figure against the current San Jose fee schedule; it is a secondary figure). Regulation: Cannabis Division under the Police Department; 16 licensed shops in the Feb 2024 report. Council 9 Jan 2024 approved payment-plan rules allowing businesses behind on taxes/fees to remain registered, and second-storefront expansion for registered businesses. Equity — San Jose Cannabis Equity Assistance Program: Council voted 26 Mar 2019 to create it (ordinances defining “equity applicants” / “equity business owners”). Eligibility (2019 summary): lived ≥4 years in a low-income San Jose area, or attended public school ≥4 years in a low-income area, or arrested/convicted for a cannabis crime, or family member convicted. Provides job training, technical, financial, employment assistance. State pressure: SB 1294 (California Cannabis Equity Act, 2018) — $10m statewide grants, one-month application window Mar 2019. Current funding/grant amounts — Verify against City of San Jose Office of Economic Development / GO-Biz award list. Sources (secondary for tax/equity history — Verify against City of San Jose code/fee schedule): San José Spotlight, Feb 2024, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

San Diego — CITY

Local tax — history and current rate: Voter-approved programme 2016: 5% of gross receipts. Increased to 8% on 1 July 2019. Council voted 8-1 on 10 Mar 2025 to increase to 10%, effective 1 May 2025; Municipal Code authority to adjust up to 15% maximum. Revenue: $17m (FY2023, 23 retailers), $15.9m (FY2024), $7.4m (first 7 months FY2025 through 31 Jan, 25 retailers) — City Treasurer report via Cannabis Business Times, Mar 2025. Citywide cap 36 dispensary permits; 28 active DCC dispensary licences listed for San Diego in early Mar 2025 (same report). Shortfall after the increase (secondary, Dec 2025): Independent Budget Analyst found revenue $1.5m below projected $21.3m for the fiscal year; factors cited: illegal-market competition, wholesale price drop from oversupply. Hours extended to 6 a.m.–10 p.m. from Aug 2025 (same summary). Neighbouring-city comparison in the Treasurer’s Feb 2025 report: La Mesa 4%, Lemon Grove 5%, Chula Vista 7%, unincorporated San Diego County 2%. Equity — City SEED (Social Equity and Economic Development) programme: described in 2025–26 advocacy summary as needing revival / reintroduction; stakeholders urged to advocate for its return at Council. Current SEED status, eligibility, and funding — Verify against City of San Diego Development Services / SEED page before final publication; do not present SEED as currently operating on the basis of the advocacy summary. Consumption: Sessions By the Bay described (Jan 2026 advocacy summary) as “the first and only consumption lounge in all of San Diego” — Verify against City permits; consumption is a local opt-in (see §3.1.2). Source (secondary for the 2025 increase — Verify against San Diego Municipal Code / Treasurer’s report): Cannabis Business Times, Mar 2025, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

San Diego — COUNTY (unincorporated areas)

Local tax: 2% of gross receipts for retail in unincorporated areas (Treasurer comparison, Feb 2025; East County Magazine, July 2026 summary). Status: historically minimal legal dispensary presence in unincorporated areas due to restrictive zoning; County was preparing a Cannabis Environmental Impact Report (EIR) with a hearing scheduled 14 Jan 2026, covering zoning (buffers, sensitive-use definitions), tax rebates/grants for equity businesses, and a social equity programme for the unincorporated county. Final EIR expected before the Board later in 2026. Adoption status — Verify against County of San Diego Planning & Development Services. Equity: County equity programme was in development via the EIR process, not yet an operating permit programme in the sources returned.

Sacramento — CITY

Local tax — Cannabis Business Operations Tax (CBOT): 4% of gross receipts for all cannabis businesses (City Council reports 2020, 2023). Transfers between separate licensed businesses are taxable events at each transfer point (2023 Law & Legislation Committee report, File 2023-00751). Gross receipts self-reported monthly, due end of following month. Storefront dispensary annual BOT range cited in 2020: $81,000–$650,000 per storefront. Phased CORE relief (proposal, not yet confirmed as adopted): 5 Sep 2023 committee item proposed a Phased Tax model for CORE businesses: 0% Year 1, +1% per year to 4%. Verify whether Council adopted the phased model — do not present it as current law. Permits: storefront dispensary cap was 30 under SCC §5.150.350; Council unanimously approved +10 storefront permits in 2020, exclusive to CORE participants, toward a goal of 50% CORE-eligible ownership of all cannabis permits. ~160 CORE graduates at that time (programme operated by Greater Sacramento Urban League + Sacramento Asian Chamber of Commerce). Equity — Cannabis Opportunity Reinvestment and Equity (CORE) Program: Council authorised creation 28 Nov 2017; established by Resolution 2018-0323 (9 Aug 2018) with zero-dollar permit fees for CORE participants and appropriated funding. Contract awarded 26 Mar 2019 to Sacramento Asian Chamber of Commerce (SACC) + Greater Sacramento Urban League (GSUL) to administer. Purpose: assist individuals/communities facing barriers due to disproportionate enforcement of cannabis crimes before Prop 64. GO-Biz round cited in secondary reporting: $2.5 million to Sacramento in the FY 2025-26 round summary — Verify against GO-Biz award list. Sources: Sacramento File 2023-00751; Sacramento File 2020-00917, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Sacramento — COUNTY

Gap / Verify: No Sacramento County (unincorporated) cannabis tax, permit, or equity figure was retrieved in Part 3. The City of Sacramento programme above must not be attributed to the County. Follow-up: Sacramento County Office of Cannabis / County Code, and the DCC map filter for Sacramento County unincorporated areas.

Long Beach (City)

Local tax — Measure MA (8 Nov 2016), LBMC §3.80.261 (Long Beach Finance page, opened via search): Medical retail (dispensary and/or delivery): 6% of gross receipts; Adult-use retail (dispensary and/or delivery): 7% of gross receipts — reduced from 8% by Council ordinance approved 9 Apr 2024, effective 23 May 2024 (the same ordinance reduced cultivation tax from $14.96 to $13.09 per sq ft); Medical/adult-use cultivation: $13.09 per sq ft under cultivation (2024 rate; originally $12.00/sq ft, CPI-adjusted — Long Beach Legistar File 23-0379); Minimum tax: $1,000 annually for all cannabis businesses; Equity half-rate: equity businesses meeting LBMC §5.92.1615 pay half the rate set for non-equity businesses (medical up to 8% cap / adult-use up to 12% cap in code; initial rates 6%/8% as above). Council direction Apr 2023 (File 23-0379) to amend §3.80.261 to halve adult-use + medical cultivation and retail taxes for equity businesses; Pilot Tax Credit Program (9 Apr 2024 Cannabis Tax Relief Plan): 1% reduction above + 3% tax credit for qualifying cultivation/adult-use retail businesses in good standing (combined potential 4% relief); credits applied on remittance of quarterly/annual taxes + gross-receipts reports; Revenue: $11,826,370 Measure MA revenue FY2024 (City Council report via student-press summary — Verify against City Finance CAFR), allocated to city services including Racial Equity and Reconciliation Initiative, homeless assistance, police funding.

Equity — Long Beach Cannabis Social Equity Program: Equity Dispensary Ordinance/Resolution (Council 17 May 2022, File 22-0522): 8 additional storefront dispensaries owned exclusively by verified equity applicants, RFP to begin July 2022 after ordinance effective; equity business = ≥51% ownership by one equity applicant (LBMC §3.80 definition); transfer restriction: equity businesses may not transfer >51% to a non-equity individual for 10 years from application date (as amended). Equity dispensary operators charged the same headline tax rates as other operators (6% medical / 8% adult-use at the 2022 file date) before the half-rate amendment — see tax paragraph above for the current half-rate position. Sources: Long Beach Finance, cannabis taxes; Long Beach Legistar File 23-0379; Long Beach Legistar File 22-0522, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Fresno — CITY (tax, supplementing §3.2.2)

Measure A tax authorisation (2018): Council may set rates up to 10% of gross receipts for retailers, testing labs, distributors, manufacturers, and up to $12.00/sq ft canopy/year for cultivators (CPI escalator), per the June 2018 council report (File ID18-0750). Current rates actually set by Council under that authorisation — Verify against current Fresno Municipal Code Chapter 7, Article 14.

Riverside County

Gap / Verify: No current Riverside County (unincorporated) cannabis tax or equity figure was retrieved in Part 3. Do not carry forward the secondary Ordinance 925 ban summary as a tax/equity statement. Follow-up: Riverside County Code / County Cannabis Programme page + DCC map.

San Bernardino County

Tax/equity: No county cannabis business tax applies because commercial activity is prohibited in unincorporated areas (see §3.2.2). Incorporated cities (e.g. San Bernardino city, Adelanto, Needles, Barstow) set their own taxes — not covered in Part 3; Verify city-by-city. The State Auditor’s Feb 2025 Table 1 (Report 2024-048) is a useful official source for provisional-licence transition by jurisdiction, including Adelanto (118 provisional licences 1 Jan 2023; 60 active 30 June 2024) — see §4.7 in the Part 3 working notes / §3.5 below.

Orange County cities — Santa Ana & Costa Mesa (tax, supplementing §3.2.2)

Santa Ana: see §3.2.2 for the stale comparative exhibit (adult-use retail 8% or $25/sq ft; medicinal retail 6%; cultivation 6% or $10/sq ft; distribution 6% or $4/sq ft; testing 1% or $1.50/sq ft). Current rates — Verify against Santa Ana Municipal Code tax chapter. Revenue context (PHI, Feb 2026): ~$10m/year for youth services/public safety. Costa Mesa: §9-29.5 — distributor/manufacturer/R&D 1%, storefront retail 7%, non-storefront retail 7% (code text returned). Revenue context (PHI, Feb 2026): ~$3.6m/year. Other OC cities in the PHI summary (secondary, Feb 2026): Stanton >$1m/year, La Habra >$1m/year, Laguna Woods ~$750,000/year. Verify each city’s tax code; do not publish PHI revenue figures as official city finance figures.

Humboldt County

Tax — Measure S repeal (the most important Humboldt change in this period): Measure S (voter-approved 8 Nov 2016) authorised a commercial cannabis cultivation tax. Board voted Oct 2025 (unanimous) to set the Measure S rate to $0 effective 1 Jan 2026 — described by staff as essentially repealing the tax without using repeal language, because a voter initiative cannot be repealed without a countywide vote. Board introduced an ordinance 27 Jan 2026 to remove the Measure S chapter from the County Code (leaving a placeholder), approved by unanimous consent (Supervisor Bushnell recused), set for adoption 10 Feb 2026. The repeal text as quoted in press coverage: “the Humboldt County Commercial Marijuana Cultivation Tax Ordinance is hereby repealed in its entirety. The repeal is effective for the calendar year 2026 and each subsequent year until such time as the board reenacts the tax, which it may do without voter approval as long as the reenacted provision does not exceed the amount or scope of what the voters approved in Measure S.” Verify the 10 Feb 2026 adoption against Humboldt County Board minutes before final publication; the Jan 2026 item was introduction, not adoption. Permitting/equity context: Humboldt County Cannabis Division administers local cultivation permits; the County had 597 active provisional licences 1 Jan 2023, 208 active 30 June 2024 (State Auditor Table 1) — the highest provisional count of any jurisdiction in that table, reflecting the scale of the legacy-cultivator transition problem. No Humboldt-specific GO-Biz equity grant amount was retrieved in Part 3 — Verify against GO-Biz award lists. Sources: Citizen Portal summary of 27 Jan 2026 meeting, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (SECONDARY — Verify against Board minutes); State Auditor Table 1, Feb 2025, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Mendocino County

Tax: County cannabis business tax with a “true-up” / minimum tax for cultivators — the subject of sustained cultivator opposition in Mar 2026 County committee material (“please remove the True-up tax… requirement to pay taxes on non-existent sales”). Cultivator letters in that material state the County has reduced excise tax over the past 4 years, but the burden remains prohibitive; they cite Humboldt’s repeal and Trinity’s 80% reduction as comparators. Current Mendocino tax rates and true-up mechanics — Verify against Mendocino County Code Chapter 10A / Treasurer-Tax Collector page; no rate is stated here because none was retrieved from an official page.

Equity — Local Equity Entrepreneur Program (LEEP): Funded by GO-Biz Cannabis Equity Grants Program for Local Jurisdictions; County applied in all six rounds opened to date, five funded, over $8 million awarded (plus $100,000 County matching funds from the Board). Over $6.76 million distributed to verified local equity applicants via direct grants + fee relief as of Feb 2026 (Cannabis Division Monthly Activity Report, Feb 2026). Round 6: $612,689.88 (Grant CEG-2025-620, Type 2 Assistance), award notified 23 Jan 2025, term ending 31 Oct 2026; all Round 6 direct-grant agreements executed by Feb 2026; $92,237.88 allocated to fee relief for equity-verified applicants. Programme offers: fee relief, direct grants for start-up/ongoing business expenses, direct technical assistance tailored to individual needs. Source: Mendocino County Cannabis Division Monthly Activity Report, Feb 2026, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Trinity County

Tax: Cultivator correspondence in Mendocino County material (Mar 2026) states “Trinity County lowered their cannabis tax by 80%.” No Trinity rate, ordinance, or effective date was retrieved from a Trinity County official page in Part 3 — Verify against Trinity County Code / Treasurer-Tax Collector before final publication. Equity — Trinity County Cannabis Division Local Equity Program: GO-Biz award notified (County Cannabis Division page, 2026 update): additional $950,000 from GO-Biz. Survey 12–13 Jan 2026 of Local Equity Program participants: ~65% preferred 100% fee waiver until funds exhausted over a reduced waiver spread longer. Effective 21 Jan 2026, licence fee waivers returned to 100% for renewals (County memo, Feb 2026 Equity Update). Contact on the County page: Cannabis Division, (530) 623-1351 (public office number — retained because it is an official public contact, not a personal number). Source: Trinity County Cannabis Division, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Santa Barbara County (unincorporated areas)

Tax — Ordinance 5026, County Code Chapter 50A (County Treasurer-Tax Collector / ReadySBC page): Tax on cannabis operations in unincorporated areas, on gross receipts from transfers from a County licence to any other licence. Taxes are cumulative; all transfers are taxable, including gratuitous transfers (valued at reasonable assigned value if not invoiced), with limited non-taxable circumstances. Rates (gross receipts): Nursery 1%; Manufacturing 3%; Cultivation 4%; Retail 6%; Microbusiness 6%; (Distribution rate was truncated in the search rendering — Verify the full Chapter 50A rate table; do not assume distribution = retail.) Quarterly reports due 1 Jan / 1 Apr / 1 July / 1 Oct, delinquent if not remitted by 5 p.m. on the last day of those months. Reports + Metrc transfer reports with pricing to Cannabistax@countyofsb.org (public programme email). Q1 FY2025-26 collections reported to the Board 27 Jan 2026: ~$1.7 million, ~33% of adopted cannabis budget projection; 47 permitted operators in the quarter, 40 with required receipts submitted (secondary summary of Board meeting — Verify against Board minutes). City of Santa Barbara (distinct from County): Commercial Cannabis Business Tax — Non-Medical Retail & Delivery 8% effective 1 July 2026 (City Finance page, crawled 2026); Cultivation 2% (same page, truncated — Verify full City rate table, including medical retail, manufacturing, distribution). Sources: ReadySBC cannabis tax; Ordinance 5026 PDF; City of Santa Barbara cannabis tax, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Monterey County (unincorporated areas) & Salinas (City)

Monterey County — Measure Y tax (County Code Chapter 7.100): Original phased design (2016, HdL model): sales tax starting 5% of gross receipts, +2.5% per fiscal year from 1 July 2020 to max 10%; general cultivation starting $15/sq ft canopy, +$5/year to max $25/sq ft; nursery $2/sq ft to max $5/sq ft. 2020 freeze: Board ordinance (File 20-424, introduced 5 May 2020, for adoption 19 May 2020) suspended FY2020-21 automatic increases, resuming FY2021-22 on 1 July 2022 (with the specific 2021 increases then prescribed: indoor/mixed-light cultivation + nurseries +$1.00 on 1 July 2021; manufacturers/distributors +1%; retail +0.5%). Estimated FY20-21 revenue impact of the freeze: ~$4m loss. Current (2026) rates after all phased increases and freezes — Verify against current Chapter 7.100; do not publish the 2016 phased maxima as current rates. Programme: County Cannabis Program (Cannabis Program Manager); cultivation limited to indoor/greenhouse in the original ordinance (outdoor commercial grows excluded pending further Board action — 2016 position, Verify current position). State Auditor Table 1: Monterey County had 392 active provisional licences 1 Jan 2023, 50 active 30 June 2024 (87% transitioned in the period).

Salinas — City: Ordinance Jan 2016 permitting limited commercial cannabis: cultivation, manufacturing, delivery (retail by delivery only, no storefront in the original ordinance), dispensary — total 12 permits in the original scheme (3 each for dispensary, growing, distribution/delivery, manufacturing) per 2016 Council coverage. Measure L tax (Nov 2016 ballot, via secondary summaries): cultivation $15/sq ft for Years 1–3, ceiling $25/sq ft thereafter (Council may increase up to $25/sq ft or 10%); other business types 5% Years 1–3, max 10%. Verify current Salinas Municipal Code tax rates and permit caps — the 12-permit / delivery-only structure has been amended since 2016 and must not be carried forward unchecked. Salinas is the Monterey County seat and the Salinas Valley greenhouse-cultivation centre — that geographic fact is context, not a licence count (see §3.5). Sources: Monterey County File 20-424, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Palm Springs, Coachella, Desert Hot Springs (Riverside County cities)

Palm Springs: Allows cultivation, manufacturing, testing, retail. Early medical-cooperative city; dispensary cap of 6 removed in preparation for adult-use; distance requirements between dispensaries retained. Tax (Measure E, Nov 2016; 2022 Cathedral City staff comparison): retail 10% of gross proceeds; cultivation $10/sq ft (2022 comparison: $60,000/year on a 6,000 sq ft facility). Labour peace agreement threshold: 5+ employees (vs state 20) per 2022 Cathedral City staff survey of neighbours — Verify current Palm Springs Municipal Code §5.55. Current rates/caps — Verify against current Palm Springs code; the 10%/$10 figures are 2022-comparison figures, not a 2026 code opening.

Coachella: Tax (2022 comparison): retail 6% of gross proceeds; cultivation $15/sq ft up to 20,000 sq ft, $7.50/sq ft over ($90,000/year on 6,000 sq ft at the $15 rate). Permitting: Commercial Cannabis Activity Regulatory Permit, Municipal Code Chapters 5.68 & 5.69; application form covers cultivation, manufacturing, distribution, retail, testing, etc. (form returned).

Desert Hot Springs: Allows cultivation, manufacturing, testing, dispensing; early municipal cultivation-permitting city. Tax (2017 guide, updated by 2022 comparison): retail 10%; cultivation $10.20/sq ft in the 2022 comparison ($61,200/year on 6,000 sq ft) vs $25/sq ft for first 3,000 sq ft + $10/sq ft thereafter in the 2017 guide — conflict: the 2017 tiered $25/$10 structure and the 2022 flat $10.20 figure cannot both be current; Verify against current Desert Hot Springs Municipal Code. No manufacturing tax is claimed in a 2026 property listing (“No Manufacturing Tax in the City of Desert Hot Springs”) — that is a marketing claim, not a code citation; Verify. Permits: Conditional Use Permit (Planning) + Regulatory Permit (Cannabis Compliance); Cannabis Hotel Regulatory Permit is a distinct permit type; unlimited cultivation permits claimed under Ordinances 552, 553 & 581 in marketing material — Verify against current code. Sources: Cathedral City staff report, 11 May 2022, taxes in neighbouring jurisdictions; Coachella document; City of Desert Hot Springs Q&A, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

South Lake Tahoe (City) & El Dorado County (unincorporated)

El Dorado County (unincorporated) — Commercial Cannabis Activities Tax, County Code §3.22 (Treasurer-Tax Collector page + Municode): Applies to any person engaged in commercial cannabis activity in the unincorporated area, whether permitted or not, and including businesses without a physical presence in the unincorporated area if they engage in activity there. Current rates as rendered: Nurseries 4% of gross receipts; Manufacturing Level 1 2.5%; Manufacturing Level 2 0% (exempt under §3.22.050); Retail store or delivery-only 4%; Distributor 2.0%; Testing laboratories 0.5%. Retailer rate band in code: retailer 4%–10%, delivery-only retailer 0.5%–10%; Board may set/adjust by resolution/ordinance within minima/maxima, with different rates by activity, state licence type, or medicinal vs adult-use. Transport through the County on public roads without delivery in the County is excluded from “commercial cannabis activity” (§130.41.100(2)) — consistent with §26090(f).

South Lake Tahoe (City): city permits/taxes are separate from the County tax above. City of South Lake Tahoe cannabis tax rate and permit cap were not retrieved in Part 3 — Verify against City of South Lake Tahoe Municipal Code / Finance page. Do not attribute the County 4% retail rate to the City. Sources: El Dorado County Treasurer-Tax Collector, commercial cannabis tax, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

3.5 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say

Rule applied in Part 3: no ranking, clustering estimate, or “top county” claim is made from licence-search counts compiled by third parties. The following are the only concentration statements grounded in official sources opened/returned here:

  1. DCC map, Feb 2026 (official): 4.27 retail licences per 100,000 people statewide; 256/540 jurisdictions allow ≥1 type; 302/540 allow no retail. No county breakdown is stated on the page as opened.
  2. California State Auditor, Report 2024-048, Table 1 (official, Feb 2025): active provisional licences by jurisdiction, 1 Jan 2023 vs 30 June 2024, and transition rates. Largest provisional populations 1 Jan 2023: City of Los Angeles 1,177 → 951 active 30 June 2024 (19% transitioned in period; 938 projected still provisional 1 Jan 2026 at that rate); County of Mendocino 765 → 558 (27%); County of Humboldt 597 → 208 (65%); County of Trinity 296 → 121 (59%); County of Monterey 392 → 50 (87%); City of Oakland 305 → 148 (51%); County of Lake 171 → 8 (95%); City of Long Beach 137 → 20 (85%); City of Sacramento 160 → 24 (85%); City and County of San Francisco 70 → 19 (73%); City of San Diego 55 → 3 (95%); Totals in table: 4,607 provisional 1 Jan 2023 → 2,279 active 30 June 2024 (51% transitioned); 1,615 projected not transitioned by 1 Jan 2026 at the then-current rate. Auditor’s caveat: figures are estimates; “other outcomes” (expiry, surrender, revocation, merger into a single licence) are not readily identified in DCC’s database; DCC may still issue new provisional licences to local equity applicants until 1 Jan 2031 (see §4.7 in Part 3 working notes / Part 4.10 below). Source: State Auditor Table 1, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  3. DCC licence-search data as repackaged by third parties is NOT used here: e.g. Schwass Law’s claim of 20,432 active DCC licences (Mar/Apr 2026) and Higher Origins’ “7,676 total active” counter are third-party compilations from DCC search data, not DCC-published county tables. They are listed here only to show they were seen and excluded as non-official for concentration purposes.

Therefore, for the final dossier: if a county-by-county licence-count table is required, it must be built from a dated DCC licence-search export (Part 5) with the export date, status filter (active only vs all statuses), and the city-vs-unincorporated distinction stated — the DCC map alone does not provide it, and Part 3 did not retrieve an official DCC county-breakdown table.

3.6 Local fees — general note

Local application, permit, inspection, and renewal fees vary by jurisdiction and are set by city/county fee schedules, not by DCC. No comprehensive local fee table was retrieved in Part 3. Examples that were grounded: Oakland: business-tax declaration/payment mechanics (§3.4); permit fees are in OMC fee schedule — Verify current schedule. Long Beach: $1,000 minimum annual cannabis business tax (not a permit fee). San Jose: ~$140,000 regulatory fees per dispensary (secondary, Feb 2024 — Verify). Los Angeles City DCR: $8,486–$11,806 per activity (public-comment summary of 2024 fee study — Verify). Sacramento City: zero-dollar permit fees for CORE participants (Resolution 2018-0323). Do not publish a “typical local fee” range without a jurisdiction and a fee-schedule citation.
