PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

4.1 Framework

Statute: MAUCRSA, Business & Professions Code Division 10 (§26000 et seq.). Licence classifications at §26050; cultivator types at §26061 (as amended). Regulations: California Code of Regulations, Title 4, Division 19 (DCC). Application/licensing in Chapter 1, Article 3 (§§15000 et seq.); fees at §15014; business modifications at §15023; manufacturing at Chapter 8 (§17006 et seq.); retail/distribution/testing/microbusiness/event rules in later chapters. DCC’s unofficial consolidated text is stated to be “regulations in effect as of October 1, 2026” — Westlaw hosts the official CCR. Source: DCC regulations text, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Regulator: Department of Cannabis Control (DCC), formed July 2021 by consolidation (AB 141 / SB 158 / SB 160 trailer bills) of the Bureau of Cannabis Control, CDFA CalCannabis Cultivation Licensing, and CDPH Manufactured Cannabis Safety Branch. DCC licence-types hub (opened): DCC licence types, accessed 1 Nov 2026: “The Department of Cannabis Control (DCC) issues licenses based on the type of cannabis activity that your business will perform. If you will do more than one activity, you may need more than one license.”

Licence validity: §26050 — a licence is valid for 12 months from issuance and may be renewed annually (per §26050 text as reproduced in secondary source — Verify against leginfo). A / M designations: §26050 (reproduced text): “With the exception of testing laboratory licenses, which may be used to test cannabis and cannabis products regardless of whether they are intended for use by individuals who possess a physician’s recommendation, all licenses issued under this division shall bear a clear designation indicating whether the license is for commercial adult-use cannabis activity as distinct from commercial medicinal cannabis activity by prominently affixing an ‘A’ or ‘M,’ respectively. Examples … ‘A-Type 1’ or ‘M-Type 1.’ Except as specifically specified in this division, the requirements for A-licenses and M-licenses shall be the same.” Practical consequence (for Part 1 cross-reference): most licensees hold both an A and an M licence for the same activity/premises. Schedule III / 280E relief, per the federal baseline, applies to state-licensed medical (M) activity only; A activity remains Schedule I / fully 280E-subject, and a dual holder must split medical vs adult-use activity, inventory, and accounting. That split is a tax/accounting requirement, not a separate DCC licence type. June 2026 emergency-rule note (secondary): DCC adopted emergency rules effective 4 June 2026 allowing eligible retailers with combined A+M designations to obtain separate A and M licences through a modification process — Verify against DCC rulemaking page before final publication; do not present as a new licence type.

No state residency requirement to hold a licence (secondary synthesis, consistent with DCC guidance — Verify against DCC application requirements page). General application requirements (all types, per DCC/§15002 framework and secondary synthesis — Verify item-by-item against §15002): 21+ owners; owner disclosures + DOJ live-scan fingerprints; financial-interest-holder disclosures; premises diagram; standard operating procedures; security plan; evidence of local authorisation; CEQA compliance (annual licences); $5,000 surety bond per licensed premises, 4 CCR §15002(c)(22) [AUDIT CORRECTION: citation corrected from §15008 alone; at least $5,000, aggregated bond allowed for multiple licences held by the same business]; workers’ compensation / labour peace agreement where applicable (20+ employees, §26051.5 framework — Verify threshold and form).

4.2 Cultivation licences — by size and type

Basis: canopy = area where mature (flowering) plants are grown. Licence type is determined by (a) production/lighting type and (b) canopy size or plant count. Mixed-light Tier 1 vs Tier 2 turns on amount of artificial light (Tier 1 = lower artificial-light threshold as determined by DCC; exact wattage threshold — Verify against §15000 definitions / DCC cultivation page, as secondary sources state it variously).

Size tierOutdoor (no artificial light)Indoor (exclusively artificial light)Mixed-light (natural + supplemental)
Specialty cottage (Type 1C)Up to 25 mature plants OR up to 2,500 sq ft canopyUp to 500 sq ft canopyTier 1 and Tier 2 — up to 2,500 sq ft canopy
Specialty (Type 1 / 1A / 1B)≤5,000 sq ft OR up to 50 mature plants on noncontiguous plots501–5,000 sq ftTier 1 and Tier 2 — 2,501–5,000 sq ft
Small (Type 2 / 2A / 2B)5,001–10,000 sq ft5,001–10,000 sq ftTier 1 and Tier 2 — 5,001–10,000 sq ft
Medium (Type 3 / 3A / 3B)10,001 sq ft – 1 acre (43,560 sq ft)10,001–22,000 sq ftTier 1 and Tier 2 — 10,001–22,000 sq ft
Large (Type 5 / 5A / 5B)>1 acre>22,000 sq ft>22,000 sq ft
Nursery (Type 4)Cultivation solely as a nursery (immature plants, clones, seeds) — no canopy size tier in the fee schedule; application $520 / annual $4,685 (see §4.9)
Processor (Type 14 in §26050 list; cultivation fee schedule)Site that conducts only trimming, drying, curing, grading, or packaging of cannabis and nonmanufactured cannabis products — application $1,040 / annual $9,370

Statutory codes (§26061 as reproduced): Type 1 = specialty outdoor; 1A = specialty indoor; 1B = specialty mixed-light; 1C = specialty cottage; 2 = small outdoor; 2A = small indoor; 2B = small mixed-light; 3 = outdoor medium (text in one reproduction says “Type 3, or ‘outdoor,’ … 10,001 sq ft to one acre” — the 3A/3B indoor/mixed-light medium codes follow the same pattern); 4 = nursery; 5 = large outdoor; 5A = large indoor; 5B = large mixed-light.

Large licences — are they still / now available? YES, since 1 Jan 2023. Former §26061(c): “No Type 5, Type 5A, or Type 5B cultivation licenses may be issued before January 1, 2023.” From 1 Jan 2023, eligible licensees may convert active/previously active cultivation licences into a Large (5/5A/5B) or Medium licence, and a licensee may hold more than one Medium licence (DCC conversion guidance, via secondary summary — Verify against current DCC cultivation page). Former §26061(d) restriction: from 1 Jan 2023, a Type 5/5A/5B licensee may apply for/hold a Type 6 or 7 licence and a Type 10 licence, but shall not be eligible for a Type 8, 11, or 12 licence. Verify whether that restriction survives in current §26061 — it is stated here as the former statutory text, not as confirmed current law. Large-licence fee mechanics (see §4.9, Verify note): base fee + per-2,000-sq-ft increment above the base canopy. A 2024 industry comment citing §15014.2 states a large outdoor cultivator pays an additional $640 per 2,000 sq ft over one acre (=$0.32/sq ft), vs $0.48/sq ft for a 10,000 sq ft small outdoor licence ($4,820) and $0.32/sq ft for a 1-acre medium outdoor licence ($13,990). That $640 figure was not in the §15014 text as opened (June 2025 version, which lists no Large row in (f)/(g)) — Verify against current §15014.2 before final publication.

What cultivation allows: growing/harvesting; on-premises trimming/drying/curing (except Processor-only sites, which may not cultivate); transfer only to licensed distributors/manufacturers/retailers via track-and-trace; no direct retail sale to consumers on a cultivation licence alone. DCC cultivation info pages (to cite in final dossier — hub opened, sub-pages not opened as full text in Part 4): Hub: DCC licence types; DCC Data Dashboard Glossary (opened via search) confirms the canopy definitions above in DCC’s own glossary language: DCC dashboard glossary, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

4.3 Manufacturing licences — Types 6, 7, N, P, S

Opened 1 Nov 2026, DCC “Manufacturing: License types”:

“You need a manufacturing license to make cannabis products in California. The type … depends on: What activities you do / Whether you do extraction and post processing / What types of chemicals you use (if any). You need a different license if you manufacture in a shared-use facility.”
  • Type 7 — Volatile solvent extraction: use volatile solvents for extraction or post-extraction processing; also may use non-volatile solvents, mechanical methods, infusion, packaging/labelling. Volatile solvents = chemicals producing a flammable gas/vapour, e.g. butane, heptane, hexane, propane.
  • Type 6 — Non-volatile solvent manufacturing or mechanical extraction: use non-volatile solvents (e.g. ethanol, carbon dioxide, cooking oils, butter) or mechanical methods (e.g. rosin presses, dry ice); also infusion + packaging/labelling. May not use volatile solvents.
  • Type N — Infusion: make cannabis products through infusion (mixing cannabis extract or plant material with other ingredients); also package/label. May not conduct extraction (Type N = “manufacturers that produce cannabis products other than extracts or concentrates that are produced through extraction,” 4 CCR §17006(c)).
  • Type P — Packaging and labelling only: “can only package and label cannabis products.” No extraction, no infusion.
  • Type S — Shared-use facility manufacturers: operate in a registered shared-use facility; may extract using butter or food-grade oils, water, glycerin, vegetable oil, animal fat, or mechanical methods; make products through infusion; package/label. “Shared-use facilities are places where multiple Type S manufacturers rotate on a schedule and share space and equipment. A Type 7, 6 or N license can register all or part of their manufacturing premises as a shared-use facility.” A Type S licence cannot be applied for until the shared-use facility itself is registered/approved.

Regulation: 4 CCR §17006 (a)–(e) — text as reproduced via Cornell LII (secondary reproduction — Verify against official CCR). Source opened: DCC manufacturing licence types, accessed 1 Nov 2026. A/M: manufacturing licences bear A or M designations (except as specified). A Type 7/6/N/P/S licence is therefore issued as e.g. A-Type 7 / M-Type 7.

4.4 Distributor licences — Type 11 and Type 13 (transport-only)

  • Type 11 — Distributor: full distributor. Transports cannabis goods between licensees; provides storage; arranges testing (sends samples to a Type 8 lab, receives results); may also conduct transport-only self-distribution in defined circumstances. Fee schedule distinguishes “Distributor (Type 11 or 13), unless only engaging in transport only self-distribution” from “Distributor Transport Only Self-Distribution (Type 13)” — see §4.9 for the two different fee scales.
  • Type 13 — Distributor, transport-only: “can move cannabis and cannabis products between cultivation, manufacturing or distribution premises” (DCC licence-types hub summary via Lake County republication of the DCC page). Cannot store for others or arrange testing as a full distributor. Self-distribution variant: a licensee transporting only its own goods, at the much lower $200/$500/$1,000 fee tiers (see §4.9).
  • Numbering conflict — flag in research notes: the older §26050 list as reproduced in one secondary source lists Type 13 as “Cannabis event organizer” and does not list a transport-only distributor, while current DCC material lists Type 13 as transport-only distributor and treats event organiser as a separate licence (see §4.6). The renumbering / consolidation history must be resolved against the current §26050 text on leginfo before the final dossier prints a type-number table. Do not print both numberings as if they coexist.

4.5 Retail licences — Type 9 and Type 10

  • Type 10 — Storefront retailer: physical location open to the public where cannabis goods are sold to adults 21+ and medicinal patients; may also deliver.
  • Type 9 — Non-storefront retailer (delivery-only): sells only through delivery; no public storefront; premises may be closed to the public (§26070(a)(1) framework: “A retailer shall have a licensed premises which is a physical location from which commercial cannabis activities are conducted. A retailer’s premises may be closed to the public. A retailer may conduct sales exclusively by delivery.”).
  • Both types: sales/delivery hours 6 a.m.–10 p.m. under Title 4, §15403 (cited in League SB 1186 FAQ); delivery vehicles, manifests, and track-and-trace entries required before goods leave the premises (§26090(b)–(d) framework).
  • A/M: both bear A/M designations; a retailer may hold A-Type 10 + M-Type 10 (and, per the June 2026 emergency-rule note in §4.1, may now be able to separate combined designations by modification — Verify).

4.6 Microbusiness — Type 12

  • Type 12 — Microbusiness: a single licence covering at least three of the following activities at one location: (1) Cultivation — up to 10,000 total sq ft (outdoor, mixed-light, indoor, and/or nursery combined); (2) Manufacturing — non-volatile solvents, mechanical extraction, or infusion only (i.e. Type 6 / Type N scope; volatile-solvent Type 7 extraction is excluded); (3) Distribution or distribution transport-only; (4) Retail — storefront or non-storefront.
  • Fee is revenue-based, $5,000–$300,000 (see §4.9) — the broadest range of any type, reflecting the combined activities.
  • Historical definition note: the §26001 definition as reproduced in a 2026 Plumas County document describes microbusiness as cultivation <10,000 sq ft + distributor + Level 1 manufacturer + retailer — an older, narrower formulation. The current DCC “at least three of four” formulation above is the operative one (DCC licence-types hub summary via Lake County republication). Verify the current §26001 definition on leginfo.
  • Large/medium cultivator exclusion (secondary): entities with medium/large cultivator licences are not eligible for a microbusiness licence in the older formulation — Verify current eligibility.

4.7 Testing laboratory — Type 8; Event organiser; Other types

Type 8 — Testing laboratory: Tests cannabis goods prior to sale at retail (potency, pesticides, microbials, heavy metals, etc. — testing scope is Part 11, not Part 4). Independence: a Type 8 licensee may not hold any other cannabis licence type (secondary synthesis, consistent with MAUCRSA independence rule — Verify against §26050 / §26051). A/M exception: testing licences do not bear A/M designations — they may test goods regardless of whether intended for medicinal or adult-use patients (§26050, see §4.1). Fees revenue-based: $3,000 (≤$160,000) to $112,000 (>$4.4m) — see §4.9. Must be ISO-accredited (accreditation requirement is in the testing chapter — Verify current §15700 et seq. before final publication).

Cannabis event organiser licence (+ Temporary cannabis event licence): Event organiser (annual): licence to hold events where cannabis will be sold; fee by planned operations: 0–5 events/year $3,000; 6–10 $5,000; 11–20 $9,000; >20 $20,000 (4 CCR §15014(d)). Application fee $1,000 (§15014(a)). Temporary cannabis event licence: per-event licence, application fee $1,000 (§15014(a); §15601). State temporary event licence only in local jurisdictions that authorise such events (§26200(e)(1)(D)); organiser must provide DCC a list of participating licensees 60 days before the event, updated to a final list. Type-number conflict: see §4.4 — do not assign a Type number to event organiser in the final dossier until §26050 is checked.

Other / legacy types in the §26050 list — flag, do not publish as current DCC types without verification: Type 14 — Processor: in the §26050 list and in the cultivation fee schedule (see §4.2). Current DCC licence-types hub treats “processor” as a cultivation activity, not a standalone hub category. Type 15 — Combined activities: in the §26050 list as reproduced. No current DCC hub category for Type 15 was found in Part 4. Verify whether Type 15 was ever implemented or was superseded by microbusiness (Type 12). Type 3A (indoor medium): the §26061 reproduction lists Type 3 as outdoor medium and implies 3A/3B; the DCC fee schedule lists “Medium Indoor” and “Medium Mixed-Light Tier 1/2” without type numbers. The fee schedule, not the type number, is the reliable citation for fees.

4.8 Requirements, ownership, transfer, renewal

Ownership / financial interest: “Owner” is defined in 4 CCR §15003 (threshold: 20%+ ownership / control, plus officers/directors/managers as specified — Verify the current §15003 percentage and control tests; do not quote a percentage from secondary summaries alone). Financial interest holders (below owner threshold) must be disclosed under §15002(c)(15); owners under §15002(c)(16).

Transfer — the rule that matters most, and is most often misstated: 4 CCR §15023(c), opened via Justia reproduction (current through Register 2025, No. 26):

“Licenses are not transferrable or assignable to another person or owner.”

On sale/transfer of the business/operations: New owners submit §15002(c)(16) information within 14 calendar days of the effective date of the ownership change. If at least one existing owner remains and will remain as owner under the new structure, the business may continue to operate under the active licence while DCC reviews the new owners’ qualifications. If all owners transfer out, the business shall not operate under the new ownership structure until a new licence application has been submitted to and approved by DCC and all application/licence fees paid. The former owner’s inventory transfers to the new owner’s track-and-trace account upon issuance of the new licence. No change in ownership occurs when owners leave by transferring to other existing owners — the departing owners provide a signed statement confirming transfer within 14 calendar days. Financial-interest-holder changes (non-owner): submit §15002(c)(15) information within 14 calendar days (§15023(d)). Other changes notified within 14 calendar days (§15023(e)): contact information, legal/business name, trade name/DBA/FBN, bond. Add A or M designation (excluding cultivators): §15023(f) modification. Microbusiness add/remove activity: §15023(g). Physical modification of premises (excluding cultivators): §15027, $500 fee for testing/distributor/retailer/microbusiness/manufacturing (see §4.9). Death/incapacity/receivership/assignment: §15024(a) — DCC Form LIC-027 (Notifications and Requests to Modify a License) is the form; email licensechange@cannabis.ca.gov (public programme address on the form). Sources: Justia §15023, accessed 1 Nov 2026; DCC form via DCC staging domain — Verify the current form on cannabis.ca.gov before citing the URL in print.

Ownership caps: No statewide ownership cap / canopy cap per person was confirmed from an opened official source in Part 4. The former 4-acre aggregate canopy limit for a person holding multiple cultivation licences (pre-2023 guidance) and the contiguous-premises 1-acre / 22,000-sq-ft limits in the provisional-licence rules (see §4.10) are not the same as a current ownership cap. Flag as Verify: check current §26050 / §15000 et seq. for any person-level cap, and check local caps (which are common — e.g. San Diego 36 dispensary permits, Santa Ana retail cap 30 in the stale exhibit, Sacramento storefront cap 30+10) separately. Do not state “no cap” as a finding.

Renewal: Licence valid 12 months; renewal application + renewal fee under §15020 before expiration. Annual fee must be paid before DCC issues/renews the licence (§15014(b)). For revenue-based types, the fee tier is based on estimated gross revenue for the 12-month licence period (§15014(c)); for manufacturers, on gross sales/revenue in the 12 months preceding application (existing) or expected first 12 months (new applicant), with fair-market-value rules for non-arm’s-length transfers (§15014(e)). All fees nonrefundable (§15014(h)).

Insurance / bonding: State surety bond: $5,000 per licensed premises, payable to the State of California, for every applicant/holder (Bus. & Prof. Code §26051.5(a)(10); 4 CCR §15008). Purpose as stated in secondary summaries of the statute: to cover costs the state incurs destroying cannabis/cannabis products following a violation. Verify the current §15008 text and whether the bond amount has been amended. Local bonds are additional and vary (e.g. Coalinga $25,000 cited in a surety-bond secondary source — do not generalise). Insurance minimums (general liability, product liability, workers’ compensation): no statewide minimum dollar figure was confirmed from an opened official source in Part 4. DCC application materials require evidence of insurance/workers’ comp where applicable, and local jurisdictions commonly impose their own minima. Flag as Verify / Unavailable for a statewide table — do not invent a minimum.

4.9 Fees — Title 4, §15014 (opened, current through Register 2025, No. 26, 27 June 2025)

Sources opened 1 Nov 2026: Justia §15014; Cornell §15014.

Application fees (§15014(a)): Testing Laboratory, Distributor, Retailer, Microbusiness annual licences: $1,000 each; Cannabis Event Organizer: $1,000; Temporary Cannabis Event (per event): $1,000; Physical modification of premises (Testing, Distributor, Retailer, Microbusiness, Manufacturing): $500; Type 6, 7, N, or P manufacturing: $1,000; Type S manufacturing: $500; Cultivation: see cultivation application table below.

Annual fees — Testing Laboratory (Type 8), by gross revenue (max per licence): ≤$160,000: $3,000 | ≤$320,000: $6,000 | ≤$480,000: $8,000 | ≤$800,000: $13,000 | ≤$1.2m: $20,000 | ≤$2.0m: $32,000 | ≤$2.8m: $48,000 | ≤$4.4m: $72,000 | >$4.4m: $112,000

The cultivation fee structure “has not changed since initial regulations were set in 2017” per a Dec 2024 industry comment to the Cannabis Advisory Committee — that is an advocacy claim, not a DCC statement; the §15014 text as opened is the authority, and any post-June-2025 fee amendment must be checked against the current CCR.

4.10 End of provisional licences — dates, mechanics, what happened

Timeline (AB 141 / SB 160, Cannabis Trailer Bill, July 2021; 4 CCR §§15001.1, 15001.2, 15001(f) as summarised in contemporaneous law-firm analysis — Verify current regulation text, as §§15001 et seq. have been renumbered/consolidated since):

  • Temporary licences: from 1 Jan 2018, issued only to applicants with a local permit/licence/authorisation; replaced by provisional licences near end of 2018.
  • Provisional licences: originally 1-year lifespan; reauthorised 2019 for 2 years (issue/renew until 31 Dec 2021).
  • Trailer Bill deadlines: 31 Mar 2022: last day to submit provisional applications for most types (exceptions below). 30 June 2022: DCC ceases issuing new provisional licences for most types. Large-canopy cultivation (contiguous premises >1 acre outdoor or >22,000 sq ft mixed-light/indoor, including local equity applicants): apply by 31 Dec 2021, issue by 30 June 2022, renew only to 1 Jan 2023, in effect only to 1 Jan 2024. Smaller cultivation (≤1 acre outdoor / ≤22,000 sq ft mixed-light/indoor, non-equity): apply by 30 June 2022, issue by 30 Sep 2022, renew to 1 Jan 2025, in effect to 1 Jan 2026. Local equity applicants (other than large-canopy above): apply by 31 Mar 2023, issue by 30 June 2023, renew to 1 Jan 2025, in effect to 1 Jan 2026. All other licence types: apply by 31 Mar 2022, issue by 30 June 2022, renew to 1 Jan 2025, in effect to 1 Jan 2026.
  • CEQA trigger: from 1 Apr 2022, provisional renewal and annual licensure required CEQA compliance before the state licence could be awarded/renewed, with progressively stricter showings of CEQA progress. Local jurisdiction is lead agency if its process is discretionary (e.g. CUP); DCC is lead agency if the local process is ministerial/exempt.
  • End state: By 1 Jan 2026, all cannabis licensees must operate under a state annual licence. No provisional licence may be in effect after 1 Jan 2026 (general rule). A secondary summary (CannBus Q3 2026) states: “Since January 1, 2026, all cannabis licenses issued by the DCC are annual — the old provisional-license system has been phased out statewide.”
  • Equity exception that survives the general sunset — Verify: the State Auditor (Feb 2025) notes “state law allows [DCC] to issue new provisional licenses for local equity applicants until January 1, 2031.” That sentence, if current, means the “all annual by 2026” statement needs an equity-applicant footnote in the final dossier. Do not publish either statement without reconciling it against current §26050.2 / §15001 text.
  • What happened to provisionals that did not convert: per the Auditor’s methodology, outcomes include expiry, surrender, revocation, or merger into a single (e.g. Large/Medium) licence — DCC’s database “does not readily identify these outcomes or statuses,” so a clean “converted vs closed” count by jurisdiction is Unavailable from that report. The Auditor’s projection (1,615 licences not transitioned by 1 Jan 2026 at the Jan 2023–June 2024 rate) is a projection made in Feb 2025, not an actual 2026 count.

Sources: JD Supra table of provisional phase-out dates (secondary, summarising §§15001.1/15001.2); State Auditor Table 1, accessed 1 Nov 2026; City of Santa Rosa summary: srcity.org — State Cannabis License, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

4.11 Equity fee waivers / fee relief programme (state)

See §3.1.4 for the full programme. For Part 4 purposes, the operative points are: Waivers if funding available + deferrals otherwise; eligibility = negatively affected by cannabis criminalisation; verified local-equity participants attest; others document. 12-month eligibility, annual reapplication. Renewal gross-revenue test: under $5 million (tax documentation required). Linked tax benefits: CDTFA 20% excise-tax retention (ended 31 Dec 2025 per DCC page) + FTB $10,000 credit (through 31 Dec 2027). Local equity grants are separate (GO-Biz, §3.4) — DCC fee relief does not itself grant local permit fee waivers, though local programmes commonly do (e.g. Sacramento CORE zero-dollar permit fees; Oakland fee exemptions for equity applicants under OMC 5.80/5.81). Source opened: DCC equity fee relief, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

4.12 CEQA and environmental requirements for cultivation

CEQA (California Environmental Quality Act, Pub. Resources Code §§21000 et seq.): Annual DCC licences require CEQA compliance. Lead agency: local jurisdiction if its permitting is discretionary; DCC if local process is ministerial / CEQA-exempt (see §4.10). CEQA review evaluates water, air quality, traffic, wildlife, and other impacts. Local jurisdictions must inform DCC of potential environmental impacts; DCC may be lead or responsible agency depending on the local process. Verify the current DCC “CEQA Review for Cannabis Businesses” page for the current lead-agency checklist and for any CEQA streamlining / exemptions for small cultivators.

California Department of Fish and Wildlife (CDFW) — Lake and Streambed Alteration (LSA): Fish & Game Code §§1600 et seq.; §1602(a) requires an entity to notify CDFW before engaging in activities that would substantially change the bed/channel/bank of a stream, or substantially divert/obstruct the natural flow of a stream. If CDFW determines an agreement is needed, the project proceeds under a Lake and Streambed Alteration Agreement with mitigation measures (e.g. the Trinity County example: Agreement EPIMS-TRI-23346-R1I, Dobbins Gulch, Hayfork, for a seep well + Class III stream crossing improvement). Mendocino County’s pilot policy with CDFW (County Code §10A.17.100(A)(2), Sensitive Species Performance Standards) requires, for surface-water-source projects / stream crossings / §1602 activities: a final LSA Agreement, verification that no agreement is needed, an “operation of law” letter, or at minimum a submitted LSA Notification — with a final agreement required within the 1-year cultivation permit term for renewal. CDFW is also trustee agency for fish/wildlife (Fish & Game Code §§711.7, 1802) and responsible agency under CEQA (Pub. Resources Code §21069), and may require CESA authorisation if take of a protected species may occur.

State Water Resources Control Board — Cannabis Cultivation Policy + General Order + SIUR: Cannabis Cultivation Policy (Principles and Guidelines for Cannabis Cultivation): adopted by the State Water Board 5 Feb 2019, approved by OAL 16 Apr 2019. Purpose: ensure diversion/discharge associated with cultivation does not negatively impact water quality, aquatic/riparian habitat, wetlands, springs. Implemented through the General Order + SIUR + DCC licensing. Cannabis Cultivation General Order: Order No. WQ 2019-0001-DWQ, revised to WQ 2023-0102-DWQ. A simplified Waste Discharge Requirement (WDR). All commercial cannabis cultivators must obtain coverage under the General Order (Water Board: “Enrollment … is a required step to obtaining a [DCC] license for cannabis cultivation”). Classifies outdoor operations into Tiers by disturbed area (Tier 1: >2,000 sq ft and <1 acre) and Risk levels by slope/setbacks: Class I watercourse (perennial) 150 ft setback; Class II (intermittent/wetland) 100 ft; Class III (ephemeral) 50 ft. Low Risk = no disturbed area on slope >30% + full setback compliance. Water rights — Cannabis Small Irrigation Use Registration (SIUR): expedited appropriative right for diversion + storage of up to 6.6 acre-feet/year, diversion season 1 Nov–31 Mar, with dry-season forbearance (cease diverting surface water in the dry season; storage required for dry-season irrigation). Riparian rights cannot be used for cannabis dry-season irrigation (no storage under riparian claim). Other valid rights: Licence, Permit, valid pre-1914 appropriative claim. SDURs (Small Domestic Use Registrations) may not be used for a commercial cultivation licence. Fully Appropriated Stream (FAS) year-round sources are not eligible for SIUR. Portal: https://public2.waterboards.ca.gov/CGO (as cited in a 2025 county staff report — Verify the current portal URL on waterboards.ca.gov before final publication). Sources: Water Boards cannabis programme; Water Boards cannabis FAQs; Cannabis Cultivation Policy PDF, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Other environmental requirements (flagged, not detailed — Part 11 territory, listed here only because Part 4 asks for CEQA/environmental requirements): Pesticide use: Department of Pesticide Regulation + county agricultural commissioner rules; cannabis-specific pesticide guidance — Verify current DPR list. Energy: indoor cultivation electricity-use reporting to DCC on renewal (secondary summary: “All cultivation and microbusiness licensees authorized to engage in cannabis cultivation are required to report total electricity use for each power source to the DCC upon license renewal”) — Verify against current §15000 et seq. Waste, odour, lighting, water-quality BMPs: in the General Order Attachment A + local ordinances (e.g. Santa Barbara County multi-technology carbon filtration / odour-control equipment compliance, Jan 2026 Board update).

4.13 Whether applications are open

State level: DCC does not operate a statewide application window or cap that closes applications for the main licence types; applications are accepted on a rolling basis through DCC’s two licensing systems (cultivation/manufacturing system vs retail/distribution/testing system — see DCC equity page: “The Department of Cannabis Control has two licensing systems. Use the one that corresponds to the license type you’re applying for.”). Local level is the binding constraint: a state application cannot succeed without local authorisation, and many cities/counties operate capped, competitive, lottery, or phased windows (e.g. Los Angeles phased Social Equity rounds; Sacramento storefront lottery for the +10 CORE permits; Santa Ana RSP caps in the stale exhibit; San Diego 36-permit cap). A jurisdiction that is “open” on the DCC map may still have no available local permits. Therefore the correct Part 4 answer is: “State applications: open (rolling), subject to local authorisation. Local applications: jurisdiction-specific — check the city/county cannabis programme page for the current window/cap.” Any statement that “applications are open in [city]” without a dated local source is Verify.

Official info-page links to cite (hub + sub-pages; hub opened, sub-pages to be re-opened for final citation): Licence types hub: DCC licence types; Manufacturing types (opened): DCC manufacturing types; Equity fee relief (opened): DCC equity fee relief; Where businesses are allowed (opened): DCC where allowed; DCC regulations text (Oct 2026): DCC regulations text; Cultivation / distribution / retail / microbusiness / testing / event sub-pages under DCC licence types hub — Verify each sub-page URL on the live hub before final publication; the hub’s sub-page slugs were not all opened in Part 4.

4.14 Estimated startup costs — NON-OFFICIAL estimates, marked as such

No official DCC or state source publishes a startup-cost estimate. The following are industry/secondary estimates only and must be labelled as such in the final dossier:

  • Dispensary (storefront, Type 10): $250,000–$2,000,000 total startup (IndicaOnline 2026 guide; Cova/MJBizDaily-derived range; Meadow Oct 2026 guide). Breakdown in those guides: state application $1,000 + annual licence $2,500–$96,000 (official fee schedule — see §4.9); local permit $1,000–$25,000+ (varies by city, non-official range); real estate + build-out $100,000–$1m+; build-out renovation $350,000–$3,500,000 / new construction $900,000–$3,500,000 (CannDelta Feb 2025, via Meadow); staffing, inventory, security, compliance systems additional. The widely circulated “$700,000 for an average 1,800 sq ft store” figure is, per Meadow Oct 2026, “a recycled industry estimate, not primary research.”
  • Lower dispensary range in one older guide: $80,000–$250,000 upfront + $30,000–$70,000/month operating (Cova, updated Oct 2026) — conflicts with the $250k–$2m range above; the conflict reflects different inclusions (rent/build-out/inventory/working capital) and must be noted, not averaged.
  • General cannabis startup (all types): “Probably $2 million operating capital at the outset” (attorney quote, Super Lawyers guide) + “a couple of hundred thousand dollars out the gate to even have an opportunity to apply” for a competitive local application (legal/professional costs). Timeline: “not unheard of to take a year” from scratch; fastest seen 6–7 months (same guide, attorney quotes — anecdotal, not data).
  • Cultivation: no credible non-official total was retrieved that separates land, greenhouse/indoor build-out, water/General Order compliance, CEQA, and working capital. Do not publish a cultivation startup total without a sourced build-out model. The official cultivation cost floor that can be stated is: application fee ($135–$8,655) + annual fee ($1,205–$77,905, plus Large increment — Verify) + $5,000 bond + local permit fees/taxes + CEQA/water-permit costs (jurisdiction-specific).

Sources for the non-official ranges (label as non-official in the final dossier): IndicaOnline, how to open a dispensary in California; Meadow, cost to open a dispensary; Cova, true cost of opening a dispensary; Super Lawyers, what you need for a cannabis business in California, all accessed 1 Nov 2026.

  1. Type 13 numbering conflict: event organiser (old §26050 list) vs transport-only distributor (current DCC). Resolve on leginfo current §26050.
  2. Type 14 Processor / Type 15 Combined activities: in §26050 list, no current DCC hub category found. Verify implementation status.
  3. Large-licence fee formula (§15014.2, $640/2,000 sq ft): not in §15014 as opened. Verify.
  4. Type 5 holder restriction (may hold 6/7/10, may not hold 8/11/12): former §26061(d) text. Verify current law.
  5. Mixed-light Tier 1/2 wattage threshold: not confirmed from an opened official page. Verify §15000 definitions.
  6. Statewide ownership/canopy cap per person: not confirmed. Verify; do not state “no cap.”
  7. Insurance minimums (statewide): not confirmed. Verify / mark Unavailable for a statewide table.
  8. Provisional sunset vs equity-applicant provisional issuance to 1 Jan 2031: reconcile current §26050.2 / §15001 text before stating either as absolute.
  9. June 2026 emergency rule on separate A/M retailer licences: secondary only. Verify DCC rulemaking.
  10. $5,000 bond — current §15008 text and whether the amount/form has changed: Verify.
  11. DCC sub-page URLs for cultivation/distribution/retail/microbusiness/testing/event: re-open from the live hub; only manufacturing + hub + equity pages were opened as full text in Part 4.
  12. Startup costs: no official estimate exists; all ranges above are non-official and conflicting — label as such, do not average.
  13. Local fees: no comprehensive table retrieved; jurisdiction fee schedules are the follow-up source.

PART 5 — LICENCE DATA SOURCES

Do NOT try to list every licence holder here. This part inventories sources. No licence-holder list is reproduced. Date accessed for all sources below: 1 November 2026, as instructed. Method note: Official pages were opened where the tool returned text. Three browser.open attempts — the CDFA Industrial Hemp Registration Summary PDF, the CDFA Hemp Program Updates IHAB 13 May 2026 PDF, and the DCC compliance-with-state-law page — failed with a transient-grant error. Per instruction those requests were not repeated, including through another tool or endpoint. Facts from those three sources are therefore reported below from search-result text only and flagged as such, not as directly opened pages. A failed fetch does not prove the source is unavailable in a live browser; re-check those three URLs in the live browser if a verbatim total is needed.

5.0 Headline rule that governs every DCC source

California has one state commercial-cannabis regulator for licensing — the Department of Cannabis Control (DCC) — but the public record is split across: (1) a daily-updated licence search (record-level, interactive, no bulk download found); (2) Looker dashboards (aggregated, interactive, no bulk download found); (3) annual legislative / supplemental reports (PDF downloads — the only DCC licence-count downloads actually located); (4) compliance-action and final-decision tables (interactive web tables, no bulk download found); (5) a recalls portal (interactive search, no bulk download found). There is no official, single, downloadable CSV/XLSX of all current DCC licensees located in this research. Any vendor file claiming to be “the DCC list” is a scrape of the search tool at a point in time and must be dated as such. This is the single most important limitation for Run 1, Part 5.

5.1 DCC — Cannabis Unified License Search / Search for a Licensed Business

  • Holder type: All DCC commercial cannabis licensees — cultivation, manufacturing, distribution, distributor-transport-only, retailer (storefront), non-storefront retailer (delivery), microbusiness, testing laboratory, event organiser, and any other DCC licence type. Both A-type (adult-use) and M-type (medical) designations are in the same system.
  • Landing / explainer page (opened): DCC — Search for a licensed business
  • Search tool itself (historical unified address, reported in 2020 launch coverage and still cited in current secondary documentation): search.cannabis.ca.gov — Verify in live browser: whether search.cannabis.ca.gov still resolves directly on 1 November 2026 or now redirects to the cannabis.ca.gov search page. The DCC explainer page above is the current authoritative entry point and was opened successfully.
  • Format: Interactive web search + map/geolocation. No CSV/XLSX/PDF bulk download button was shown on the opened explainer page.
  • How to access: No login stated on the explainer page. Use it to: verify if a business is licensed; find a retailer near you; search for licensed businesses; file a complaint about a business.
  • Update frequency as stated: “The License Search Tool is updated daily and contains information about all of the businesses licensed by the Department of Cannabis Control.” The 2020 launch coverage said the unified tool would be updated every 24 hours, with the then-separate licensing-authority tools holding the most recent data. The current DCC wording — “updated daily” — supersedes that for present use.
  • Latest data date: Daily — i.e. the day of the search. No fixed “as of” date is published on the explainer page.
  • Fields available (from the explainer page and from recall/compliance cross-references): Business/legal name, licence number (format e.g. C10-0000599-LIC for retailer, C11-…-LIC for distributor), licence type, licence status, licensed premises location, and complaint route. The search tool itself, not the explainer page, is where address, expiry and other record fields appear — Verify field-by-field in the live browser, because the text fetch of the explainer page does not render the search results table.
  • Statuses defined on the opened page — copy exactly: Active — business has an active licence; may do the activities allowed by its licence type at the location listed. About to Expire — annual/provisional licence within 60 calendar days prior to expiration; licence remains Active. Expired – Pending Renewal — within 30 days after expiration; can still be renewed with a late fee of 50% of the licence fee. Cannot do commercial cannabis activity if status is: Expired (non-renewal); Not in Operation (approved for Disaster Relief, not currently in operation); Revoked (terminated due to licensing action); Surrendered (voluntarily relinquished/abandoned); Suspended (undergoing disciplinary action, definitive or indefinite).
  • Limitations: Record-level only; no bulk export found. A search result is a point-in-time status, not a history. Expired/surrendered/revoked records remain searchable but must not be counted as active. One business can hold several licences; licence counts ≠ business counts ≠ premises counts. The DCC dashboards and legislative reports count licences, not companies. Equity status is shown in the underlying record as an “[Equity Retailer]” label in some third-party extractions of DCC data (see §5.11), but the DCC explainer page itself does not describe an equity filter — Verify whether the live search has an equity filter. Legacy Accela portals (former BCC / CalCannabis / CDPH MCSB searches, e.g. online.bcc.ca.gov, aca6.accela.com/calcannabis) appear in old guides and are superseded — do not cite them as current without live verification.

5.2 DCC — Data Dashboards (aggregated licence, harvest, sales and enforcement data)

  • Holder / subject type: Aggregated licence counts, harvest batches, sales and price-per-unit, monthly sales summaries, and law-enforcement activity. Not a holder list.
  • Landing page (opened): DCC data dashboard
  • Dashboards named on the opened page: (1) Cannabis License Summary Report; (2) Harvest Report; (3) Sales & Price Per Unit Report; (4) Monthly Sales Summary Report; (5) Law Enforcement Division Activity Report; (6) Data Dashboard Glossary & Notes (supporting page, not a data report).
  • Glossary page (from search results): DCC dashboard glossary
  • Format: Interactive Google Looker dashboards. No CSV/XLSX download was shown in the text fetch. Looker normally offers per-visualisation download/export, but that was not verified in this text-only pass — Verify in live browser whether a download/export button exists on each Looker report. Do not assert “no export” as a final fact; assert “no bulk download link was located in the text fetch.”
  • How to access: Free, no login stated. Full-screen button in the bottom-right corner of each report (per glossary).
  • Update frequency as stated (glossary change log, from search results): Transitioned 10 March 2026 to Google Looker; automated nightly updates as data are reported to DCC. Sales and Harvest Reports display only completed months; date range displayed in red. Licence Report updated daily; date of last update displayed in red at the bottom. Older wording (pre-Looker) said the Licence Summary was updated weekly — the March 2026 Looker note supersedes it.
  • Latest data date: Verify in live browser — the dashboards are the authoritative source for a 1 November 2026 licence total, but the Looker frames did not render as readable text in this pass, so no dashboard total is quoted here as “the DCC dashboard figure.” This is deliberate: quoting a third-party scrape as a dashboard figure would be false sourcing.
  • Fields / filters described (glossary + launch coverage): Licence Summary: total of each licence type; annual vs provisional totals; licence status; expiration information. Harvest: indoor / outdoor / nursery / mixed-light / microbusiness cultivator counts; total cultivators by county; harvest batches (plants, packages, packaged weight, moisture-loss weight, waste weight). Sales & Price Per Unit: price per unit by item group (edibles, extracts, flower, plant, pre-roll, tincture, vape, topical, etc.); yearly / monthly / quarterly sales. Monthly Sales Summary: sales by customer type (adult-use vs medicinal) and by product. Law Enforcement Division Activity Report: added after the original four dashboards; contents Verify in live browser.
  • Limitations: Source data are licensee-entered California Cannabis Track-and-Trace (Metrc) data; DCC states it makes no warranties/guarantees regarding accuracy of source data (glossary). 2020, 2021, 2022 and H1 2023 harvest/sales data were refreshed 7 May 2024 for licensee corrections; outlier harvest records were removed (May 2021: 2 records; Jan 2023: 4; Oct 2023: 4) for wet/package/moisture/waste-weight errors. Small-screen rendering may obscure/distort dashboard elements (glossary warning). Sales in dashboards (Metrc) ≠ CDTFA taxable sales ≠ CDTFA cannabis-only sales — see conflict note in Part 6, §6.1.

5.3 DCC — Annual Legislative Report and Supplemental Budget Report (the downloadable licence-count source)

These are the only DCC licence files with exact direct download links and filenames located in this research.

5.3a Legislative Report FY 2024–25

  • Holder type: DCC licences by type, with issued / renewed / denied / suspended / revoked counts.
  • Direct download link (from search results, verbatim): Annual-Legislative-Report-FY24-25.pdf
  • File name: Annual-Legislative-Report-FY24-25.pdf
  • Format: PDF. Published: 1 March 2026. Covers prior fiscal year 1 July 2024 – 30 June 2025 (note: one search snippet mis-describes the period as 1 July 2023 – 30 June 2024 in its body text; the report title and publication date establish FY 2024–25 — flag this internal inconsistency for the parent to check on the PDF cover).
  • How to access: Direct PDF download, no login. Update frequency: Annual, required by Business and Professions Code §26190 (report to the Legislature each 1 March for the prior fiscal year). Latest data date: FY 2024–25 (to 30 June 2025).
  • Headline figures (from search-result text of the PDF): Issued: 849 licences; Renewed: 7,225; Denied: 97 applications; Suspended: 192; Revoked: 79. Total issued + renewed = 8,074 (excludes temporary events, per secondary summary of this report). DCC budget FY 2024–25: $157,440,000 allocated (report text); secondary summary gives $157.4m allocated / $146.6m spent — Verify spent figure against the PDF. By-type table exists in the PDF (e.g. Distributor row shown in snippet: 78 issued, 795 renewed, 17 denied applications, 0 suspended, 22 revoked, 15 denied renewals — column order Verify against the PDF table header before reuse).
  • Limitations: Flow figures (issued/renewed in a year), not a stock count of active licences on a given date. Do not present 8,074 as “active licences.”

5.3b Supplemental Budget Report FY 2024–25 (provisional licences)

  • Direct download link (from search results, verbatim): Annual-Supplemental-Report-FY24-25.pdf
  • File name: Annual-Supplemental-Report-FY24-25.pdf
  • Format: PDF. Published 1 March 2026. Required annually through 2025 under the 2021–22 Budget Act.
  • Headline figures (from search-result text): 2,158 provisional licences transitioned to annual licences in FY 2024–25. 107 provisional licences suspended or revoked under Bus. & Prof. Code §26050.2(i). County/city breakdown tables for provisional licences, transitions, revocations/suspensions are in the report (tables listed pp. 25–26 in the snippet).
  • Use: This is the authoritative source for “end of provisional licences” counts. Provisional licences could not be renewed after 1 January 2025 and are no longer effective after 1 January 2026 (with the locally-verified equity retailer exception noted in DCC materials — Verify exception wording in Part 4).

5.3c Prior-year report (for time series)

  • Direct download link (from search results, verbatim): DCC-Annual-Report_Fiscal-Year-2023_24.pdf
  • File name: DCC-Annual-Report_Fiscal-Year-2023_24.pdf
  • Published: 3 March 2025; covers 1 July 2023 – 30 June 2024.
  • Headline figures: Issued 712; Renewed 8,939; Denied 5 applications; Suspended 125; Revoked 51. Budget allocated $166,387,000.
  • Conflict / caution: The FY 2023–24 “Denied: 5” vs FY 2024–25 “Denied: 97” jump is as printed; do not smooth it. The FY 2024–25 report appears to count denied applications and denied renewals in separate columns — Verify column definitions before comparing years.

5.4 DCC — Compliance Action Records (denials, citations, disciplinary actions)

  • Holder type: Applicants and licensees subject to DCC compliance/licensing action.
  • Landing page (from search results): DCC compliance with state law — Note: Direct open of this page failed in this pass (transient error, not repeated). The description below is from search-result text of that page and of the appeals FAQ — flag as search-snippet only.
  • Sub-pages (from search results): Compliance Action Records table: described as at cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/compliance-action-records (exact full URL Verify — it was cited inside a third-party report, not returned as a full search URL). Final Decisions index: cited as cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/final-decisions (same caveat). Appeals FAQ (from search results, full URL returned): DCC appeals FAQ
  • Format: Interactive web tables (licence denials, citations, fines, suspensions, revocations). No bulk download found. How to access: Free, no login stated. Update frequency: Not stated in snippets — Verify. Latest data date: Verify in live browser — the tables are rolling.
  • Fields described: Licence denial / citation / fine / suspension / revocation; whether an APA Order of Decision has been issued (compliance-action table = matters without an APA order; final-decisions table = matters with an APA-adopted decision). Appeal route: Cannabis Control Appeals Panel (CCAP), notice within 30 days after DCC’s reconsideration period; filing triggers automatic stay; provisional revocations/suspensions cannot be appealed to CCAP.
  • Fines stated (search snippet of compliance page): Up to $5,000 per violation for a licensed person; up to $30,000 per violation per day for an unlicensed person (see also 4 CCR §17802(c)(3) as cited in a secondary analysis — Verify regulation text in Part 1/11, do not rely on the secondary cite alone).
  • Limitations: Citations resolved by administrative payment, matters still in process, and recall corrective-action plans are not represented on the final-decisions page (third-party analysis of DCC pages). Absence from final decisions ≠ absence of resolution. No aggregate “total actions” count was located — do not invent one. FY 2024–25 flow counts (192 suspended, 79 revoked) in §5.3a are the only official aggregate disciplinary counts located.

5.5 DCC — Product Recalls Portal + Archive

  • Holder type: Recalled cannabis goods (product/brand level, linked to legal business name + licence number), not licence holders as such — but the portal is the licence-linked product-safety record and is therefore inventoried here.
  • Current portal (from search results): recalls.cannabis.ca.gov — Search by recall name, recall type, product type or keyword; consumer guidance says search by brand, recall reason, licence number or product type (flower, edible, vape cartridge, etc.), then compare batch number and UID on the label.
  • Consumer guidance (from search results): DCC recalls — consumers — last reviewed 20 October 2026.
  • Licensee guidance (from search results): DCC recalls — licensees — last reviewed 20 October 2026. Licensees must notify DCC within 24 hours of initiating a recall (recalls@cannabis.ca.gov — official public mailbox).
  • Archive, pre-2024 recalls (opened): DCC recalls archive — Format: HTML table on the page. Fields: recall date, product description, product type, recall reason, legal business name, recall overview. Examples on the opened archive page (pre-2024 only): 20 Nov 2023 Lil Leftys Premium Infused – 5 Mini Blunts, Maui Waui (Left Coast, LLC); 14 July 2023 Cannalean brand products (Last Minute Venture, LLC); 19 May 2023 Cann-branded beverages (RCW Manufacturing); 23 Dec 2022 Mary Jones Soda (Lakewood Libations, Inc.); 13 Oct 2022 530 Grower – GMO flower (Metta Distribution); 28 Sept 2022 Haute Sauce (Refined Xtractions); 26 Jan 2022 Claybourne Co. Head Banger — mandatory recall (Ci Distribution, LLC).
  • Format: Interactive search portal for 2024–present; HTML table for pre-2024 archive. No bulk CSV download found. Update frequency: As recalls are published; consumer advisories also by email and social media (Facebook, X, Threads, Instagram). Latest data date: Rolling to October 2026 in secondary recall notices (e.g. 12–19 Oct 2026 licensee newsletters listing Almora Tropical Berry Ginger Lemonade beverage, MOCA Caramel Sauce edible, Mwah vaporizers, St Ides / Not Your Father’s beverages). Verify latest portal entry in live browser; do not quote a “total recalls” number — none was located officially.
  • Recall types: Voluntary and mandatory (DCC-mandated). Reasons include microbial contamination (Aspergillus), pesticide Category I contamination (e.g. paclobutrazol), inaccurate labelling / cannabinoid inflation, packaging attractive to children, packaging failure, incorrect government warning, prohibited product.
  • Limitations: A recall record names the legal business and licence number but does not by itself prove a licence was suspended/revoked. Third-party analysis (CaNORML/Phenominal, June–Oct 2026, SECONDARY) estimated ~140 of ~7,630 active licences named in a recall since start of 2024 (<2%) — do not present as official; use only to show why no official total should be invented.
  • Local mirror (not a separate official source): LA County Department of Consumer and Business Affairs recalls page — LA County DCBA recalls — links to the DCC portal; it does not publish its own recall list.

5.6 DCC / Governor — Unified Cannabis Enforcement Taskforce (UCETF) seizure and enforcement reporting

  • Holder type: Unlicensed / illicit operators and sites (not licensees). Included because the task requires UCETF seizures as a licence-data-adjacent source.
  • Source type: Governor/DCC press releases, not a searchable database. No UCETF database, dashboard download, or case list was located.
  • Press index (from search results): DCC press releases and DCC announcements
  • Format: HTML press releases. No CSV, no case-level table. Update frequency: Quarterly / periodic announcements, not real-time.
  • Latest figures (Governor announcement 29 October 2026, via DCC press index + secondary republication): 2026 to date (through September 2026): 139,293 lb seized/destroyed, valued at >$230 million; 236,846 plants eradicated. Q3 2026 alone (July–September, nine counties): 55,308 lb; 108,226 plants; >$91 million estimated value; 14 arrests; 9 firearms; $29,215 cash seized. Since UCETF creation in 2022 (co-chaired by DCC and California Department of Fish and Wildlife, coordinated by Governor’s Office of Emergency Services): 896,514 lb; 1,441,826 plants; >$1.47 billion estimated value. Earlier milestones for series: January 2026 announcement — >$1.2 billion seized/destroyed through UCETF, “up 18x since 2022”; Q3 2025 — >$222 million; Q2 2025 DCC summary (from announcements page snippet) — >$62 million seized, 444 products recalled, 256 new licences issued, 748 businesses transitioned from provisional to annual. November (year unstated in snippet, context 2025) operations: 58,358 plants eradicated, ~3 tons processed cannabis, ~$56.5 million estimated value.
  • Limitations — critical: Dollar values are estimates; no valuation method is published in the releases. Weight, plant and dollar figures cover overlapping but not identical periods in the same release (e.g. weight “so far this year” vs plants “through September”) — do not add them into a single-year total without stating the period for each. Arrests ≠ prosecutions ≠ convictions; no conviction data in these releases. No county-by-county downloadable table; county detail appears only narratively (e.g. Q3 2026: Alameda County — >19,300 lb and >38,500 plants, Hayward/Oakland indoor sites; Los Angeles County Lancaster — ~16,760 lb, >33,000 plants). A separate DCC action (17 search warrants, Southern California, ~$29.9 million, including ~$20 million at a single Glendale distribution site) is reported as a DCC action, not necessarily a UCETF quarterly total — do not merge into UCETF totals.

5.7 CDTFA — Cannabis tax revenue, sales data and Open Data Portal

  • Holder type: Cannabis retailers / microbusinesses authorised to sell at retail (tax filers), by return period. Not a licence-holder list; CDTFA does not publish licensee names in its tax releases.
  • Quarterly news releases (opened + search): Q2 2026 (opened): CDTFA NR 26-07, 27 Aug 2026 — As of 18 Aug 2026: $152.0m excise + $109.7m sales = $261.7m; Q1 2026 revised to $249.8m ($144.7m excise + $105.1m sales), up from $247.9m initial. Q4 2025 (opened): CDTFA NR 26-01, 26 Feb 2026 — As of 18 Feb 2026: $145.5m excise + $109.6m sales = $255.1m; Q3 2025 revised to $285.5m ($177.7m excise + $107.7m sales), up from $283.7m initial. Notes: Q3 2025 excise rate temporarily 19%, back to 15% 1 Oct 2025 (AB 564); vendor-compensation programme final installment — >$1.63m retained in Q4; programme ran 1 Jan 2023 – 31 Dec 2025, allowing equity-fee-waiver retailers to retain 20% of excise due for 12 months. Q2 2025 (search): CDTFA NR 25-08, 15 Sep 2025 — $147.3m excise + $112.4m sales = $259.7m (as of 18 Aug 2025); Q1 2025 revised to $243.1m ($142.4m excise + $100.8m sales), up from $237.4m initial; vendor compensation >$1.3m retained in Q2. Archive index (search): CDTFA archived news releases — lists: Q3 2025 $283.7m (Dec 2025); Q2 2025 $259.7m (Sept 2025); Q1 2025 $237.4m (May 2025, initial); Q4 2024 $219m (Feb 2025, initial — see conflict note: later revised Q4 2024 total in secondary tables is $262.9m; the $219m figure appears to be a partial/initial release figure and must not be used as the final Q4 2024 total without checking the release); Q3 2024 $250.5m (Nov 2024); Q2 2024 $263.1m (2024); Q1 2024 $259.9m (2024).
  • Cumulative totals (opened releases): Since January 2018 to Q2 2026: nearly $8.4 billion total, including almost $4.5 billion excise + almost $3.4 billion sales + $500.6 million cultivation tax (collected through 1 July 2022, when eliminated). To Q4 2025: >$7.87 billion (>$4.19bn excise + >$3.17bn sales + $500.6m cultivation). To Q2 2025: >$7.3 billion (nearly $3.9bn excise + >$2.9bn sales + $500.6m cultivation).
  • Open Data Portal (opened landing page only): CDTFA data portal — Format: Web portal, “centralized access to CDTFA’s publicly available data in easy-to-use formats.” Categories listed include Cannabis Taxes, Taxable Sales in California, Sales and Use Taxes, Local and District Taxes, and CDTFA Interactive Data Visualizations. Exact dataset names / file names / direct download links were NOT returned by the text fetch — Verify in live browser. Do not invent a CSV filename. The portal is described as interactive/dashboard-style; whether each Cannabis Taxes dataset has an export button is Verify. How to access: Free, no login stated on landing page. Update frequency: Quarterly, following each news release, plus revisions for amended/late returns — Verify stated frequency on the portal itself.
  • Tax Guide (context, not a data file): CDTFA Tax Guide for Cannabis Businesses and Tax Facts for Cannabis Businesses — CDTFA tax facts (from search results). Confirms: 15% excise on gross receipts; sales tax applied to price after excise is added; statewide sales/use rate 7.25% plus district taxes; retailers file electronically, returns due last day of month following the period.
  • Fields available in releases: Quarter, excise tax collected, sales tax collected, total, cumulative since Jan 2018, revised prior-quarter figures, vendor-compensation retained amounts (2023–2025 only). No county breakdown, no licensee-level data, no product-category breakdown in releases.
  • Limitations: Figures exclude outstanding returns / returns still being processed at the “as of” date; every quarter is routinely revised upward 1–3% (secondary observation consistent with CDTFA’s own revision notes — treat as pattern, not CDTFA promise). “Taxable sales” (all goods reported on sales/use returns, including pipes, papers, merchandise) ≠ “cannabis sales” subject to excise (cannabis/cannabis products only). CDTFA began separately reporting cannabis-only sales in 2023. Pre-2023 “$5.8 billion market” headlines used taxable sales and overstate cannabis-only sales — see Part 6 conflict note. Local cannabis business taxes are not administered by CDTFA and are not in these totals. LAO (Legislative Analyst’s Office) quarterly updates — LAO EconTax (from search results) — give excise-due figures (Q2 2026: $152m; Q1 2026: $144m; Q4 2025: $145m; Q3 2025: $177m) and fiscal-year projections (2025–26: $630m per Sept 2026 update; 2026–27: $650m). LAO projections are forecasts, not collections — do not present as revenue.

5.8 CDPH — Medical Marijuana Identification Card (MMIC) statistics (counts only)

  • Holder type: MMIC cards issued (patient / primary caregiver), by fiscal year. Counts only — no names, no county breakdown on the state page.
  • Page (opened): CDPH MMIC card data — Title: “Medical Marijuana Identification Card Program | Patient, Primary Caregiver, Medi-Cal, and CMSP Card Data.”
  • Format: HTML table on the page. No CSV/PDF download shown. How to access: Free, no login. Programme is county-run (applications through county health departments); CDPH publishes the statewide roll-up. Update frequency: Not stated on the page (page “Last Updated” field was blank in the fetch) — Verify. The table now includes FY 2025–26, so it is updated at least annually after fiscal-year close. Latest data date: FY 2025–26 (table as fetched).
  • Fields: Fiscal year; Total cards issued; Patient; Primary Caregiver; Medi-Cal (subset of Patient totals); CMSP — County Medical Services Program (subset of Patient totals).
