Limitations — critical for Part 6: Cards issued in a fiscal year ≠ active patients. Cards are valid 12 months; the table is a flow (issuances), not a stock of current cardholders. California does not publish a statewide active-patient count on this page. No county breakdown on the state page. County MMIC counts, if published, are in individual county health department reports — none located as a statewide county table; Verify county by county if Part 3/6 needs it. A secondary market report (CannBus Q3 2026) cited “1,047 in FY 2024–25 (999 patient, 48 caregiver); 123,305 total since FY 2004–05” attributed to CDPH. That secondary citation conflicts with the opened CDPH table above (2,896 / 2,770 / 126; total 128,210) and must not be used. The CDPH page itself is authoritative; the secondary figure appears to be a different cut (possibly a partial-year or filtered extract) and is rejected here. Physician recommendations without a state MMIC are not counted here — the MMIC is optional for purchase; only the card gives the sales/use-tax exemption. So MMIC issuances understate the medical-using population by an unknown amount — Unavailable.

5.9 CDPH — Industrial Hemp Enrollment and Oversight (IHEO) authorised firms list

  • Holder type: CDPH-registered industrial hemp processors/manufacturers (food, extract, human food, cannery, pet food, cosmetics, inhalables for out-of-state only).
  • FAQ landing page (opened): CDPH IHEO FAQs — States: FAQ hub for AB 45 (2021) implementation; links to Consumer, Processor/Manufacturer, and Retailer/Enforcement FAQs; and a link labelled “List of the current authorized Industrial Hemp processors and manufacturers.”
  • Processor/Manufacturer FAQs (from search results): CDPH Processor/Manufacturer FAQs — Confirms: IHEO authorisation required to manufacture, distribute, pack or hold industrial hemp products in California, in addition to a commodity registration (e.g. Processed Food Registration); one IHEO authorisation per commodity/location; inhalable industrial hemp products may be manufactured in California only for sale outside California (in-state sale prohibited).
  • Authorised firms list — direct file (from search results, NOT opened; mirror URL returned by search — treat with caution): Search returned a mirrored copy at a non-cdph.ca.gov host stating “current as of January 14, 2026,” 5 pages, fields: Business Name, City, State, License Number, Authorized For, Expiration Date. Examples in that mirror include IH Food, IH Extractor In-State, IH Pet Food, IH Cosmetics, IH Cannery, Inhalables Out of State. Do NOT cite the mirror URL as the official source in the final report. The official list is linked from the CDPH IHCP FAQ page above; the exact official CDPH PDF URL and filename must be verified in the live browser from that link. The mirror is useful only to establish the list’s format and “as of” dating practice.
  • Format: PDF list (per mirror). No search interface found; no CSV. Update frequency: Periodic reissue with a “current as of” date on the list itself — frequency not stated on the FAQ page — Verify. Latest data date: 14 January 2026 (mirror copy). A later 2026 edition Verify in live browser.
  • Fields: Business name, city, state, licence number, authorised category, expiration date. Limitation: Inclusion “does not mean that all industrial hemp products from a business have been approved” (mirror header, consistent with CDPH practice) — it is a registration/authorisation list, not a product approval list.
  • Forms (for Part 7 cross-reference, from CDPH Food and Drug Branch forms page, search result): CDPH Food and Drug Branch forms — CDPH 8597 IH (cannery), 8610 IH / 8611 IH (extract/human food), 8676 IH (pet food), 8678 IH (cosmetics), 8763 (inhalables). These are application forms, not holder lists.

5.10 CDFA — Industrial hemp cultivation registrations (growers, breeders, research institutions)

  • Holder type: Registered industrial hemp growers, hemp breeders, and established agricultural research institutions (EARIs), registered through the county agricultural commissioner in each county of cultivation. There is no state-level grower registration — CDFA FAQ states this explicitly.
  • FAQ page (opened): CDFA hemp FAQ — Confirms: Division 24, Food & Agricultural Code; §§81003(a)(1) (growers), 81004(a)(1) (breeders), 81004.5(a)(1) (EARIs) require county registration prior to cultivation; procedures in 3 CCR §4901; annual fee $900 for grower or breeder (3 CCR §4900); county board may add a fee for actual county costs (FAC §81005(c)); registration valid one year; renewal application at least 30 calendar days before expiry. Confirms: California does have a USDA-approved state plan, effective 1 January 2022 (CDFA revised plan submitted 20 Dec 2021; USDA approved). This is the Part 7 anchor — California is not on the USDA direct-licence programme for in-state growers. Confirms: “Publication of a list of registered cultivators is available on the CDFA’s Industrial Hemp Program webpage.”
  • Registered growers list — direct download (from search results, verbatim): Dir_IHGrowers.pdf — File name: Dir_IHGrowers.pdf — Format: PDF table. Fields shown in search text: Business Name, Mailing Address, Phone Number, Email Address, County, Registration Valid From – To (e.g. registrations running May 2026–May 2027, Nov 2025–Nov 2026 in the fetched extract). Privacy note for the final report: This official list does publish business mailing addresses and business contact details as filed. The parent task prohibits reproducing personal phone/emails of individuals; business contact details from this official list may be cited in aggregate or for a business, but do not reproduce the full list or individual contact details in the Run 1 report — cite the list, its fields, and its date, and link it. Update frequency / latest date: Not stated in the search extract — Verify in live browser (the extract shows registrations valid into 2027, indicating a 2026 edition).
  • Registration summary — direct download (from search results, verbatim; direct open failed in this pass, not repeated): IndustrialHempRegistrationSummary.pdf — File name: IndustrialHempRegistrationSummary.pdf — Title in search text: “Industrial Hemp Program Registration Summary — As of February 3, 2026.” Format: PDF table by county. Fields: Registrants (Grower / Breeder / EARI / Total), Registered Sites (Grower / Breeder / EARI), Registered Acres (Grower / Breeder / EARI). County rows visible in search text (partial — do not treat as complete): Alameda 2 registrants, 9 sites, 270.24 acres; Butte 3 / 13 / 63.95; Colusa 2 / 3 / 305.00; Contra Costa 1 / 2 / 17.00; Fresno 21 (19 grower + 2 EARI) / 124 sites / 4,118.12 acres; Imperial 3 / 5 / 162.51; Kern 3 / 4 / 292.19; Riverside 8 / 9 / 347.90; Sacramento 1 / 2 / 80.00; San Diego 11 / 14 / 56.45; Sonoma 3 / 8 / 12.51; Sutter 1 / 5 / 59 acres (truncated in snippet). Total row was NOT returned in the search text — do not invent a statewide total from this PDF. The Fresno concentration (4,118 acres) is the largest county row visible, consistent with Fresno as the leading county, but a statewide total must come from the PDF total row in the live browser.
  • Hemp programme updates presentation (from search results; direct open failed, not repeated): Hemp Program Updates IHAB 13 May 2026 PDF — Search text table (Industrial Hemp Advisory Board, 13 May 2026 presentation; “as of 4/28/2026”): 2025 (full year as presented): 90 registrations; 276 sites; 376 plantings; 8,259.7 registered acres; 6,647 planted acres; 5,421.6 harvested acres; 584.11 destroyed acres. 2026 (partial, to 28 Apr 2026): 21 registrations; 99 sites; 75 plantings; 1,730.07 registered acres; 740.1 planted; 121 harvested; 0 destroyed. Do not compare 2026 partial to 2025 full-year as a trend. These are the most recent official CDFA year-to-date figures located.
  • Limitations: County registration ≠ CDFA direct licence; a grower operating in multiple counties has multiple county registrations. “Registrations” ≠ “registrants” ≠ “businesses.” Registered acres ≠ planted acres ≠ harvested acres (see §6.7 conflict). CDFA states it does not maintain a list of certified sellers of industrial hemp seed (contact California Crop Improvement Association instead) — Verify if Part 7 needs seed-seller data. Local county restrictions may apply beyond state registration; county agricultural commissioner offices are the local source.

5.11 Local (county/city) permit and equity-programme records where published

There is no statewide downloadable local-permit file. The DCC licence is contingent on local authorisation, but local records live jurisdiction by jurisdiction. The following are the published records actually located; everything else is Verify locally and must not be asserted as a published list.

  • Los Angeles, City — Department of Cannabis Regulation (DCR): Licensed-retailer map/list: DCR “Find Licensed Retailers” page (linked from LA Office of Finance page finance.lacity.gov — legal cannabis retailers — “To view a map and list of businesses with temporary approval or licenses … visit the Department of Cannabis Regulation Find Licensed Retailers page”). Format: interactive map + list. Exact DCR URL Verify in live browser (not returned as a full URL in search). DCR Commission presentation 15 Oct 2026 (PDF, from search results): LA CRC Presentation 15 Oct 2026 PDF — 740 records eligible to renew; 368 renewal applications submitted through 15 Oct 2026; 141 Social Equity records issued renewal fee waivers totalling $2,197,238; 2026 storefront retail lottery planned early December 2026 (registration November 2026; ~2,000 verified Social Equity Individual Applicants from 2019/2022/2025 verification periods eligible); cultivation + delivery licensing opened to the public 19 Oct 2026 (Council File 26-0301), social-equity exclusivity sunset 31 Dec 2025. LA Office of Finance cannabis business-tax page (above) also gives tax classifications L711 (medical sales, $50 per $1,000 gross receipts) and L712 (adult-use sales, $100 per $1,000) — tax, not a licence list, but useful for Part 3 cross-check. Limitation: DCR records are city-only; Los Angeles County unincorporated areas are a separate jurisdiction (LA County DCBA).
  • Los Angeles County — Department of Consumer and Business Affairs (DCBA): Cannabis pages at dcba.lacounty.gov/cannabis/ (recalls mirror at dcba.lacounty.gov/cannabisrecalls/). No county-wide downloadable permit list was located — Verify.
  • Oakland, City: Equity programme page: Oakland Cannabis Equity Program (from search text). News release 20 April 2026: Oakland, 20 Apr 2026 — equity certification mark — “Oakland currently has 50 permitted Equity cannabis business” [sic]; consumer list at www.legendary.green (Oakland Legendary Cannabis Certified Equity Label). Format of legendary.green list Verify (not opened). Older secondary claim (15 licensed dispensaries open + 11 conditionally approved, via CRC) conflicts with the 50-equity-business figure because the two count different universes (all dispensaries vs equity businesses) — do not merge.
  • San Francisco, City and County: Office of Cannabis equity incubator list: sf.gov equity incubator — lists possible equity incubators (business names + contact emails as filed) by business type sought. This is an incubator-matching list, not a licence list — do not present it as permitted businesses. No SF permitted-business download was located in this pass — Verify via SF Office of Cannabis permit pages.
  • State equity finder: DCC equity page: DCC equity — consumers can find licensed equity retailers via the REAL CA Campaign search tool by ZIP/city; equity retailers labelled “[Equity Retailer].” Exact REAL CA URL Verify (not returned as a full URL). DCC local-equity promising-practices page: DCC local equity promising practices — case examples (Oakland shared-use manufacturing; LA equity-exclusive licensing; SF direct grants) — narrative, not a dataset.
  • Other jurisdictions (San Jose, San Diego city/county, Sacramento city/county, Long Beach, Fresno, Riverside, San Bernardino, Orange County cities, Humboldt, Mendocino, Trinity, Santa Barbara, Monterey/Salinas, Palm Springs, Coachella, Desert Hot Springs, South Lake Tahoe): No downloadable permit/equity list was located for these jurisdictions in this Part 5 pass. That is a gap to be closed in Part 3, not a finding that no list exists. Each city/county planning or cannabis office publishes permits differently (agendas, GIS maps, PDF lists, or Accela portals). The parent must not claim a statewide local list exists. One useful statewide-adjacent source: the California State Auditor Report 2025-048, Table 1 (Auditor Table 1, Nov 2025 — from search results) gives active provisional licences by jurisdiction for 17 jurisdictions: 1 Jan 2023: 4,607 total; 30 June 2024: 2,279; 30 June 2025: 294. Examples: City of Adelanto 118 → 60 → 11; City of Los Angeles 1,177 → 951 → 113. This is an auditor analysis of DCC databases, not a local permit list, but it is the best jurisdiction-level provisional-licence time series located.

5.12 State Water Resources Control Board (Water Boards) — cannabis cultivation enrolment

  • Holder type: Cannabis cultivators enrolled for waste-discharge coverage under the Statewide Cannabis General Order.
  • Statewide programme page (from search results): Water Boards cannabis programme — Cannabis Policy + General Order Order No. WQ 2023-0102-DWQ (adopted 7 Nov 2023, revising WQ 2019-0001-DWQ). Enrollment is required for all legal cultivation facilities and is a required step to obtain a DCC cultivation licence.
  • Regional programme pages (from search results): Central Valley: Central Valley cannabis and General Order info Central Valley general order; San Francisco Bay: SF Bay cannabis; Central Coast termination guidelines (PDF): Central Coast termination guidelines PDF
  • Format: Online enrolment via the Cannabis Cultivation Programs Portal (application portal + surveys for termination, annual monitoring). No public downloadable enrollee list was located — Verify. Enrolment is proved by a Notice of Applicability (NOA) — either General WDRs or Conditional Waiver — issued to the individual cultivator.
  • Update frequency / latest data: Not published as a dataset. Regional board reports give snapshots: San Diego Region Executive Officer’s Report 11 June 2025 (PDF, from search results): San Diego EO Report 11 June 2025 PDF — as of 30 April 2025, three southern regions combined: San Diego 25 active applications / 18 active enrolment / 19 terminated; Santa Ana 103 / 90 / 31; Los Angeles 526 / 489 / 194; Totals 654 / 597 / 244. Los Angeles active enrolment detail: 485 Conditional Waiver NOAs (primarily indoor, Los Angeles County) + 4 WDR NOAs (Tier 1/2 outdoor, Ventura County). Secondary reporting of the State Water Board Executive Director’s Report 6 Oct 2026 (Marijuana Herald, 16 Oct 2026, SECONDARY): ~4,000 active cultivation water-quality enrolments, ~400 fewer than a year ago; ~500 further enrolments expected to be terminated in December 2026 for multi-year unpaid fees; 147 licensed cultivation properties suspected of diverting water without legal authorisation (DCC compliance notices sent Aug 2025); 10 of 31 authorised programme positions filled; 28% fee increase proposed for FY 2027–28. Flag as secondary reporting of an official report — verify against the official Executive Director’s Report before quoting in the final report. Historical (for series only): End of 2018 — 2,145 cultivators under statewide permit + 398 water rights issued; 3,380 still under North Coast/Central Valley regional permits transitioning by 1 July 2019; 517 inspections, 968 notices of violation in 2018 (Water Boards 2018 Accomplishments Report, PDF: 2018 Accomplishments Report PDF). Feb 2019 board item: 5,470 enrollees statewide (R1, R5, DWQ); North Coast 3,417 (Humboldt 1,208; Mendocino 485; Sonoma 58; Trinity 976 under R1).
  • Limitations: No bulk enrollee download; regional snapshots are not additive to a statewide total without double-counting risk (applications vs active enrolment vs terminated are different statuses). Water-rights (Small Irrigation Use Registrations, Division of Water Rights) are a separate permit from General Order enrolment — do not conflate.

5.13 California Department of Fish and Wildlife (CDFW) — Lake/Streambed Alteration (LSA)

  • Holder type: Cultivators (and other entities) whose activity substantially diverts/obstructs/changes a river, stream or lake bed/channel/bank — Fish & Game Code §1602.
  • Programme page (from search results): CDFW LSA programme
  • General Agreement for cannabis (regulation): 14 CCR §722 — General Lake or Streambed Alteration Agreement for Activities Related to Cannabis Cultivation (covers only stream crossings — bridge, culvert, rock ford — and water diversions on non-finfish waters for cannabis cultivation; CEQA-exempt).
  • Format: Individual LSA notifications/agreements via EPIMS; project-level Notices of Determination filed on CEQANet (e.g. EPIMS-TRI-47638-R1, Trinity County, approved 19 May 2025; EPIMS-TRI-23346-R1I, approved 10 June 2025 — both from CEQANet search results). No central downloadable list of cannabis LSA holders was located — Verify. CEQANet is searchable by SCH number / county / lead agency, not by “cannabis LSA” as a bulk category.
  • Requirement (from Water Boards/CDFW guidance, search result): Annual cultivation licences require demonstration of §1602 compliance via an LSA Agreement or written verification that none is required. (Historical CDFA-era guidance; DCC-era requirement to be confirmed in Part 4 — the DCC cultivation page states cultivators must have an LSA Agreement or written confirmation one is not needed.)
  • Limitations: LSA is project-specific and time-limited; absence of an LSA record does not prove non-compliance (verification letter suffices where no alteration occurs).

5.14 Secretary of State — Business entity search (bizfile Online)

  • Holder type: State-registered business entities (corporations, LLCs, limited partnerships, nonprofits, and out-of-state entities registered to do business in California) — not cannabis licensees as such. Included because it is the entity-identity cross-check for every licensee’s legal name.
  • Records-request page (from search results): SOS information requests
  • Search portal (from multiple search results, verbatim): bizfileonline — business search (portal home: bizfileonline.sos.ca.gov)
  • Format: Interactive web search; free PDF copies of filed documents (formation/registration, amendments, terminations, Statements of Information). Certificates of Status available online within minutes (fee applies for certified copies/status — secondary sources cite $5 certificate / $10 certified-copy handling; Verify fee on bizfile).
  • How to access: Free search; no login stated for basic search. Basic search by entity name or number; Advanced search adds status / type / filing-date filters. Results capped at 500 matches (secondary guidance — Verify).
  • Update frequency / latest data: Reflects filings once processed; new formations/status changes may lag a few business days (secondary guidance). No fixed dataset date.
  • Fields (official SOS page, search text): Entity name; entity number; formation/registration/conversion date; status; standing with FTB, SOS Agent and VCFCF; jurisdiction; entity street and mailing addresses; plus over 17 million corporate/LLC/LP images online.
  • Limitations — state explicitly in final report: SOS does not issue business licences or permits (CalGOLD is the permit guide). SOS records do not include ownership, telephone numbers, email addresses, or subsidiaries/associated businesses. Sole proprietorships, non-filing general partnerships, and fictitious business names (DBAs, filed with county clerks) are not in bizfile. Registered general partnerships / LLPs require a mail/in-person order from the Sacramento office. A clean SOS record ≠ good tax standing (FTB Entity Status Letter is the tax-standing check) and ≠ a DCC licence.

5.15 Tribal records (public)

  • Finding: No central public tribal cannabis-licence list was located from an official tribal or state source in this pass.
  • What exists: USDA AMS approved hemp plans list (see §5.16) includes tribal plans. A secondary tribal directory (500nations, undated, SECONDARY — do not cite as official) listed California tribal hemp plans as: Cahuilla Band of Indians (under review in that old table), La Jolla Band of Luiseño Indians, Pala Band of Mission Indians, Santa Rosa Band of Cahuilla Indians, Yurok Tribe (approved in that table) — all Verify against the current USDA AMS list, which is the only official tribal-plan source. Secondary tribal-market reporting (April–Oct 2025/2026, SECONDARY) claims California ranks second nationally with 15 tribally owned stores operated by 12 tribes, naming Twenty-Nine Palms Band of Mission Indians (Red Falcon, Coachella / Twentynine Palms / Yucca Valley), Iipay Nation of Santa Ysabel (Mountain Source; SoCal First Nations distribution), Sycuan Band of the Kumeyaay Nation (Sessions by the Bay, National City — with consumption lounge), Tule River Tribe (Eh-Lo Dispensary, Springville area). None of this is an official tribal or DCC record and must not be presented as a verified tribal list in Part 10. Tribal cannabis operations on tribal land are governed by tribal law; DCC licensing requires a sovereign-immunity waiver and local authorisation, which tribes have resisted — that legal friction is why no unified list exists.
  • Action for Part 10: Check each named tribe’s own government website / tribal code for an ordinance, and USDA AMS for a hemp plan. Do not build a tribal table from secondary directories.

5.16 USDA — Hemp records relevant to California

  • California state plan status (official): USDA AMS “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans” — USDA AMS state and tribal plan review — updated 17 February 2026 (search text). California row: “CA Approved Plan (pdf)*” — approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021; CDFA FAQ gives effective date 1 January 2022. Use CDFA’s 1 Jan 2022 effective date for California operations; USDA’s list confirms approval status.
  • Hemp Public Search Tool (official, per AMS FAQ): USDA AMS hemp FAQ — FAQ states: “Information on licensees in your area can be obtained through the Hemp Public Search Tool.” Exact public-tool URL was not returned as a full URL in search — Verify in live browser. Do not confuse with the law-enforcement tool below.
  • Law-enforcement search tool (NOT public): HeMP Law Enforcement Search Tool at https://hempsearch.ams.usda.gov (from AMS user guide, search text) — requires email request to farmbill.hemp@usda.gov + Login.gov / eAuth credentials; searchable by producer name, growing state/county, city, licence number. Public researchers cannot use this tool. It returns state/tribal-authorised producers as well as USDA-licensed producers, with FSA lot maps where available. AMS itself warns the tool may not capture all approved growing locations at first.
  • Grower information page: USDA AMS information for hemp growers — confirms: licence/authorisation must come from the state, tribal or USDA programme for the growing location; if the state/tribe has an approved or pending plan, the grower must apply there, not to USDA. Therefore USDA direct licences are NOT the California source — CDFA county registrations (§5.10) are.
  • FSA acreage reporting: All licensed hemp producers must set up a Farm Profile with the Farm Service Agency and report hemp within 30 days of planting (annual deadline 15 July per AMS FAQ). FSA crop-acreage data are a separate USDA dataset (see §6.7) — not a licence list.
  • Limitations: USDA AMS publishes plan approvals, not a public California grower roster. For California growers, CDFA is the roster (§5.10). For tribal growers on tribal land, the tribal plan + AMS plan list are the roster anchors.

5.17 Other / third-party licence-data sources (for transparency, NOT official)

  • Cannlytics open dataset (docs: Cannlytics cannabis licences dataset docs — from search results): California subset ca sourced from https://search.cannabis.ca.gov/. For-profit data company; sampling bias warned by the provider itself; adult-use focus noted. Use only to show that third-party scrapes exist; never cite as DCC data.
  • Phenominal / CaNORML compliance reports (example: Phenominal example licence report PDF — from search results): Re-packages DCC License Search + Compliance Action Records + Final Decisions + Recalls Portal + CCAP decisions, with a stated DCC refresh date (e.g. 19 Aug 2026) and sync date (30 Oct 2026). Useful as a finding aid to locate DCC records for a single licence, but its counts and characterisations are secondary.
  • REAL CA Campaign equity-retailer search (via DCC equity page): Consumer finder, not a dataset.
  • County GIS / Accela portals (e.g. former CalCannabis Accela): Legacy portals appear in old guides; current DCC search supersedes them.

PART 6 — MARKET DATA

6.0 How to read California market numbers (read before any table)

California publishes three different “sales” concepts that are routinely conflated in secondary reporting. The final report must keep them apart:

  1. CDTFA “taxable sales” — everything reported on cannabis retailers’ sales/use-tax returns, including non-cannabis merchandise (pipes, papers, apparel). This is the pre-2023 headline series ($5.8bn in 2021).
  2. CDTFA “cannabis sales” (excise base) — retail sales of cannabis/cannabis products only, i.e. gross receipts subject to the 15%/19% excise tax. Separately reported from 2023. This is the series behind “$4.4bn in 2023, $4.2bn in 2024, $3.9bn in 2025” (see §6.1). Implied cannabis sales = excise collected ÷ rate in force (with vendor-compensation retentions as a small adjustment — see below).
  3. DCC Metrc track-and-trace sales — licensee-entered retail sales by customer type (adult-use vs medicinal) and product, shown on DCC dashboards. DCC 2021 total (adult-use + medical) was $5.35bn ($4.8bn+ adult-use + $540m medical), vs CDTFA taxable $5.8bn — the ~$450m gap is largely non-cannabis merchandise + medical-sales reporting differences + timing. DCC 2022 total was $4.9bn ($4.54bn adult-use + $360m medical).

Vendor compensation: 2023–2025 equity-fee-waiver retailers could retain 20% of excise due for 12 months. Retentions reduce collected excise slightly below due excise: Q2 2025 >$1.3m retained; Q4 2025 >$1.63m retained (final installment). Programme ended 31 Dec 2025. Excise-implied sales calculated from collected excise therefore slightly understate gross cannabis sales in 2023–2025. The effect is ~0.5–1% per quarter — material for precision, immaterial for trend.

Revisions: Every CDTFA quarter is revised upward for amended/late returns. Always cite the latest revised figure for a quarter and note the initial figure where it differs (see table).

6.1 Taxable sales and excise revenue by year and quarter (CDTFA official releases)

Why the Q1–Q3 2025 “later table” differs: A secondary table (californiacannabis.org, tax page, crawled 2026) reproduces CDTFA figures to the dollar (e.g. Q2 2026: $152,042,993 / $109,686,814 / $261,729,807 — matching the opened CDTFA release to rounding) but shows higher Q1–Q3 2025 figures than the CDTFA revision notes quoted above. The most likely explanation is further revision after the next quarter’s release (CDTFA revises each quarter more than once). For the final report: use the CDTFA Open Data Portal as the single source for the full revised quarterly series and cite the portal, not the secondary table. The table above preserves both so the reader can see the revision trail.

Annual cannabis-only retail sales (CDTFA excise-base series, as reported secondarily from CDTFA data)

  • 2023: $4.45bn cannabis sales; taxable sales $5.2bn (CDTFA, via MJBizDaily/Beard Bros summaries, SECONDARY reporting of official data). Cannabis = ~85% of taxable sales.
  • 2024: $4.21bn cannabis sales (Beard Bros / MJBizDaily, SECONDARY reporting of CDTFA data). Taxable sales 2024: $4.9bn (MJBizDaily Feb 2026, SECONDARY).
  • 2025: $3.93bn cannabis sales, −6.5% vs 2024 (Beard Bros / MJBizDaily Feb 2026, SECONDARY reporting of CDTFA data). Taxable sales 2025: $4.8bn (MJBizDaily). Q4 2025 cannabis sales: $979m (MJBizDaily), vs ~$1,025m in Q4 2024.
  • Excise collections, full year (from secondary annualisation — Verify against portal): 2024: $627.5m; 2025: $623.2m (Beard Bros). The near-flat excise total despite falling sales is explained by the Q3 2025 19% rate.
  • Peak and decline (multiple secondary summaries, consistent): Licensed retail sales peaked ~$5.2bn in 2021 on the old taxable-sales definition; on the DCC Metrc definition the 2021 peak was $5.35bn total ($4.8bn adult-use + $0.54bn medical). By either definition, sales have declined every year since 2021: 2022 $5.3bn taxable / $4.9bn Metrc; 2023 $4.4bn cannabis-only; 2024 $4.2bn; 2025 $3.9bn.
  • 2026 year-to-date (CDTFA official + MJBizDaily secondary): Q1 2026 cannabis sales $963.9m (MJBizDaily, from CDTFA); Q2 2026 $1.013bn (MJBizDaily: “only slightly more than $1.006bn in Q2 2025” and “second consecutive quarter above $1bn” in the DCC/Metrc framing — note MJBizDaily’s Q2 2025 $1.006bn differs from the excise-implied Q2 2025 figure (~$982m at 15% on $147.3m, or ~$999m on $149.9m) — definition difference: MJBizDaily Q2 figure appears to be a DCC/track-and-trace or revised-CDTFA figure; Verify before quoting a Q2 2025 sales level). H1 2026 ≈ $1.977bn (Q1 $963.9m + Q2 $1,013m), described as “flat vs H1 2025” (Beard Bros).
  • Q3 2025 weakness (Beard Bros, SECONDARY): Retailers reported $960.4m cannabis sales in Q3 2025 (19% quarter), −6.9% vs Q3 2024, described as the lowest quarterly total since CDTFA began reporting cannabis-only sales in 2023. Excise-implied check: $177.7m ÷ 19% = $935m; $180.7m ÷ 19% = $951m — both below $960.4m, consistent with vendor-compensation retentions + revision timing. Do not present $960.4m as CDTFA-direct without the portal check.
  • Cumulative retail sales since Jan 2018 (MJBizDaily, SECONDARY reporting of CDTFA): >$16bn cumulative retail sales (Q2 2026 article). CDTFA tax cumulative ($8.4bn to Q2 2026) is the official cumulative figure; the $16bn sales cumulative is derived and Verify.

DCC Metrc sales detail (for Part 6 product/category context)

  • DCC Monthly Sales Summary (dashboard, §5.2) is the official source for adult-use vs medicinal split and product mix, but no dashboard values were readable in this pass — Verify in live browser.
  • Secondary product-mix snapshots (all SECONDARY, do not present as DCC-direct): Q2 2026 units (MJBizDaily, from DCC): 39.1m units across top 4 categories (vapes, packaged eighths, infused pre-rolls, edibles). Q1 2026 (Dales Report newsletter, Oct 2026, SECONDARY): 36.4m units, flat vs prior year; category revenue per quarter: vapes $350.8m, flower $312.8m, pre-rolls $184.5m — note: these appear to be quarterly category totals from Headset/DCC data, not CDTFA; Verify. Dec 2025 top-5 categories (CannBus, SECONDARY, “from DCC data as reported by Cannabis Business Times”): Vapes $124.4m; Flower $107.6m; Pre-rolls $64.7m; Edibles $45.6m; Concentrates (figure truncated in snippet) — Verify. Long-run mix shift (CannBus, SECONDARY, modelled): Flower share of sales 44.3% (Apr 2021) → 30.2% (2025); Vapes 21.5% → 31.4%. Direction (flower down, vapes up to #1) is consistent across secondary sources; exact shares are modelled, not official. Medicinal share: DCC dashboard H1 2023 showed medical $143.3m vs adult-use $2.3bn (~5.9%) (CannBus calc., SECONDARY). Medicinal share of licensed flower 2024 ~6.5% (92.7k lb medicinal vs 1.337m lb adult-use, CannBus calc., SECONDARY). Latest medical/adult-use split Unavailable from official sources located — flag as gap.

6.2 Licence counts over time and closures

  • Official flows: See §5.3 — FY 2023–24: 712 issued / 8,939 renewed / 5 denied / 125 suspended / 51 revoked. FY 2024–25: 849 issued / 7,225 renewed / 97 denied / 192 suspended / 79 revoked. Renewals fell by 1,714 year-on-year — consistent with a shrinking active base and provisional wind-down, but renewal counts alone do not prove closures (a licence can lapse without a revocation/surrender record in the same year).
  • Provisional wind-down (official-adjacent): Auditor Table 1 (17 jurisdictions): 4,607 → 2,279 → 294 active provisional (Jan 2023 → June 2024 → June 2025). DCC Supplemental: 2,158 transitioned to annual in FY 2024–25. The remaining provisional tail at 1 Jan 2026 (when provisional licences ceased to be effective, subject to the equity exception) is Verify — a Mendocino County secondary claim (476 of 535 Mendocino cultivation licences still provisional on 1 Jan 2026, unable to renew/convert) is SECONDARY and county-specific; do not generalise statewide.
  • Cultivation contraction (SECONDARY reporting of DCC Outlook): Active cultivation licences 8,493 (end 2021) → ~4,805 (end 2024), −43%. This is the strongest cultivation-closure signal located, but it is secondary — verify against the Outlook PDF.
  • Retail contraction (SECONDARY): Active retail sites 1,927 (end 2023) → 1,760 (Q2 2026), −9% (Beard Bros, from DCC/CDTFA data). Storefront + delivery licence counts in §5.18 (1,210 + 241, Jan 2026) are not directly comparable to “sites.”
  • Inactive > active claim (SECONDARY): 10,828 inactive/surrendered vs 8,514 active (law-firm analysis of DCC registry, date unstated). If true, it means the cumulative registry (all statuses ever issued) now has more dead than live licences — a useful “churn” framing, but not a closure rate. Do not present as “10,828 closures.”
  • No official “closures” series (licences surrendered/revoked/expired by year, all types) was located beyond the FY flow counts above. Gap: Parent should request the DCC Licence Summary dashboard time series or the next Legislative Report for a true closure series.

6.3 Licensed vs illicit market (official first)

Official source: ERA Economics, California Cannabis Market Outlook, 2024 Report, prepared for DCC, January 2025, released 3 March 2026 (per secondary citations; the Outlook PDF itself was not opened in this pass — Verify title/date against the PDF). All “official” illicit figures below are ERA-via-DCC, as reported in legislative analysis and Governor releases. Secondary reporting is consistent across at least four independent summaries, which increases confidence in the headline numbers but does not replace the PDF check.

  • Licensed share of California consumption, 2024: ~38–40% licensed / ~60–62% unlicensed. The 38% / 62% split appears in the Senate Health Committee analysis of AB 564 and in Cannabis Business Times reporting of the ERA report; the Governor’s April 2025 release summary says “about 40%” licensed. Use “about 38–40% licensed” with the range stated, not a false-precision single figure. The share is described as “stable” vs prior years — the licensed market is growing in volume but not gaining share.
  • Consumption volumes, 2024 (ERA): Licensed: ~1.4 million lb (dry-flower equivalent). Unlicensed: ~2.4 million lb. Total consumption ~3.8m lb.
  • Production volumes, 2024 (ERA): Licensed production: ~1.4–1.43 million lb, +11.8% vs 2023, +70%+ vs 2020. Unlicensed production: ~11.4 million lb per year (≈8× licensed). Of unlicensed production, ~9 million lb illegally leaves the state (export). This export figure is the key to the “California grows for the national illicit market” finding — do not present 11.4m lb as California consumption.
  • Gross value / tax-gap framing (SECONDARY / legislative, not ERA-direct): One legislative/press summary claimed California “could have generated $22.7bn in tax revenue” if the entire market were captured, vs $8.4bn actually collected since 2018. That $22.7bn is a hypothetical, not a market size — do not quote as market value. A separate secondary claim puts the total California cannabis economy at $12.9bn (Dales Report, citing “state agricultural data” — Verify; do not use without source).
  • Illicit value via seizures (Governor/UCETF, §5.6): 2024 seizures $534m estimated retail value (Governor release, via Hemp Gazette summary); since 2019, >$3.1bn seized/destroyed over >1,500 operations, >800 tons (Governor April 2025 release summary). Seizure value ≠ market size — it is an enforcement output, not a demand estimate.
  • Why the licensed share stays low (ERA + legislative analysis, consistent): Wholesale/retail price declines, taxes/fees/compliance costs, and restricted retail access (56% of cities/counties allow no retail (DCC, February 2026) [AUDIT CORRECTION: the 57% figure is March 2025 secondary reporting and is superseded] — see Part 3; CannBus cites DCC March 2025 report: 46% allow at least one business type, 57% allow no retail). ERA explicitly states the licensed market is not shrinking in volume — retail volume for nearly all product categories rose in 2024; dollar decline is price-driven (see §6.5).
  • No official 2025 or 2026 illicit-share update was located. The 2024 ERA report is the latest official illicit estimate at the 1 Nov 2026 cut-off. Gap.

6.4 Patient / MMIC counts (see also §5.8)

  • Official: CDPH MMIC issuances table (§5.8). Headline trend: Issuances peaked at 12,659 in FY 2009–10, fell to 2,701 in FY 2020–21, recovered slightly to 3,056 in FY 2025–26. Post-Prop 64 issuances run ~2,700–3,300/year (FY 2020–21 to FY 2025–26), vs 6,000–7,000/year pre-2018 and 10,000+ in FY 2009–11.
  • What this does NOT mean: There is no official active-patient count. Do not multiply issuances by years, do not call 128,210 “patients” (it is cumulative cards issued since FY 2004–05, including renewals and deceased/expired cards).
  • Medical sales (secondary): DCC Metrc medical sales $540m in 2021, $360m in 2022 (Cannabis Business Times summary of DCC dashboards, SECONDARY); H1 2023 medical $143.3m vs adult-use $2.3bn (CannBus calc.). No official medical-sales total for 2023–2026 was located — gap.

6.5 Official price data and price declines

Official source: ERA Economics / DCC California Cannabis Market Outlook (2024 Report), Tables 1–2, as reported in legislative analysis and CannBus summary. The Outlook PDF itself was not opened — Verify table numbers against the PDF before final use.

  • Wholesale flower price, nominal, annual average ($/lb): 2020: $1,377; 2021: $1,297; 2022: $791; 2023: $750; 2024: $721. (CannBus table from Outlook Table 2.)
  • Inflation-adjusted decline: Wholesale prices −57% from Q4 2020 peak to Q4 2024 (ERA, via Substack/legislative summary). By grow type, Q4 2024 vs Q4 2020 inflation-adjusted: Outdoor −74%; Indoor −46% (ERA, via secondary summary). Nominal 2020→2024 decline is −47.6% ($1,377 → $721); the −57% figure is inflation-adjusted and Q4-to-Q4, not annual-average nominal — do not conflate.
  • 2024 within-year: Nominal wholesale prices −3.9% for full-year 2024, but +4.8% year-on-year in Q4 2024; gross industry value +7.5% in 2024 despite price falls, because licensed production rose 11.8% (Senate Health AB 564 analysis, quoting ERA). This is the “volume up, dollars down, then stabilising” nuance the final report must preserve.
  • Retail price, eighth-ounce (SECONDARY, GreenBlazer summary of DCC data): ~$38.86 in early 2020 → $22.72 by mid-2024 (inflation-adjusted). A separate secondary piece (Medium, Oct 2026) puts the average legal eighth at ~$21.28 in 2026 and raw flower in an eighth at ~$2.58 wholesale at $334/lb outdoor — both secondary; Verify against DCC Sales & Price Per Unit dashboard.
  • Retail flower price, 2025 (SECONDARY, Cannabis Business Times price survey, Jan 2026 article): California average retail flower $62.25/oz in November 2025 (all-time low in that survey, just above Michigan’s $59.79); $2.36/g average for ounce-packaged flower in 2025; $7.30/g for 1-gram packages (premium small-format). Headset (via MJBizDaily Feb 2026): flower price −1.9% in 2025 to $28.34/unit (unit definition unstated — likely average item price, not per gram — Verify).
  • Wholesale spot prices, late Sept 2026 (SECONDARY, Cannabis Benchmarks via Dales Report): Outdoor $334/lb (−11% since Jan 2026); Mixed-light $566/lb; Indoor $983/lb (−31% in 2026). Outdoor $334/lb vs 2021 >$1,200/lb is the starkest single price fact, but it is a spot benchmark, not a DCC official price — label it as such. Hall of Flowers preview (SECONDARY) claims “57% wholesale decline since 2020” with 2024→2025 flower prices −3.04% (“most stable period on record”) — consistent direction with ERA, but secondary.
  • DCC dashboard price source: Sales & Price Per Unit Report (Metrc) is the official retail price-per-unit source by item group — Verify in live browser for 2025–2026 values; no dashboard price table was readable here.
  • Units vs dollars (official ERA finding, via legislative analysis): “Decrease in total dollar amount of retail sales is driven by lower prices, not less production” and “quantity sold, in flower weight and units of edibles/vape cartridges, has continued to increase.” Q1 2026 units 36.4m, flat vs prior year (Dales/Headset, SECONDARY); 2025 units 208.7m vs 210.4m in 2024 (−0.8%, Headset via MJBizDaily, SECONDARY). The units-flat / dollars-down pattern holds in both official (ERA) and secondary (Headset) lenses.

6.6 Market concentration

  • No official market-concentration measure (e.g. top-10 share, HHI, county concentration index) was located from DCC, CDTFA or ERA summaries in this pass. Do not invent a concentration ranking.
  • What official sources do say about distribution (for Part 3 cross-reference, not a concentration claim): DCC Harvest Report gives cultivators by county (dashboard, Verify). CDFA hemp summary (§5.10) shows Fresno County dominant for hemp (4,118 of the visible county acres at 3 Feb 2026). LA County holds 156 of 281 equity-labelled storefront licences (Sept 2026 secondary extract, §5.18) — a geographic fact about equity licences, not overall market concentration. Retail access: 57% of jurisdictions allow no retail (DCC March 2025 report, via CannBus, SECONDARY reporting of official data); 56% ban retail outright (Dales, SECONDARY). The 1-point difference is rounding/definition — use “over half” with the DCC March 2025 citation, Verify exact figure.
  • Gap: If the final report needs concentration, the DCC Licence Summary (by county) + Monthly Sales Summary (by licensee, if published at that granularity — likely aggregated only) must be checked in the live browser. Licensee-level sales are not published by DCC in the sources located.

6.7 Hemp acreage, registration counts and hemp product market figures

CDFA registrations (official, §§5.10)

  • 2025 full year (IHAB presentation 13 May 2026, search text): 90 registrations; 276 sites; 376 plantings; 8,259.7 registered acres; 6,647 planted acres; 5,421.6 harvested acres; 584.11 destroyed acres.
  • 2026 partial to 28 Apr 2026: 21 registrations; 99 sites; 75 plantings; 1,730.07 registered; 740.1 planted; 121 harvested; 0 destroyed. Partial-year — do not annualise.
  • 3 Feb 2026 county summary (search text, partial rows): Fresno 4,118.12 acres (124 sites) is the largest visible county; next visible: Colusa 305.00; Kern 292.19; Alameda 270.24; Riverside 347.90. Statewide total row not returned — Verify.
  • Fee: $900/year grower/breeder (CDFA FAQ, opened).

USDA NASS National Hemp Reports (official federal series)

  • 2023 report (released 17 Apr 2024; NASS presentation PDF: NASS presentation, 17 Apr 2024): California planted 2,100 acres; harvested 1,900 acres; production value $118.4m (value shown as “↑118.4” in presentation snippet — Verify value and (D) disclosure flag against the report table). US totals 2023: 27,680 planted; 21,079 harvested; $258.176m value (presentation snippet).
  • 2024 report (released 17 Apr 2025, via secondary summaries — report PDF not opened): California planted 3,150 acres; harvested 2,600 acres. Secondary summary: California 3rd nationally for planted (after Texas, South Dakota), 2nd for harvested (after South Dakota); ~7% of US planted, ~8% of US harvested. US totals 2024: 45,294 planted; 32,694 harvested; value ~$445–450m (secondary summaries differ by $5m — Verify against NASS table).
  • 2025 report (released 16 Apr 2026; NASS PDF: NASS National Hemp Report, 16 Apr 2026): California planted 4,000 acres; harvested 3,800 acres (table: CA 2,100 / 3,150 / 4,000 planted 2023/2024/2025; 1,900 / 2,600 / 3,800 harvested). US totals 2025: 49,267 planted (+9% vs 2024); 43,707 harvested (+34%); $739m total value (+64% vs 2024) — open-field value $646m (+53%); under-protection value $93.3m (from $28.7m in 2024) on 3.99m sq ft (+13%). Floral hemp (open): $574m (from $386m in 2024), 33.2m lb from 16,880 acres. California-specific 2025 value by utilisation was not returned in the search text — Verify against the NASS California table; do not allocate the US $739m to California.
  • Secondary California claim (hempcbdbusinessplans, undated/2026, SECONDARY — conflicts with CDFA, see below): California 2025: 67 growers, 12 breeders, 7 EARIs, 89 manufacturers; 4,687 registered acres (+31% vs 2024); 4,000 planted; 3,800 harvested; Fresno 55% of registered acres / 2,100 of ~5,000 acres as of 5 Aug 2026; manufacturers split 15 inhalables (out-of-state), 51 food, 24 cosmetics (totals 90, close to the 89 headline — rounding/overlap). This secondary “4,687 registered acres” conflicts with CDFA’s 8,259.7 registered acres for 2025 and must not be used as the CDFA figure. Possible explanations (different cut date, different definition of “registered,” exclusion of EARI/breeder acres) are unverified — flag, don’t reconcile by guesswork. Note the secondary planted/harvested figures (4,000 / 3,800) do match USDA NASS 2025 exactly, which suggests the secondary author copied NASS for planted/harvested but used a different source for registrations/acres.
  • Historical secondary (for context only, SECONDARY): 2019: 501 farmers, 35,001 acres (Kern 7,841; Riverside 7,196); 2020: 38,464 acres registered by 558 growers; 2023 recovery: 90 growers, 2,100 planted / 1,900 harvested (matches NASS). Verify 2019–2020 against CDFA historical summaries before use.

Hemp product market figures (no official California hemp-product sales total located)

  • No CDPH or CDTFA California hemp-product sales/revenue total was located. Hemp products are not subject to the cannabis excise tax and are not in CDTFA cannabis releases. Gap — state this explicitly in the final report.
  • US hemp value (official NASS, above) is the only official hemp-market dollar figure available: US $739m in 2025 (all utilisations, open + protected). California’s share of that value is Unavailable from the search text — the NASS California value table must be opened.
  • Secondary US hemp context (label as secondary): CRS Farm Bill Primer (10 Jan 2025, from USDA reports): US farm-level hemp value $291m in 2023 vs $824m in 2021; 2023 harvested leaders South Dakota 2,900; Montana 2,450; Oregon 2,050; California 1,900; Missouri 1,410; Idaho 1,260; Kentucky 1,210 acres. Per-acre gross values (Hemp.com analysis of NASS 2024, SECONDARY, derived): floral ~$32,600/acre; fibre ~$594/acre; blended open-field ~$12,800/acre — derived estimates, not NASS-published per-acre prices; do not present as official prices.
  • Intoxicating-hemp market: No official California intoxicating-hemp sales figure was located. The federal dates in the parent brief (PL 119-37 §781 effective 12 Nov 2026, limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026; 0.4 mg total-THC per-container cap after 11 Dec 2026) post-date this 1 Nov 2026 cut-off and therefore cannot have a California sales effect in the data above. Do not backfill a 2026 intoxicating-hemp market size.

6.8 Cannabis-friendly banks and credit unions

Official position first: There is NO official California list of cannabis-friendly banks/credit unions published by DFPI, DCC or CDTFA that was located. What exists officially is guidance, not a roster. Any bank/credit-union list below is SECONDARY and must be verified institution-by-institution before publication (cannabis programmes open/close without a state announcement).

  • Official guidance (historical, still cited): California Department of Business Oversight (DBO, now DFPI) Cannabis Banking Guidance, 3 Oct 2019 (via Manatt / RLR summaries of the DBO release, SECONDARY reporting of official guidance — Verify original DFPI/DBO PDF): “The DBO will not bring regulatory actions against state-chartered banks or credit unions solely for establishing a banking relationship with licensed cannabis businesses,” provided institutions comply with FinCEN BSA expectations (customer due diligence, ongoing monitoring, SAR filing — “Marijuana Limited” SARs where appropriate — and Cole Memo priorities as then in force). The guidance does not change federal law; federal regulator risk remains. A DBO examiner questionnaire accompanied the guidance.
  • Regulator: California Department of Financial Protection and Innovation (DFPI) — regulates California-chartered banks/credit unions (consumer services line cited secondarily as 1-866-275-2677, Ask.DFPI@dfpi.ca.gov — Verify). DFPI sits under the new Business and Consumer Services Agency (BCSA) from 1 July 2026 (secondary WilmerHale alert, May–June 2026 — Verify agency structure for Part 9).
  • Federal framework (context, not California law): FinCEN 2014 cannabis banking guidance (BSA/SAR expectations) remains the operative federal compliance framework cited by every secondary source; no federal cannabis banking law exists (parent brief baseline). Card-network processing for marijuana remains generally unavailable; cash, ACH, wires, debit and cash-logistics are the typical service set.
  • Secondary institution lists (all SECONDARY, September 2026 unless noted; Verify each before naming in the final report): Safe Harbor Financial — fintech, not a bank (own FAQ: Safe Harbor FAQ — deposits held by FDIC/NCUA-insured partner banks/credit unions, insured to $250,000 per depositor per ownership category; serves 41 states including California; services: checking, debit, ACH, wires, bill pay, cash logistics, lending; launched 2015). Credit unions named secondarily for California: Salal Credit Union; KeyPoint Credit Union (checking/savings, remote deposit, ACH, wires, cash pickup); North Bay Credit Union (cannabis accounts, ACH, wires, debit; payments via Greenbax); Numerica Credit Union; Timberland Bank (bank, despite the name, listed among credit-union groupings in secondary lists — Verify charter); Partner Colorado Credit Union (via Safe Harbor). Banks named secondarily for California: Herring Bank; Summit Banking; First Citizens Bank; Needham Bank; Blue Sky Bank; Home Bank of California. Fintech/connectors named secondarily: Green Check (connects operators to banks/credit unions/lenders). Services typically offered (secondary consensus): Business checking, cash deposits (often via secure transport), ACH, domestic wires, remote/mobile deposit, online/mobile banking, bill pay, treasury/cash management, debit cards; lending is limited and institution-specific. Due diligence typically requires DCC licence, local permits, ownership, expected revenue, source of funds, cash volume, vendors, tax returns, POS reports, Metrc records (secondary summary — plausible, but Verify against an institution’s onboarding checklist before stating as a requirement). Hemp/CBD banking (secondary consensus): Broader than marijuana banking; compliant (non-intoxicating) hemp/CBD businesses generally have wider options, while intoxicating-hemp businesses face greater restrictions (secondary summary, Sept 2026). No official DFPI distinction was located — Verify.
  • Failed/expired proposal (do not present as law): SB 930 (state-chartered cannabis banks, 2022-era proposal) is cited secondarily as “proposed / not fully implemented.” Verify status on leginfo in Part 1; do not list SB 930 as an available banking route.

PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES, REGISTRATIONS AND AUTHORISATIONS

Date accessed: 1 November 2026. Cut-off: 1 November 2026. Official pages opened in this run: CDFA Industrial Hemp FAQ (cdfa.ca.gov/plant/industrialhemp/faq.html), CDPH AB 8 FAQ (AB 8 FAQ), CDPH form CDPH 8610 IH (3/2026) (cdph8610ih.pdf), CDPH form CDPH 8763 (3/2026) (cdph8763.pdf), CDPH forms index (Food and Drug Branch), Fresno County Agricultural Commissioner Industrial Hemp page (Fresno County Industrial Hemp). Everything else in this Part is a search extract, a county document, or a secondary legislative analysis, labelled as such — do not upgrade those labels.

7.1 Overview: three agencies, no single “hemp licence”

California has no single hemp licence. The correct question is always: which activity, which agency, which commodity, which location?

ActivityAgencyInstrumentPre-2028 position
Cultivation (grower, breeder, EARI)CDFA, via county agricultural commissionerCounty registration, Food and Agricultural Code (FAC) Division 24Current law; see §7.2
Handling / processing / dealing in hemp as a farm product for resale or processingCDFA Market Enforcement Branch (MEB)Processor / dealer licence, FAC Division 20Current law; overlap with CDPH IHEO unresolved — see §7.4
Manufacturing / processing food, extract, pet food, cosmetics, inhalablesCDPH, Food and Drug BranchBase commodity registration/licence plus Industrial Hemp Enrollment and Oversight (IHEO) authorisationCurrent law; see §7.3
Extracting cannabinoids from hemp; manufacturing inhalables; selling intoxicating hempDCCNo DCC hemp licence type exists at the cut-offFrom 1 January 2028 only — see §7.5
Retail sale of compliant hemp products in general retailNo DCC licence; CDPH product rules + general retail lawNo hemp-retail licenceCurrent law; see §7.7
Retail sale in a DCC-licensed cannabis storeDCCProhibited at cut-offFrom 1 January 2028, intoxicating hemp enters the DCC system — see §§7.5, 7.7

7.1.1 Timeline — do not collapse these dates

DateEvent
2021, AB 45Industrial hemp products brought under CDPH / Sherman Law framework; IHEO created
1 January 2022USDA-approved California state hemp plan effective (plan submitted 20 December 2021)
23 September 2024CDPH emergency regulations DPH-24-005: no detectable THC per serving, 21+, ≤5 servings per package
20 October 2025CDPH regular rulemaking approved by OAL (further serving/age track approved 21 April 2026 per CDPH page — Verify current Title 17 §§23000, 23005, 23010, 23015, 23100)
1 January 2026AB 8 operative in part: THC/synthetic ban in consumable hemp extract, inhalable hemp-THC sale ban in California, cosmetics topical ≤0.3% exception
12 November 2026Federal PL 119-37 §781 takes effect — but see next row
11 December 2026Federal PL 119-103 §2019 limit expires; after this date the full federal total-THC hemp definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. No California response located — Verify after 11 December 2026
31 December 2026USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp delayed to this date
1 January 2028AB 8 Phase 2: DCC licensing for hemp extraction and inhalable manufacture; intoxicating hemp as cannabis in the DCC system

Federal / Schedule III interaction (fixed baseline, not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity only. California licenses medical (M-type) and adult-use (A-type) under one system and most licensees hold both — dual holders must split medical from adult-use activity. Whether any tribal medical programme counts as “state-licensed” for Schedule III remains Verify (see §7.8 and Part 10).

7.2 CDFA cultivation — grower, breeder, EARI

Official source opened: CDFA Industrial Hemp FAQ, cdfa.ca.gov/plant/industrialhemp/faq.html — date accessed 1 November 2026.

7.2.1 Definition — verbatim

FAC §81000(a)(7) / Health and Safety Code (HSC) §11018.5, as quoted in the CDFA FAQ:

“Industrial hemp” or “Hemp” means an agricultural product, whether growing or not, limited to types of Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including seeds, derivatives, extracts, resin, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, with a delta-9 THC concentration of no more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis.

No personal-use hemp cultivation. The FAQ states California law does not provide for cultivation of industrial hemp for personal use like the Adult Use of Marijuana Act provides for cannabis. Regardless of purpose, all cultivation requires registration with the respective county agricultural commissioner.

7.2.2 Three registrant classes — county registration only

ClassStatuteNotes
GrowerFAC §81003(a)(1)Commercial cultivation
Hemp breederFAC §81004(a)(1)Variety development; requires a variety development plan (see §7.2.5)
Established agricultural research institution (EARI)FAC §81004.5(a)(1)Research; requires an approved research plan

All must register with the county agricultural commissioner prior to cultivation. Procedures in 3 CCR §4901. There is no state registration to grow industrial hemp; only county registration is required. A separate registration is required for each county in which the applicant intends to grow (3 CCR §4900(a)(2)). Contact the applicable county/city for additional local restrictions — CDFA does not clear local restrictions.

7.2.3 Fee, term, renewal — with a conflict to preserve

  • Fee: $900.00 per applicant for grower or hemp breeder registration, submitted with the application to the commissioner (3 CCR §4900(a)(1); CDFA FAQ). Renewal fee: $900 per registrant in each county of continued cultivation (3 CCR §4900(b)(1)).
  • County fee on top: under FAC §81005(c) a county board of supervisors may also establish a fee to cover the actual costs of the commissioner and county. Check the county — the $900 is not the whole cost.
  • Per-site question — Verify before publishing as a general rule: a Santa Clara County checklist states the $900 “includes one cultivation site” and additional sites are “$900 per site,” paid separately by check/money order payable to CDFA Cashier. That per-site reading was not in Run 1 and may be a Santa Clara interpretation. Verify against current 3 CCR §4900 / the CDFA application form.
  • Term: one year from date of issuance by the commissioner (3 CCR §4900(a)(3)); renewed registration is valid one year from date of renewal issuance.
  • Renewal deadline: at least 30 calendar days prior to expiration — submit a new registration application + fee + supporting documents to the county commissioner (3 CCR §4901(e); CDFA renewal FAQ, Rev. 07/2023).
  • Late-renewal conflict — do not harmonise: 3 CCR §4900 text located in this run: if the renewal fee is not fully paid within 30 days after expiration, the registration is forfeited; a forfeited registration may be restored on payment of the unpaid fee plus a penalty of 15% of the unpaid amount. CDFA renewal FAQ (Rev. 07/2023): “There is no late fee associated with registration renewal at this time. However, any disruption in your registration may result in existing industrial hemp crops to be identified as noncompliant” and destroyed under 3 CCR §4950 if the crop does not meet FAC Division 24 / 3 CCR §4901. Verify which provision is current. Do not publish either as the sole late-renewal rule.
  • Amendments (from the CDFA grower application form, Rev. 05/23): changes to business name, contact information, or key participants must be reported within 15 calendar days (3 CCR §4901(b)(2)(A)); changes to cultivation site and/or cultivar must be requested prior to planting for commissioner approval (§4901(b)(2)(B)), on an Application to Amend. The form declaration also states: “The cultivation site(s) on this application is not on a premise that is licensed by the department to cultivate or process cannabis” — see §7.2.6.

7.2.4 Eligibility bars

From the CDFA FAQ, citing FAC §§81012–81014:

  • §81012: a registered grower, breeder, or EARI found committing three negligent violations in a five-year period is restricted from registration for five years beginning on the date of the finding of the third violation.
  • §81013: a person convicted of a felony relating to a controlled substance under state or federal law is restricted from registration for 10 years following the date of conviction.
  • §81014: a person who materially falsifies any information in an application or registration is restricted from registration. Whether the §81014 bar is time-limited: Verify on leginfo — the FAQ extract gives the restriction without a period.
  • County application practice (Fresno County, see §7.2.7) requires FBI criminal history reports for all key participants. Whether that is a statewide CDFA requirement or a county practice: Verify — do not state it as statewide law on the Fresno page alone.

7.2.5 Approved cultivars, seed, and research/breeding plans

  • Approved cultivars only: FAC §81002 requires registered growers to grow only varieties on the list of approved seed cultivars. The current list is in 3 CCR §4920, listing cultivars in FAC §81002(b). Verify the current §4920 list on the live CCR page before publishing any cultivar list.
  • Breeder: variety development plan required.
  • EARI: approved research plan required; EARI-grown/tested material under an approved research plan and breeder-grown/tested material under an approved variety development plan are excepted from the standard pre-harvest test rule in FAC §81006(e) — see §7.2.8.
  • FSA reporting: FAC §81004.6 requires each registered EARI, grower, and breeder to report hemp production in the state and any changes to production location to USDA Farm Service Agency. Cited in AB 8 analysis; Verify §81004.6 text.

7.2.6 No hemp on DCC cannabis premises — FAQ typo flagged

  • FAC §81006(c), as summarised in the FAQ and in a secondary reproduction of §81006: industrial hemp shall not be cultivated on premises licensed to cultivate or process cannabis; hemp, regardless of THC content, cultivated on a DCC-licensed cannabis cultivation premises is considered cannabis under BPC §26001 and subject to MAUCRSA licensing.
  • Flag: the FAQ wording in this run says §81006(c) “prohibits industrial hemp cultivation on premises licensed by CDFA to cultivate or process cannabis.” That “CDFA” appears to be a FAQ typo for DCC. Verify §81006(c) verbatim on leginfo and do not copy the FAQ typo into the final file.
  • Minimum acreage — secondary only, Verify: a reproduction of §81006 states industrial hemp shall be grown in acreages of not less than one-tenth of an acre at the same time, except when grown by a registered EARI or registered hemp breeder under an approved plan. Do not publish as confirmed until leginfo §81006 is opened.

7.2.7 County process — Fresno County as a worked example (not a statewide template)

Official page: Fresno County Agricultural Commissioner, Industrial Hemp, fresnocountyca.gov — Industrial Hemp — date accessed 1 November 2026. Fresno requires, in substance:

  • Register in the county of cultivation; all sites mapped; each cultivar approved prior to planting.
  • Seed/clones must have been produced in compliance with the state plan from the state of origin; COA showing parent plants tested at total THC ≤0.3% plus proof the producer was registered in its state of origin; or cultivars on the List of Certified Industrial Hemp Varieties for California may be cultivated.
  • Prior to harvest, all plantings sampled by county inspectors; samples sent to approved laboratories (see §7.2.8).
  • Harvest report to the county agricultural commissioner within 72 hours of completing harvest.
  • Grower/breeder registration packet: completed signed registration form; FBI criminal history reports for all key participants; for growers, COA and Grower Registration for each cultivar; variety development plan for breeders only; check/money order for $900 made out to CDFA Cashier, submitted to the Fresno County Department of Agriculture.
  • Fresno page notes requirements are “currently in flux” as CDFA implements/modifies regulations to comply with USDA rules — do not treat that stale flux note as a 2026 legal finding without a dated re-check.

Santa Clara County checklist (county document, secondary for statewide purposes): a reference tool only, not proof of registration; do not plant until registration is approved; planting before approval may result in crop destruction and violations for unpermitted cannabis cultivation. Change of registration: new application + fees required for any change in registrant name, county, or grower↔breeder change; lesser changes (business name, contact, primary contact, site, cultivar) are handled as a change request — Verify whether a fee applies to those lesser changes.

7.2.8 Sampling, testing, pass/fail, destruction — the measurement-of-uncertainty point is critical

Pre-harvest test required: FAC §81006(e) requires registrants, except EARI/breeder material under an approved plan, to obtain a laboratory test report indicating THC percentage before harvest of each crop, from random sampling of dried flowering tops.

  • Timing/method: sampling no more than 30 days before harvest; sampling requested and samples collected per 3 CCR §§4940–4941; tested per §§4942–4944. Pre-harvest report to the commissioner at least 30 days before harvest to initiate sampling (county/Fresno secondary summary — Verify against §§4940–4941).
  • THC calculation, 3 CCR §4890(a)(25), quoted in the CDFA FAQ: “THC concentration” or “percentage concentration of THC” means the post-decarboxylated value of delta-9 THC on a dry weight basis to the nearest thousand, or three decimal places, measured by either (1) suitable analytical instrumentation described in §4942(b) that results in decarboxylation of THC-acid to delta-9 THC, or (2) a calculated value using delta-9 THC plus 87.7 percent of THC-acid when instrumentation does not decarboxylate.
  • Acceptable hemp THC level / measurement of uncertainty, 3 CCR §4890(a) and §4944(b)(18), quoted in the FAQ: a THC concentration within the distribution/range that includes 0.3% or less when measurement of uncertainty is applied. FAQ example: reported 0.35% with uncertainty ±0.06% gives a range 0.29%–0.41%; because 0.3% is within the range, the sample is within the acceptable hemp THC level. A raw result above 0.3% does not automatically fail if the uncertainty range includes 0.3%. Do not present 0.3% as a hard laboratory cut-off without this definition.
  • Lab report wording, FAC §81006(e)(5) reproduction: report must be issued by a CDFA-approved laboratory using a department-approved testing method, using postdecarboxylation or similarly reliable methods; must indicate percentage THC on a dry-weight basis, date and location of samples, GPS coordinates and total acreage of the crop. If ≤0.3%, the words “PASSED AS CALIFORNIA INDUSTRIAL HEMP” shall appear at or near the top. If >0.3%, the words “FAILED AS CALIFORNIA INDUSTRIAL HEMP” shall appear at or near the top.
  • Destruction triggers, FAC §81006(e)(8) / 3 CCR destruction provisions (reproductions, Verify on leginfo/CCR):
    • First test >1% THC: destroy/dispose; destruction shall begin within 48 hours and be completed within seven days after receipt of the laboratory test report.
    • First test > acceptable level but ≤1%: registrant may request one additional retest per 3 CCR §4945.
    • Second test >0.3% but <1%: destroy/dispose as soon as practicable, but no later than 45 calendar days after receipt of the electronic copy of the second laboratory test report; commissioner shall issue a notice of abatement within 48 hours of receipt of the electronic copy.
    • Failed crop cannot be harvested.
  • Destruction methods, 3 CCR §4890 definition reproduced in search: “Destroy” or “dispose” means transitioning noncompliant product into a nonretrievable or noningestible form, including plowing/tilling/disking into soil; mulching, composting, chopping, or bush mowing into green manure; burning; burying and covering with soil. CDFA shall make a template destruction plan and destruction report available on its website. Destruction plan must be submitted to the county commissioner and approved prior to commencing destruction (Fresno summary — Verify against current 3 CCR). Run 1 directory notes cite 3 CCR §4950 for grower-performed destruction — Verify section number in this session; not re-verified here.
  • Record retention and distribution, FAC §81006(e)(10) reproduction: except for EARI, a registrant shall retain an original signed copy of the laboratory test report for two years from its date of sampling; make an original signed copy available to the department, commissioner, or law enforcement on request; and provide an original copy to each person purchasing, transporting, or otherwise obtaining fiber, oil, cake, seed, or any component of seed from the registrant.
  • Possession outside a field, FAC §81007 reproduction — Verify: except as provided in §81007(b) or as necessary for testing under §81006(f), possession outside a field of lawful cultivation of resin, flowering tops, or leaves removed from the hemp plant is prohibited; a de minimis amount / insignificant number of hemp leaves or flowering tops in hemp bales from normal and appropriate processing does not constitute possession of marijuana.

7.2.9 Pesticides, water, organic — do not conflate hemp with DCC cultivation

  • Pesticides: DPR provides statewide guidance for all agricultural crops including hemp; EPA has published a list of approved pesticides for hemp. No California hemp-specific pesticide list was opened — Verify DPR guidance page.
  • Water discharges: State Water Resources Control Board regulates discharges from agricultural operations including irrigation runoff, tile drains, storm water, via the Irrigated Lands Regulatory Program. This is distinct from the cannabis General Order in Part 4 — do not conflate hemp irrigation regulation with DCC cultivation water permits.
  • Organic: CDFA State Organic Program enforces federal Organic Foods Production Act and California Organic Food and Farming Act for labelling/selling hemp as organic — detail Verify with that programme.
  • Industrial Hemp Advisory Board: FAC §81001 establishes the board to advise CDFA on seed law/regulations, annual budgets, and assessment rate. FAQ in this run notes 2 vacancies at crawl time — do not publish as a durable fact without a dated check.

7.3 CDPH IHEO — registration vs authorisation (state this plainly)

Official pages opened: CDPH AB 8 FAQ; CDPH form CDPH 8610 IH (3/2026); CDPH form CDPH 8763 (3/2026); CDPH forms index — all date accessed 1 November 2026 (links in the headnote to this Part).

A manufacturer needs BOTH (a) the underlying commodity registration/licence AND (b) the IHEO authorisation for that commodity, per commodity and per location (Run 1 summary; 8763 confirms a new application is required if the firm has changed location or ownership).

CommodityBase formIHEO supplement / form
Processed food (incl. extract / human food)CDPH 8610 (base, 7/2025 extract)CDPH 8610 IH — and see duplicate question below
Human food with cannery licenceCDPH 8597 (base)CDPH 8597 IH
Processed pet foodCDPH 8676 (base)CDPH 8676 IH
CosmeticsCDPH 8678 (base registration)CDPH 8678 IH
Inhalables—CDPH 8763 is itself the IHEO authorisation form (3/2026)

Form-number discrepancy to resolve before publication: Run 1 and the commission list CDPH 8597/8610/8611/8676/8678/8763. The CDPH forms index lists CDPH 8610 IH (Extract and/or Human Food) and, separately, a base CDPH 8611 (Processed Food Registration, 7/2025 extract, with IHEO fee line “See CDPH 8611 IH”). Search extracts show a CDPH 8611 IH form with the same Extract/Human Food title and same human-food fee tiers as 8610 IH. Verify whether 8610 IH and 8611 IH are duplicate/parallel versions (e.g. in-state vs out-of-state, or a renumbering) or whether one supersedes the other. Do not list both as distinct authorisations without that check.

What every IHEO form requires (from opened forms):

  • Approving state/tribal agency and registration/licence information, with copy attached.
  • List of all current/proposed industrial hemp sources with documents showing approved source status, plus the source's state/tribal registration/licence number and issuing entity. A manufacturer using an unregistered source fails the application on its face.
  • Extract types (full-spectrum, broad-spectrum, concentrate, crude oil, other) and extraction methods — non-volatile: CO2, ethanol, food-grade butter/oil, glycerin, mechanical, water/food-grade dry ice; volatile: butane, pentane, hexane, other.
  • Up to three product labels (or all labels if fewer).
  • Gross annual revenue (GAR) tier for the IHEO fee — see §7.3.1.
  • Majority-owner signature; authorised representatives/signatories; notification to Food and Drug Branch immediately of any changes under HSC Division 104 Parts 5 and 6 (Sherman Law).
  • Misrepresentations/omissions may be grounds for denial, revocation, or suspension. Refusal to submit to inspection and violations under Sherman Law may be grounds for denial, suspension, and/or revocation and may be subject to other penalties (CDPH 8610 IH declaration).
  • Out-of-state manufacturers consent to Sherman Law and to CDPH inspection of manufacturing/holding/distributing sites and records.
  • Incomplete applications will be returned — stated on every IHEO form.
  • Own-extract rule: if a cosmetics or inhalable manufacturer manufactures its own extract, it must also register as an extract manufacturer (CDPH 8678 IH and 8763 instructions). Do not present cosmetics/inhalable IHEO as a substitute for extract IHEO where the firm extracts in-house.

7.3.1 IHEO fees — revenue-based, per commodity, IN ADDITION to the base fee

Do not present a single “IHEO fee.” GAR rule (form instructions): determine current or estimated GAR of the relevant industrial hemp commodity; if no revenue from that manufacturing, GAR must be based on fair market value if sold in an arm's-length wholesale transaction.

Category / formTier 1 (≤$100,000 GAR)Tier 5 ($3,000,001–$5,000,000)Top tier
Extract manufacturer, CDPH 8610 IH (3/2026, opened)$2,750$9,500>$50,000,000: $42,000

Processed pet food, CDPH 8676 IH (search extract, 3/2026)$1,300$3,600>$25,000,000: $9,000
