Full tier tables (from opened 8610 IH and 8763; pet food and cosmetics from search extracts — Verify full fee tables against the live PDFs before final publication):

  • Extract: T1 ≤$100k $2,750; T2 $100,001–$500k $3,500; T3 $500,001–$1.5m $5,000; T4 $1.5m–$3m $7,000; T5 $3m–$5m $9,500; T6 $5m–$10m $13,500; T7 $10m–$20m $18,500; T8 $20m–$30m $24,000; T9 $30m–$50m $32,000; T10 >$50m $42,000.
  • Human food: T1 $1,900; T2 $2,800; T3 $3,700; T4 $4,700; T5 $5,900; T6 $5m–$7.5m $7,100; T7 $7.5m–$12.5m $8,500; T8 $12.5m–$17.5m $9,900; T9 $17.5m–$25m $11,500; T10 >$25m $14,000 (same band breaks as extract, except T6–T10 bands differ — see form).
  • Pet food: T1 $1,300; T2 $2,000; T3 $2,500; T4 $3,000; T5 $3,600; T6 $4,300; T7 $5,200; T8 $6,200; T9 $7,400; T10 $9,000.
  • Cosmetics: T1 $1,600; T2 $2,400; T3 $3,000; T4 $3,600; T5 $4,300; T6 $5,200; T7 $6,200; T8 $7,400; T9 $8,800; T10 $10,500.
  • Inhalable: T1 $1,700; T2 $2,600; T3 $3,300; T4 $4,000; T5 $4,800; T6 $5,700; T7 $6,800; T8 $8,100; T9 $9,700; T10 $12,000.

Base registration fees (separate from IHEO, Verify current versions): Processed Food Registration (CDPH 8610 base, 7/2025 extract) payment codes A–M $524–$2,695 depending on employees/square footage, plus $100 Food Safety Fee, plus $250 additional fee where applicable. Pet food processor licence fee $190.00 (CDPH 8676, 7/2025), late fee $30 if over 30 days past expiration. Cosmetics registration fee $507 appears only on a 2011 CDPH 8678 form in search — do not use as a 2026 fee; Verify current CDPH 8678 base fee.

IHEO term / renewal / transfer — Verify: forms include “License Number / Expiration Date” fields and renewal-applicant boxes (8763 expressly). The exact IHEO term (e.g. annual, aligned to base registration) was not stated on the forms opened — Verify on CDPH IHEO FAQ / base registration instructions. Do not assume a 1-year term from the CDFA cultivation term. Transfer: CDPH 8763 instructions require a new application if the firm has changed location or ownership; for extract/human food, the form requires immediate notification of changes. That points to effectively non-transferable, but do not state it as a statutory rule without HSC §111921 et seq. text.

7.3.2 CDPH testing, purity and labelling rules that belong in Part 7

From the CDPH AB 8 FAQ (opened):

  • All industrial hemp raw extract must be tested by an independent laboratory for contaminants and cannabinoid content before inclusion in a product.
  • Industrial hemp products must be tested for the same contaminants as DCC cannabis products: pesticides, heavy metals, residual solvents, etc., to the same standards.
  • Raw extract used in food/food additives/beverages/dietary supplements shall not contain THC or synthetic cannabinoids and should be CBD or CBN isolate with purity >99%.
  • Purity determination: quantitative COA must confirm CBD or CBN exceeds 99% of total integrated chromatographic peaks of tested analytes.
  • Testing laboratory must be either DCC-licensed or ISO/IEC 17025-accredited by a competent third-party accrediting body.
  • Labelling under AB 45 (Run 1): labels/QR/barcode linking to COAs with THC content; warning that children and pregnant/breastfeeding persons should consult health professionals; statement “THE FDA HAS NOT EVALUATED THIS PRODUCT FOR SAFETY OR EFFICACY”; ban on untrue health-related statements. Verify current labelling sections after AB 8 amendments — Run 1 flagged HSC §111921.5 section number as Verify.
  • Consumable hemp food/beverage/supplement products: no detectable total THC per serving, 21+, ≤5 servings per package (DPH-24-005) — see §7.1.

7.3.3 CDPH IHEO authorised firms list — do not cite the mirror

  • FAQ hub links “List of the current authorized Industrial Hemp processors and manufacturers.”
  • Mirror copy in Run 1 search stated “current as of January 14, 2026,” 5 pages, fields: Business Name, City, State, License Number, Authorized For, Expiration Date; categories IH Food, Extractor In-State, Pet Food, Cosmetics, Cannery, Inhalables Out of State.
  • Do NOT cite the mirror URL as official. Exact official CDPH PDF URL/filename must be verified live from the FAQ link. A later 2026 edition after 14 January 2026: Verify.
  • Inclusion does not mean all products from a business have been approved — it is a registration/authorisation list, not a product approval list.

7.4 CDFA handling / Market Enforcement Branch — often missed

From the CDFA FAQ, Manufacturing/Processing/Sale section (opened):

  • Industrial hemp handlers, manufacturers, and processors are subject to FAC Division 20, governing processors, storers, dealers, and distributors of agricultural products.
  • FAC §55521: any person acting as a processor or cash-buying processor of industrial hemp must obtain a licence prior to engaging in such acts.
    • “Processor” (FAC §55407): a person engaged in processing/manufacturing any farm product who solicits, buys, contracts to buy, or otherwise takes title to, or possession/control of, any farm product from the producer for processing/manufacturing and selling/reselling/redelivering it in dried, canned, extracted, fermented, distilled, frozen, eviscerated, or other preserved/processed form. Excludes a retail merchant with a fixed/established place of business in California who does not sell at wholesale any farm product processed/manufactured by him.
    • “Cash buying processor” (FAC §55402.5): a person engaged in processing/manufacturing any farm product who obtains title/possession/control from a producer and buys/agrees to buy by cash payment of the full agreed price at the time of obtaining possession/control or contracting.
  • FAC §56181: any person engaged in buying, receiving on consignment, soliciting for sale on commission, or negotiating sale of industrial hemp from a licensee or producer for resale must obtain a licence. Includes brokers negotiating purchase/sale, cash buyers, commission merchants accepting in trust/receiving on consignment/selling on commission/handling as agent or co-signer, and dealers obtaining title/possession/control/delivery from a licensee/producer.
  • CDFA Market Enforcement Branch (MEB) is tasked with licensing, inspection, and enforcement for handling, manufacturing, processing, and selling hemp farm products for resale or processing, and “will begin accepting applications for licenses on January 1, 2021” per FAQ. That 2021 date is historical; Verify current MEB application process, fees, and whether MEB licences are still required alongside CDPH IHEO for the same activity — the FAQ does not resolve overlap, and Part 7 must not assume MEB and IHEO are mutually exclusive.
  • Farmers' markets: CDFA California Farmers' Market (CFM) Program has published FAQs on sale of hemp at certified farmers' markets. Detail Verify with CFM programme; do not assert a general farmers'-market hemp right.
  • Animal feed / livestock drugs: CDFA Feed, Fertilizer and Livestock Drugs Regulatory Services Branch (FFLDRS) enforces state law on manufacturing, distribution, labelling, and use of commercial livestock feed and livestock drugs, and has published a notice on hemp-derived ingredients. Detail Verify with FFLDRS; do not assert hemp in animal feed is generally lawful.

7.5 DCC hemp role — pre-2028 vs from 1 January 2028

From the CDPH AB 8 FAQ (opened) and AB 8 legislative analyses (Senate Floor Analysis, Third Reading; Assembly Floor Analysis, Concurrence — secondary legislative analyses, Verify against enrolled AB 8 text on leginfo):

7.5.1 Pre-2028 / current at 1 November 2026 cut-off

  • No change to IHEO requirement for manufacturing industrial hemp products or extracting cannabinoids from industrial hemp.
  • From 1 January 2026, industrial hemp extract used in food/food additives/beverages/dietary supplements/processed pet food cannot contain THC or any synthetic cannabinoids; COA must confirm CBD/CBN >99% of total integrated chromatographic peaks.
  • Cosmetics from 1 January 2026: extract cannot contain THC, except topically applied cosmetics may contain total THC ≤0.3% on a dry-weight basis. Cosmetics manufacturers need IHEO authorisation plus a cosmetic manufacturing registration from CDPH.
  • Inhalables from 1 January 2026: hemp flower, pre-rolls containing hemp flower or hemp-derived cannabinoids, and inhalable cannabis products containing THC derived from hemp cannot be sold, offered, or provided in California. Manufacturing inhalables for sole purpose of sale in other states is not prohibited; manufacturers must maintain IHEO authorisation with CDPH and products must be under 0.3% THC confirmed by COA; inhalable products can only be sold outside California. Firms warehousing/distributing hemp flower only are not required to obtain IHEO authorisation.
  • DCC page in Run 1 (opened): licensed manufacturers may only use cannabinoids from licensed sources; licensed distributors cannot transport hemp; licensed retailers cannot sell hemp products; licensed testing labs can test hemp as the law allows; DCC is developing regulations to allow licensee use of hemp; growing rules unchanged.
  • CDFA FAQ in this run: industrial hemp products cannot be sold in cannabis retail stores at this time.

7.5.2 From 1 January 2028 (AB 8 Phase 2)

  • Anyone extracting cannabinoids from industrial hemp will need to be licensed by DCC (CDPH AB 8 FAQ, verbatim in substance).
  • Manufacturers of food/food additives/beverages/dietary supplements/processed pet food that contain only CBD or CBN isolate and no other cannabinoid still need IHEO authorisation from CDPH. Extract used in those products still cannot contain THC or synthetic cannabinoids.
  • Inhalable manufacturers will be regulated by DCC and will no longer require IHEO from CDPH after 1 January 2028.
  • CDPH will continue to issue IHEO authorisations for foods (including extracts, food additives, beverages, dietary supplements), processed pet foods, cosmetics, and inhalables through 1 January 2028. After 1 January 2028, CDPH will only issue IHEO for foods, processed pet foods, and cosmetics.
  • CDPH does not plan to initiate rulemaking to implement or enforce AB 8 (FAQ, verbatim). DCC rulemaking is required for 2028 integration. Secondary law-firm summary (Shay Gilmore Law, 13 May 2026, secondary) states DCC budget documents confirm: “DCC will begin development of rulemaking to implement AB 8 in 2026–27 with expected completion in 2027–28, prior to the start of hemp retail and licensing requirements.” Verify against DCC budget/rulemaking page; do not present a DCC hemp licence type, fee, or application form as existing before that rulemaking.
  • AB 8 analysis summary (secondary, Verify on enrolled text):
    • Beginning 1 January 2028, products containing concentrated cannabinoids other than CBD isolate derived from industrial hemp must comply with MAUCRSA, including track-and-trace identification, advertising restrictions, security and transportation safety requirements, quality assurance standards, laboratory testing, and taxation.
    • A licensee is prohibited from possessing industrial hemp, as specified, until 1 January 2028.
    • Industrial hemp, upon entry into the licensed market, must be held in quarantine by the distributor and tested by a licensed testing laboratory to confirm it meets the definition of industrial hemp before being transferred to another licensee or incorporated into a cannabis product.
    • Industrial hemp or cannabis products derived exclusively from industrial hemp may be continuously transported through California without entering the licensed market, provided they are not sold in California, or shipped out of California by a licensee (wording in analysis is truncated — Verify).
    • AB 8 prohibits sale of synthetic cannabis products and inhalable cannabis products containing THC derived from hemp; permits topical cosmetics with THC not more than an amount determined by CDPH in regulation, not to exceed 0.3% total THC, to remain within the definition of industrial hemp products.
    • Provisions related to industrial hemp under Sherman Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Law are operative 1 January 2028, except standards for industrial raw hemp extract, prohibition on inhalable hemp products, and testing requirements to prove THC does not exceed CDPH limits, as determined by CDPH effective 1 January 2026.
    • AB 8 analysis also states it “requires out-of-state hemp manufacturers to register with the state” and “reverts the cannabis excise tax rate to 15 percent effective January 1, 2028” — the excise point is redundant after AB 564 (15% from 1 October 2025); the out-of-state registration point Verify against enrolled text and CDPH out-of-state IHEO rules.
  • What DCC hemp licences will be called, what they will cost, and what DCC licence type (6/7/N/P/S, 11, 10, 12, or a new hemp-specific type) an extractor/inhalable manufacturer must hold from 2028: Unavailable at 1 November 2026 cut-off. Part 7 must state this as a gap, not invent a “DCC hemp extraction licence” fee or form.
  • Tax from 2028: DCC page in Run 1: hemp products containing THC will have to pay the same taxes as medicinal and adult-use cannabis products from 1 January 2028. Verify whether that applies to all hemp-derived THC entering the DCC system or only to intoxicating hemp products as defined in AB 8.

7.6 Hemp seed sellers — no standalone hemp seed-seller licence located

  • No CDFA list of certified sellers: CDFA FAQ, opened, states verbatim in substance: “CDFA does not maintain a list of certified sellers of industrial hemp seed. For information on the certification of industrial hemp seed in California, contact the California Crop Improvement Association.”
  • Certifying agency: California Crop Improvement Association (CCIA), at UC Davis, is California's official seed certifying agency per CDFA FAQ and UC Agriculture and Natural Resources summaries. CCIA Industrial Hemp Seed Certification Standards are available via CCIA.
  • Approved cultivar pathways, California State Regulatory Plan for Hemp Production (USDA AMS PDF, search result: CAStateRegulatoryPlanforHempProduction.pdf — Verify current plan text):
    1. Seed/propagative material certified as breeder, foundation, registered, or certified seed/stock by (A) member organisations of Association of Official Seed Certifying Agencies (AOSCA), (B) Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD), or (C) an officially approved/recognised seed-certifying agency listed in 3 CCR §3875, as provided in FAC §52401.
    2. Seed/propagative material produced in a quality assurance programme approved by the same agencies.
    3. Seed/propagative material produced by an authorised participant in a state industrial hemp agricultural pilot programme under the 2014 Farm Act, or an authorised participant under an approved state/federal regulatory plan under the 2018 Farm Act, provided the crop from which seed/propagative material was harvested was tested by a method approved by the regulatory authority in the state of origin and found to have no more than 0.3% THC on a dry weight basis.
  • Certified seed standards, secondary UC ANR summary — Verify against CCIA standards: certified seed must meet minimum purity standards including at least 98% pure seed, no more than 0.10% weed seeds, minimum germination 80%. CCIA application fees in secondary CCIA page (effective 1 March 2024 in that extract): hemp minimum application fee $500; indoor production flat $1,000 per application; field production $500 per application plus $20/acre; late fee $125. Those CCIA fees are secondary and dated March 2024 — Verify current CCIA fee schedule; do not present as CDFA fees.
  • No standalone “hemp seed seller licence” was located in California law in this run. Selling seed is governed by (a) seed certification / approved cultivar rules, (b) FAC Division 24 registration if the seller also grows/breeds, and (c) general California seed law (FAC Division 18, seed labelling). Verify whether a seed dealer licence under FAC §52251 et seq. applies to hemp seed sellers; Part 7 must not invent a hemp-specific seed-seller licence.
  • CDFA does not import/distribute seeds for any crops, research or commercial (FAQ).

7.7 Hemp transport and hemp retail

7.7.1 Transport

  • CDFA lab-report-to-transporter rule, FAQ citing FAC §81006(e)(11): registrants must provide an original copy of the laboratory test report to each person transporting hemp, including hemp fiber, oil, cake, seed, or any component of seed. DMV and law enforcement may have additional requirements; contact CHP and DMV for vehicle transport.
  • No California-specific restriction on importation of unprocessed hemp material or hemp seeds/plants from another state was stated in FAQ, other than plant pest quarantines and agricultural inspection for plant pests. International importation is restricted under federal law — contact USDA. CDFA does not import or distribute seeds or planting materials.
  • DCC transport at cut-off: licensed distributors cannot transport hemp (DCC page, Run 1). Continuous transport through California without entering the licensed market is permitted on the AB 8 analysis wording (Verify — see §7.5.2).
  • USPS hemp mailing rules and CHP/DMV vehicle requirements beyond the lab-report rule: Verify current rules — not opened in this run.

7.7.2 Retail — general retail vs DCC retail

  • General retail at 1 November 2026 cut-off: only compliant hemp products may be sold: foods/beverages/supplements/pet food with >99% CBD/CBN extract, no THC, no synthetics, IHEO-registered manufacturer, independently tested, labelled per HSC Chapter 9 / CDPH rules; cosmetics with no THC except topical ≤0.3% total THC; no hemp flower, no hemp pre-rolls, no inhalable hemp-THC products for sale in California; no detectable total THC per serving in consumable hemp food/beverage/supplement products under DPH-24-005; 21+ purchase age for industrial hemp final-form products intended for human consumption; ≤5 servings per package.
  • DCC retail at 1 November 2026 cut-off: industrial hemp products cannot be sold in DCC-licensed cannabis retail stores (CDFA FAQ). DCC retailers cannot sell hemp products in the interim (DCC page, Run 1). From 1 January 2028, intoxicating hemp products enter the DCC system and will be sold through DCC licensees under MAUCRSA, with track-and-trace, testing, packaging/labelling, advertising, security, transport, and taxation — exact retail licence type for hemp products in 2028: Verify after DCC rulemaking.
  • Out-of-state / interstate retail: manufacturing inhalables solely for out-of-state sale is not prohibited (CDPH FAQ). Sale into California from out of state must still meet California product rules (no detectable THC/serving, >99% CBD/CBN, etc.) — Verify CDPH out-of-state manufacturer IHEO / registration requirements for firms shipping into California; the IHEO forms expressly contemplate out-of-state manufacturers consenting to Sherman Law and CDPH inspection.

7.8 Tribal hemp plans — cross-reference to Part 10 only

  • Run 1 §5.15 finding, carried forward: No central public tribal cannabis-licence list was located from an official tribal or state source.
  • USDA AMS approved hemp plans list is the only official tribal-plan anchor: AMS state and tribal plan review, updated 17 February 2026 in search text. California row: “CA Approved Plan (pdf)”, approved under final rule effective 22 March 2021; CDFA effective date 1 January 2022 controls for California operations.
  • New in this run (AMS search extract, updated 17 February 2026): the extract shows, in the tribal table, Cahuilla Band of Indians — Approved Plan (pdf)*, Pala Band of Mission Indians — Approved Plan (pdf), Santa Rosa Band of Cahuilla Indians — Approved Plan (pdf)* (* = approved under final rule effective 22 March 2021). The extract is truncated and does not show the full tribal table in this run.
  • Older secondary lists add, but do not verify: La Jolla Band of Luiseño Indians, Torres Martinez Desert Cahuilla Indians, Yurok Tribe, Soboba Band of Luiseño Indians (500Nations table last updated 20 February 2021; Hemp Gazette, 28 January 2020, naming La Jolla, Santa Rosa, Yurok). All beyond the three AMS-extract names above: Verify against the current full USDA AMS table before publishing any tribal hemp list.
  • Secondary tribal-market reporting (April–October 2025/2026, secondary) claims California ranks second nationally with 15 tribally owned stores operated by 12 tribes, naming Twenty-Nine Palms Band of Mission Indians (Red Falcon), Iipay Nation of Santa Ysabel (Mountain Source), Sycuan Band of the Kumeyaay Nation (Sessions by the Bay), Tule River Tribe (Eh-Lo Dispensary) — none is an official tribal or DCC record and must not be presented as a verified tribal list in Part 10, and only as a cross-reference in Part 7.
  • Part 7 action: check each named tribe's own government website / tribal code for an ordinance, and USDA AMS for a hemp plan. Do not build a tribal hemp table from secondary directories.
  • Tribal Schedule III “state-licensed” question remains Verify for any tribal medical programme.

7.9 Application steps — consolidated checklists

(i) CDFA grower

  1. Choose county of cultivation; check county/city local restrictions with the county agricultural commissioner.
  2. Select only §4920 approved cultivars; obtain seed/clone COA and state-of-origin registration proof, or use a certified variety.
  3. Complete CDFA Grower Registration Application (Rev. 05/23); attach FBI criminal history reports for key participants where the county requires them; pay $900 to CDFA (plus any county actual-cost fee).
  4. Submit to the county agricultural commissioner before planting; do not plant until registration is approved.
  5. Report any business/contact/key-participant change within 15 calendar days; request site/cultivar changes prior to planting.
  6. Pre-harvest: report to commissioner at least 30 days before harvest to initiate sampling; sampling ≤30 days before harvest; obtain lab report; retain original signed copy 2 years; give original copy to each purchaser/transporter.
  7. Harvest report to commissioner within 72 hours (Fresno practice — Verify statewide).
  8. Renew: new application + $900 at least 30 calendar days before expiration, in each county of cultivation.

(ii) CDFA breeder

As (i), plus variety development plan; breeder fee $900; breeder-grown/tested material under an approved variety development plan is excepted from the standard FAC §81006(e) pre-harvest test rule.

(iii) EARI

As (i), under FAC §81004.5, plus approved research plan; EARI-grown/tested material under an approved research plan is excepted from the standard pre-harvest test rule. Fee treatment for EARI: Verify — the $900 fee text in §4900 is stated for growers and seed breeders.

(iv) CDPH extract / human food manufacturer

  1. Obtain base Processed Food Registration (CDPH 8610) and/or cannery licence (CDPH 8597) as applicable.
  2. File IHEO supplement (CDPH 8610 IH / 8611 IH — resolve duplicate, see §7.3; CDPH 8597 IH for cannery) with approved-source list and documents, extract types/methods, labels, GAR tier fee.
  3. Use only independently tested raw extract (DCC-licensed or ISO/IEC 17025 lab); for consumables, CBD/CBN isolate >99%, no THC, no synthetics.
  4. Label per HSC Chapter 9 / CDPH rules with COA link; notify Food and Drug Branch immediately of changes.

(v) CDPH pet food manufacturer

Base CDPH 8676 (licence fee $190.00 in 7/2025 extract) + CDPH 8676 IH (GAR tiers in §7.3.1). Same approved-source, testing, and labelling discipline as (iv).

(vi) CDPH cosmetics manufacturer

Base CDPH 8678 + CDPH 8678 IH (GAR tiers in §7.3.1). If manufacturing own extract, also register as extract manufacturer. Topical cosmetics: total THC ≤0.3% dry weight permitted; all other cosmetics: no THC.

(vii) CDPH inhalable manufacturer — pre-2028, out-of-state sale only

CDPH 8763 IHEO authorisation (GAR tiers in §7.3.1). Products must be <0.3% THC confirmed by COA. No sale, offer, or provision in California. From 1 January 2028, inhalable manufacture moves to DCC regulation and no longer requires CDPH IHEO — licence type/fees Unavailable pending DCC rulemaking.

7.10 Enforcement and penalties

Confirmed:

  • CDPH: may embargo adulterated foods and cosmetics, issue penalties, and suspend or revoke registrations and authorisations (AB 8 FAQ, verbatim in substance). Misrepresentations/omissions on IHEO applications may be grounds for denial, revocation, or suspension (form declarations). Refusal to submit to inspection and violations under Sherman Law may be grounds for denial, suspension, and/or revocation and may be subject to other penalties (CDPH 8610 IH declaration).
  • AB 8 intoxicating hemp as cannabis: “AB 8 generally treats intoxicating hemp products as cannabis. As such, anyone manufacturing or selling intoxicating hemp products in California in violation of the law may be subject to the same civil and criminal penalties that exist for illegal cannabis manufacturing and sales.” (AB 8 FAQ, verbatim in substance.) From 1 January 2026, state/local agencies have new enforcement powers including taking products off shelves and issuing fines (DCC page, Run 1).
  • CDFA cultivation: crop destruction on failed tests (see §7.2.8); registration bars for negligent violations, felony convictions, and falsification (see §7.2.4); county commissioner inspection/sampling/enforcement as local authority.
  • Complaint route: CDPH Consumer Complaint form for non-compliant AB 8 / hemp products (AB 8 FAQ). Public programme contacts only: CDPH Food and Drug Branch, AB 8 questions: FDBIH@cdph.ca.gov, subject “AB 8”; DCC AB 8 information: info@cannabis.ca.gov.

Not confirmed — list as Verify/Unavailable in Part 7:

  • CDPH penalty schedule (dollar amounts per violation, per day) for IHEO / Sherman Law hemp violations.
  • CDFA administrative penalty / revocation procedure for hemp registrations beyond the FAC §§81012–81014 bars. Do not import DCC fine amounts ($5,000 licensed / $30,000 unlicensed per violation/day from Part 5) into hemp cultivation.
  • Whether a CDFA registration or CDPH IHEO authorisation can be transferred on sale of the business, or whether a new application is always required (CDPH 8763 points to new application on location/ownership change; CDFA county checklist points to new application + fees for registrant name/county/grower-breeder change — Verify against FAC/HSC transfer provisions).
  • DCC hemp enforcement procedure pre-2028 (DCC cannot yet license hemp, but can enforce against intoxicating hemp as illegal cannabis — exact DCC authority cited in AB 8: Verify).
  • Federal enforcement interaction after 11 December 2026 (total-THC definition, 0.4 mg/container cap, DEA-registered lab testing delayed to 31 December 2026): no California-specific guidance located — Verify after 11 December 2026.

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.0 Headline: ZERO strict-verified properties on current evidence

ZERO properties meet all three prongs, current at the 1 November 2026 cut-off, from official sources opened: (a) a current DCC licence or current local cannabis permit evidenced from an official source, AND (b) zoning / commercial cannabis use evidenced from a county/city code, permit record, or official listing, AND (c) a verifiable address/parcel or listing source opened.

Why “current” matters — the interpretation applied here, stated so the reader can check it: the commission says “a current DCC licence or local cannabis permit.” This report applies “current” to both limbs. A 2023 county CUP or a May 2023 city regulatory list, without a 2026 confirmation that the permit/licence is still in force for the same operator at the same premises, does not pass prong (a) as “current.”

On a looser, historic-permit-only reading, three candidates would be the closest tier-2 entries — Emerald Mountain Coast (Humboldt CUP, 2023), Desert Hot Springs Green Horizons / Desert Penguin at 13300 Little Morongo Rd (city regulatory permit, May 2023, plus a 2023 CEQA addendum under a new project name), and Desert Hot Springs Wellness Services Inc DBA “The Micro Buddery” at 66321 Pierson Blvd (city regulatory permit, May 2023). They are listed below as closest candidates, not as verified properties, precisely because the current-DCC / current-permit cross-check was not completed for any of them. Do not upgrade any of them without that cross-check.

For-sale status is separate: even a property passing (a)+(b)+(c) would not automatically be “for sale” — sale/listing status requires a current listing source opened, with price and date, stated separately. None of the Humboldt county-permit candidates had a sale listing opened; none of the Desert Hot Springs sale listings had official licence/zoning proof opened to 2026 currency. That gap is structural, not accidental.

8.1 How cannabis zoning works in California

8.1.1 State law does not zone land; local control is the gate

  • BPC §26200(a)(1) preserves local authority to adopt/enforce local ordinances to regulate licensed businesses, including local zoning and land-use requirements, business licence requirements, and requirements related to reducing exposure to secondhand smoke, or to completely prohibit one or more licence types within the local jurisdiction, except as set forth in the Medicinal Cannabis Patients’ Right of Access Act, BPC §§26320–26325 (SB 1186, 2022; medicinal delivery prohibition operative from 1 January 2024).
  • Dual authorisation is the zoning gate: DCC will deny a licence if the local jurisdiction has told DCC the proposed activity is prohibited there (BPC §26055 — secondary summaries; Verify §26055 text on leginfo). Local authorisation (permit, licence, or other authorisation) plus zoning/land-use compliance in the jurisdiction where the premises sit is a precondition to a DCC licence. If local authorisation is revoked, §26200(c) requires local notice to DCC and DCC to begin suspension/revocation process within 60 days.
  • County vs city: county rules apply only to unincorporated parts of the county, not to incorporated cities within the county. Incorporated cities set their own rules; they do not need to align with the county. City and County of San Francisco is consolidated — do not split.
  • Same-name places: watch Pasadena, Glendale, Auburn, Burbank, Concord, Richmond, Hayward, Ontario CA vs Ontario Canada, Alhambra, Santa Cruz, Eureka, Visalia, Fresno, Salinas, etc. Los Angeles city vs Los Angeles County unincorporated must be kept apart.

8.1.2 Statewide default buffer — correction to Run 1 framing

Run 1 framing said no single statewide buffer in DCC text opened, except advertising restrictions. That framing is incomplete and must be corrected in Run 2:

BPC §26054(b), as reproduced in FindLaw (secondary reproduction of the code — Verify verbatim on leginfo) and in San Diego County / Mendocino County official pages:

“A premises licensed under this division shall not be located within a 600-foot radius of a school providing instruction in kindergarten or any grades 1 through 12, daycare center, or youth center that is in existence at the time the license is issued, unless the department or a local jurisdiction specifies a different radius. The distance specified in this section shall be measured in the same manner as provided in subdivision (c) of Section 11362.768 of the Health and Safety Code unless otherwise provided by law.”
  • The 600-foot rule is therefore a statewide default floor, not a ceiling: a local jurisdiction may specify a different radius, and many do (see §8.1.4). It applies to licensed premises generally, not only to storefront retail, on the text as reproduced.
  • Do not publish “600 feet” as if it were the only buffer, and do not publish a local larger buffer as if it were statewide law. Always state: state default 600 ft, local radius as specified by that jurisdiction.

8.1.3 DCC premises rules (state layer, not zoning)

  • 4 CCR §15000 definition, reproduced: “Premises” means the designated structure(s) and land specified in the application that is owned, leased, or otherwise held under the control of the applicant or licensee where the commercial cannabis activity will be or is conducted. The premises shall be a contiguous area and shall only be occupied by one licensee.
  • 4 CCR §15000.1: every person conducting commercial cannabis activity shall obtain and maintain a valid licence from DCC for each separate premises at which activity is conducted.
  • 4 CCR §15006 Premises Diagram: applicant shall submit a complete, detailed, to-scale diagram showing property boundaries and proposed premises, all boundaries, entrances/exits, interior partitions, walls, rooms, windows, doorways, principal activity, dimensions, commercial cannabis activities in each area, limited-access areas, camera locations/numbers (unless exempt), and, for cultivation, all roads and water crossings, all water sources labelled by beneficial use type, water diversion/groundwater/rain-catchment locations with geographic coordinates, canopy areas with dimensions in feet and aggregate square footage (including noncontiguous areas, shelving levels, seed/R&D canopy), designated harvested-cannabis storage, shared areas (only pesticide/agricultural chemical storage, composting, secured cannabis waste areas, if contiguous and shown on each application), and common-use areas (hallways, bathrooms, breakrooms). If premises is only a portion of a property, diagram must label which part is the premises and what the remainder is used for; if two or more licensed premises share a property, diagram must show designated entrances/walls under exclusive control and all common/shared areas.
  • 4 CCR §15000.3 (2022 consolidated text, secondary reproduction — Verify current text): licensed premises shall not be in a location requiring persons to pass through a business selling alcohol/tobacco or a private residence to access it; shall not require passing through the licensed premises to access such a business or residence; shall not include living areas of a private residence (bathrooms, bedrooms, kitchens, living rooms) unless living areas are required by the local jurisdiction to be included, in which case they must be designated on the diagram but no commercial cannabis activity may occur within them.
  • DCC regulations text as of 1 October 2026 (unofficial consolidated text): DCC Text of Regulations — Westlaw hosts the official CCR, Title 4 Division 19.
  • A DCC search hit alone does not prove zoning. DCC search publishes business name + licence number/type/status + premises location, but no APN, no zoning designation, and no planning permit number in the explainer text.

8.1.4 Zones typically used (from Part 3 local rules, not a statewide zoning code)

  • Agricultural (AG-I, AG-II, AE) and Timber Production Zone (TPZ) for outdoor/mixed-light cultivation in Humboldt, Mendocino, Trinity, Santa Barbara County.
  • Light Industrial (I-L), Industrial, Commercial, Mixed-Use Corridor, Visitor-Serving Mixed-Use for indoor cultivation, manufacturing, distribution, and retail in Desert Hot Springs, Santa Ana, Costa Mesa, and other cities.
  • Commercial (CC, CR, CG, IL, MAP-1 in El Dorado County) for Type 10 storefront retail in unincorporated El Dorado County (South Lake Tahoe area), 8 a.m.–8 p.m. daily per secondary summary of County Code Title 130 Chapters 130.41/130.42.
  • Residential zones are generally excluded for commercial cannabis, except personal-use home grow (HSC §11362.2: localities may regulate/ban outdoor home grows but may not completely prohibit indoor private-residence grows) and, in some counties, cultivation on residential-agricultural lots subject to setbacks and permits — Verify per jurisdiction; do not generalise.

8.1.5 Buffer / setback variation by city/county — all local, except the §26054(b) default above

  • Santa Barbara County, Agriculture-II Zone (official county page, search result: Santa Barbara County Agriculture-II Zone): cannabis use prohibited within 750 feet from a sensitive receptor; within Coastal Zone per BPC (not in ordinance); Land Use Permit required prior to commencement; Conditional Use Permit required if cultivation is on lots in/adjacent to an Existing Developed Residential Neighborhood (EDRN) or requires use of a roadway within an EDRN as sole access, or on lots adjacent to an EDRN and/or Urban-Rural boundary; valid county annual business licence + valid state annual licence required; compliance with Cannabis Waste Discharge Requirements General Order required.
  • Santa Barbara County Code Chapter 50 (Municode, search result): no outdoor cultivation, nurseries, or microbusinesses with outdoor cultivation in the coastal zone; no outdoor cultivation within two miles of the urban-rural boundary or city boundary in the coastal zone; caps: no more than 134 acres in Area A and Area B of the Carpinteria Agricultural Overlay District (Ordinance 4529) at any one time, plus 1,417 acres in the unincorporated area outside the overlay; retail storefront operation permits capped at 8 in the county (secondary summary of §50-7(b) — Verify).
  • Humboldt County CMMLUO (HCC §314-55.4, from Legistar staff reports): cultivation of 10,000 sq ft to 1 acre permitted in areas zoned AE and TPZ with a Conditional Use Permit (§314-55.4.8.2.2); up to 43,560 sq ft outdoor and 22,000 sq ft mixed-light on parcels larger than 5 acres subject to CUP and a determination cultivation was in existence prior to 1 January 2016. Setbacks in §314-55.4.11.d were referenced but not quoted — Verify full setback table before publishing Humboldt setbacks.
  • Santa Ana: 600-foot separation from schools; separation between retailers (PHI report card, secondary).
  • Costa Mesa: 1,000-foot separation from schools/parks/youth areas in code text returned; 600-foot figure in one comparative exhibit conflicts — Verify current §9-486.
  • El Dorado County: cultivation setback 1,500 feet from schools/bus stops/places in the county application form (secondary summary — Verify).
  • San Diego County (new in this run): County Engage page states the state defines sensitive uses as schools (K-12), day cares, and youth centers and requires a 600-foot radius setback unless the local jurisdiction specifies a different distance (citing BPC §26054(b)); Board direction 27 January 2021 and Board action 19 August / 2 September 2026 adopting the Socially Equitable Cannabis Program are described on that page — Verify current San Diego County ordinance text before citing San Diego as a permissive jurisdiction for new facilities, as the page also states only five existing facilities in the unincorporated area were allowed under the prior ordinances and new facilities depend on the adopted programme.

8.2 CEQA / Water Board / Fish & Wildlife premises constraints (from Part 4)

A property that is zoned correctly can still fail on environmental grounds — zoning alone does not prove a DCC licence will issue:

  • Annual DCC licence requires CEQA compliance; lead agency is the local jurisdiction if its permitting is discretionary, DCC if local process is ministerial/CEQA-exempt.
  • CDFW Lake and Streambed Alteration Agreement (Fish & Game Code §1602) or written verification none is required.
  • State Water Board Cannabis Cultivation Policy adopted 5 February 2019, OAL approved 16 April 2019; General Order WQ 2019-0001-DWQ revised to WQ 2023-0102-DWQ; setbacks Class I watercourse (perennial) 150 ft, Class II (intermittent/wetland) 100 ft, Class III (ephemeral) 50 ft; SIUR up to 6.6 acre-feet/year, diversion season 1 November–31 March, dry-season forbearance.
  • Water Board General Order enrolment is a required step to obtain a DCC cultivation licence; SIUR / water rights are separate from General Order enrolment.

8.3 Where to search officially

SourceWhat it provesWhat it does NOT prove
DCC Licence Search — explainer search-for-licensed-business; tool search.cannabis.ca.gov — updated daily per DCC explainer; fields: business/legal name, licence number (e.g. C10-… retailer, C11-… distributor), type, status, premises locationCurrent DCC licence, type, status, premises location — if the search is run and the hit is recorded with dateAPN, zoning designation, planning permit number, sale status
DCC “Where cannabis businesses are allowed” map — where-cannabis-businesses-are-allowed — February 2026 update; filters storefront retail / delivery retail / distribution / testing / manufacturing / cultivation; no consumption/microbusiness/event filter; no CSV/XLSX download visibleWhether a jurisdiction allows a type, as a finding aidZoning of a specific parcel, or a specific property's permit
County assessor / GIS / parcel viewer (each county's own portal)APN, zoning designation, general plan designation, lot size, ownership (public record)That a cannabis permit or DCC licence exists
City/county planning portals and Legistar/agenda systems — e.g. Humboldt Legistar, Santa Barbara County CEO cannabis permits pages, El Dorado Legistar, Long Beach Legistar, Sacramento GranicusCUP / Special Permit / Land Use Permit, acreage/square footage, zone, conditionsCurrency in 2026, or a DCC licence — a 2021–2023 CUP without a current DCC check fails prong (a) as “current”
County Treasurer-Tax Collector / cannabis tax pagesThat a business is paying local cannabis taxLicence or zoning proof
Broker / MLS / LoopNet / Crexi / Realmo listingsMarketing only: address, APN, price, claimed CUP/licenceNothing on their own — even where a listing states a CUP number, APN, zoning, or licence type, it fails the strict standard unless the CUP/licence is independently opened on an official county/city or DCC source and is current. Listings stating “Buyer to independently verify” or “information obtained from sources believed reliable, not verified, no guarantee” fail on their own terms

Method limit — why ZERO is the honest headline: the strict standard requires convergence of three independent official records (current DCC licence or current local permit + zoning + address/parcel) for the same operator and same premises at the same time (2026). No single official source joins all three. Joining them requires a manual cross-check per property (DCC search by business name → county Legistar/planning by APN → assessor by APN), with dates recorded. That cross-check was not completed for any candidate in this run because DCC search results did not render as text in the searches used, and county Legistar extracts did not include DCC numbers. The final Part 8 report must either (a) complete that cross-check for at least the Emerald Mountain Coast candidate and report the DCC result honestly, or (b) state ZERO strict-verified properties with the candidate table below and the method limit stated. This file does (b). It must not lower the standard to “broker says licensed” or “county CUP in 2023” to reach a non-zero count. A live-browser DCC search is the required next step — see G8-1.

8.4.1 Candidate 1 — Emerald Mountain Coast LLC, Humboldt County — CLOSEST, FAILS ON (a) AS CURRENT

  • What was checked: Humboldt County Legistar File #23-1473, Planning Commission approved 2 November 2023: Legistar File #23-1473
  • Address/parcel (prong c — PASSES on its own): 4054 Cloud Crossing Rd, Blue Lake, CA 95525; APN 522-021-009-000; Record No. PLN-11291-CUP; Willow Creek area, terminus of Cloud Crossing Road, approx. 4.05 miles north from intersection of Old Three Creeks Road and Cloud Crossing Road.
  • Zoning / local permit (prong b — PASSES on its own, historic): Conditional Use Permit for 18,050 sq ft mixed-light commercial cannabis cultivation supported by 1,805 sq ft ancillary nursery. Site designated Timberland (T) in Humboldt County 2017 General Plan and zoned Timber Production Zone (TPZ), under HCC §314-55.4 CMMLUO. Lower Trinity River Watershed cap: 169 permits / 58 acres; with approval, 59 permits / 26.22 acres. Estimated annual irrigation water use 180,500 gallons from groundwater well + proposed rainwater catchment; storage 51,400 gallons in tanks + proposed 500,000-gallon rain catchment pond. CDFW issued a Final Lake and Streambed Alteration Agreement for restoration of wetlands/stream impacted by unpermitted cultivation; CDFW August 2023 letter notes compliance questions (slash pile in SMA, cultivation in proposed restoration area, synthetic netting) — conditions, not a revocation, on the extract.
  • DCC licence (prong a — FAILS as current): No current DCC licence number, DCC search hit, or DCC licence status for Emerald Mountain Coast LLC at this address was opened in this run. A 2023 county CUP does not prove a current (2026) DCC annual licence, renewal, or that the operator still holds the premises. To pass, Part 8 must run the DCC search for this business name/address and record licence number, type, status, and search date. Until then: FAILS strict standard.
  • Other caveats: CUP approval is 2023; conditions of approval, CEQA addendum, and watershed cap compliance must be re-checked for currency; no sale/listing price or availability was established — do not present as “for sale.”

8.5 What would have to change for a non-zero count

For a future update, a property may be added as strict-verified only when all of the following are recorded with dates:

  1. DCC Licence Search hit (business legal name, licence number, type, Active status, premises address) or a current (2026) city/county regulatory permit list row for the same holder/address, with the list's update date;
  2. County/city code or permit record showing the base zone and that cannabis use is permitted there (not merely that the base zone is I-L or TPZ);
  3. Address and APN verified against assessor/GIS or the planning record (not broker-only);
  4. Sale/listing status, if claimed, from a current listing opened, with price and date, stated separately from (1)–(3).

The single most efficient next check is the DCC search for: Emerald Mountain Coast LLC; Vesuvio Gardens LLC; Big River Farm LLC; Desert Hot Springs Green Horizons Inc / 2 Mouths DHS LLC; Desert Hot Springs Wellness Services Inc (Micro Buddery); Ceres Farm LLC (Santa Barbara). If any returns Active with a matching premises address, that property — and only that property — becomes the sole verified entry, with licence number/type/status/search date quoted.

Date accessed: 1 November 2026. Method caveat, stated plainly: most Parts 9–10 material rests on search extracts and secondary sources, NOT live page opens — a re-open attempt for the USDA AMS plan list, DCC About page, CCAP About page and CAC page returned an upstream browser 429 rate-limit and was not repeated; that failure does not prove those pages unavailable in a live browser. CORRECTION TO RUN 1 — READ THIS FIRST. Run 1 said no Attorney General notification under BPC §26308 was located. Parts 9–10 located AG Rob Bonta's 19 December 2023 opinion (36-page letter to DCC; DCC request 27 January 2023; opinion number/full text not verified — Verify on oag.ca.gov): conclusion YES, significant legal risk under the Controlled Substances Act (federal preemption risk, prosecution risk for state employees, no court has considered a preemption challenge to interstate-sales authorisation). Consequence: §26308(a)(4) trigger is NOT satisfied; no interstate agreement in effect remains true, but the reason is now documented. Sources are secondary reports only: Omar Figueroa — AG opinion report; Marijuana Moment, 20 December 2023. See also Part 10, §10.4.

Removal text conflict: older 2017 text vs 2026 extract showing §26040(b) “removed by appointing authority” and amendments operative 1 July 2026 — Verify on leginfo. 9.4 Timeline — Proposition 215 to 2026

This timeline adds to, and reconciles with, the statutes in Part 1. Bill/chapter/effective dates are as stated in Run 1 unless flagged Verify below. Confirm final status of every bill on leginfo, including vetoes.

  • 1996 — Proposition 215, Compassionate Use Act (5 November 1996, passed — secondary figures conflict: 5,382,915 / 55.6% vs 55.58% vs “56%” — adopt the Secretary of State Statement of Vote figure only; see §9.5). HSC §11362.5.
  • 2003 — SB 420, Medical Marijuana Program Act (HSC §§11362.7–11362.83): MMIC programme, safe-harbour quantities, primary caregiver rules. People v. Kelly (2010) limits the safe harbour — see §9.6.
  • 2010 — SB 1449: possession of under 1 oz → civil infraction ≤$100 (secondary, Verify).
  • 2010 — Proposition 19 failed — no percentage located, do not invent.
  • 2016 — Proposition 64, Adult Use of Marijuana Act (8 November 2016): Yes 7,979,041 (57.13%) vs No 5,987,020 (42.87%) per IGS/Wikipedia citing the Secretary of State; carried 39 of 58 counties (CaNORML); “56%” variant conflicts — adopt 57.13% with SOS citation (see §9.5).
  • 2017 — MAUCRSA (SB 94, Medicinal and Adult-Use Cannabis Regulation and Safety Act), Business and Professions Code Division 10.
  • 2019 — AB 97: provisional licence role — Verify.
  • 2021 — AB 141 consolidation: DCC becomes sole commercial cannabis licensing authority from 1 July 2021. AB 45 (Ch. 576, Stats. 2021): industrial hemp products under CDPH / Sherman Law; IHEO created. SB 544 (Ch. 547, Stats. 2021): DCC to establish standardised cannabinoid test methods by 1 January 2023 (per AB 1027 analysis).
  • 2022 — SB 1186 (Ch. 395, Stats. 2022): Medicinal Cannabis Patients' Right of Access Act, BPC §§26320–26325, operative 1 January 2024 — medicinal delivery cannot be banned in a way prohibiting timely accessible delivery. SB 1326 (Caballero, Ch. 396, Stats. 2022): interstate commerce framework, BPC §§26080, 26190, §§26300–26308 — dormant; see Part 10. AB 2188 (signed 18 September 2022) and SB 700 (signed 7 October 2023), both effective 1 January 2024: employment protections (Gov. Code §12954).
  • 2023 — SB 622 (Ch. 496): batch tagging. AG opinion 19 December 2023 on interstate commerce: significant legal risk — see the correction box at the head of this Part.
  • 2024 — AB 1775 (Haney, signed 30 September 2024, effective 1 January 2025): local jurisdictions may authorise on-site consumption cafés (BPC §26200). CDPH emergency regulations DPH-24-005 effective 23 September 2024: hemp food/beverages/supplements — no detectable THC per serving, 21+, ≤5 servings/package. AB 2223 (2024) died.
  • 2025 — AB 8 (Aguiar-Curry, Ch. 248, Stats. 2025, signed 2 October 2025, effective 1 January 2026): intoxicating hemp regulated as cannabis; preserves CBD/CBN access. AB 564 (Stats. 2025, ch. 127, signed 22 September 2025): excise tax returned to 15% effective 1 October 2025. SB 378 (signed): online marketplace strict liability. AB 1103 (signed): research. Vetoed: AB 632, AB 1332 (M-microbusiness direct shipment). Verify: AB 1397 (proposed 10% hemp-drink tax — NOT law). CDPH regular hemp rulemaking OAL-approved 20 October 2025.
  • 2026 — AB 2532 (Ch. 949, Stats. 2026, approved 30 September 2026): Poison Help line on edible/beverage labels, multi-serving beverage notice from 1 July 2027 — digest only opened, Verify full text. DCC emergency rule DCC-2026-03-E (4 June 2026): dual-designation retailer may hold two separate licences on same premises in two different legal entities; emergency expires 2 December 2026; permanent DCC-2026-04-R noticed 30 October 2026, comments due 14 December 2026, hearing 15 December 2026. Emergency text (4 CCR §§15000.1(e), 15000.2(b)–(c), 15023.1) NOT opened — Verify verbatim. AB 2506 (Hart) pending — see Part 10. AB 1826 (Lackey, introduced 11 February 2026, amended 19 March 2026) pending — see Part 11.

Errors to EXCLUDE: a StateRegsToday claim that “Proposition 207” (2020) allowed California on-site consumption is wrong — Proposition 207 is Arizona's; California authority is AB 1775 (2024) + local opt-in. A Justia Riverside extract misprints “Prop. 15” for Prop 215 — do not copy.

9.6 Key cases

  • People v. Kelly, 47 Cal.4th 1008 (2010) — decision date conflict in secondaries: 21 January 2010 vs 10 January 2010, Verify filed date; correct gloss: MMP limits are a floor for arrest protection, not a ceiling on the Compassionate Use Act defence; reject overbroad “no legal limits” gloss. SB 420 HSC §11362.77(a) safe harbour (8 oz dried + 6 mature or 12 immature plants) is arrest protection, NOT a hard cap, insofar as it burdens the Prop 215 defence.
  • Farmer v. Bureau of Cannabis Control & Lori Ajax, No. 19-cv-0597, San Luis Obispo Superior Court — opinion filed 20 November 2020, judgment 11 January 2021, BCC notice 21 January 2021, Judge Ginger E. Garrett; former 15-mile billboard regulation (16 CCR §5040(b)(3)) invalid, BCC exceeded authority, no appeal. Ban does NOT cover highways wholly inside California. Judgment itself still NOT opened — Verify on courts.ca.gov. Secondary evidence now says AB 1302 (2021, restoring 15-mile) was vetoed by Gov. Newsom — still Verify veto date/message on leginfo.
  • City of Riverside v. Inland Empire Patients Health and Wellness Center, 56 Cal.4th 729, filed 6 May 2013: CUA/MMP do not preempt local bans on distribution facilities — doctrinal foundation for §26200, but predates MAUCRSA/SB 1186, do not cite as deciding SB 1186.
  • Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005): federal Commerce Clause criminalisation despite Prop 215 — only that proposition extracted.
  • California v. Cabazon Band of Mission Indians, 480 U.S. 202 (1987): cited only as Round Valley plaintiffs' argument (PL 280 = criminal/prohibitory only; civil/regulatory licensing/tax scheme does not apply to Indians on their own lands) — NOT a holding extracted.
  • Peridot Tree WA, Inc. v. Washington Liquor and Cannabis Board, 9th Cir., 162 F.4th 1179, decided 2 January 2026: Dormant Commerce Clause does not apply to cannabis licence residency requirements (including City of Sacramento scheme) because marijuana remains federally illegal; circuit split with 1st Cir. (Maine, August 2022) and 2d Cir. Variscite, 152 F.4th 47 (2025); cert status as of 1 November 2026 Verify — do not state filed/granted.
  • Not opened, list only, do not summarise: People v. Wayman (2010) 189 Cal.App.4th 215; People v. Brooks (2010) 182 Cal.App.4th 1348; County of San Bernardino v. Mancini (2022) detail; “Catalyst v. DCC” track-and-trace suit (secondary mention only, Verify identity).

  • CDPH emergency regulations DPH-24-005 full chronology: announced 6 September 2024, filed OAL 13 September 2024 (File No. Z-2024-0913-02E), effective 23 September 2024; readopted effective 24 March 2025; regular rulemaking OAL-approved 20 October 2025, further track 21 April 2026. 2024 note that hemp flower was NOT in the emergency is pre-AB 8 — do not carry forward; AB 8 bars California hemp-flower sale from 1 January 2026. Avila Farias during AB 2506's Assembly passage, concluded tribes cannot independently engage in marijuana commerce with licensed businesses without first obtaining their own DCC licence; state law “expressly contemplates tribal participation as California licensees” for off-tribal-lands commerce. 10.4 PL 280 and the Round Valley litigation

PL 280: California is a PL 280 state: state has criminal jurisdiction over offences by/against Indians in Indian Country; state lacks civil regulatory jurisdiction over tribes/tribal territory; cross-deputisation exists. Criminal-vs-regulatory distinction for cannabis is litigated, not settled — present both sides.

Round Valley litigation, James et al. v. Mendocino County et al., N.D. Cal. Case No. 1:25-cv-03736-RMI: raids 22–23 July 2024 (Mendocino/Humboldt sheriffs, CHP) on trust allotments; complaint filed 29 April 2025 (warrant, sovereignty, Fourth Amendment, environmental-enforcement claims); plaintiffs' opposition Doc. 48 filed 19 August 2025; January 2026 ruling (secondary): federal judge dismissed MOST claims, affirmed sheriff authority to enforce criminal laws prohibiting large-scale cultivation on tribal land — full order NOT opened; “most” implies some may survive, do not state “all dismissed”; judge name conflict Illston vs Illman — Verify docket. Tribe's Compassionate Use Ordinance enacted 8 August 2006 (secondary).

10.5 AB 2506 (Hart) — PENDING, not law

As amended 8 April 2026, AB 2506 authorises the Governor to enter an agreement with a federally recognised tribe for commercial activity between DCC licensees and tribal-government licensees if DCC certifies tribal requirements meet/exceed state requirements (SB 1326 framework); PL 280 bars state civil-regulatory monitoring on tribal land without a sovereign-immunity waiver (prior AB 924 drew that pushback). Assembly passage 77–0 reported in secondary — Verify vote/date/Senate status on leginfo; do not state chaptering.

10.6 SB 1326 triggers — why interstate commerce is dormant

SB 1326 (Caballero, Ch. 396, Stats. 2022, BPC §§26080, 26190, §§26300–26308): Governor may enter interstate agreements; §26308 triggers (verbatim from secondary reproductions, Verify on leginfo): agreement “shall not take effect unless one of the following occurs: (1) Federal law is amended to allow for the interstate transfer… (2) Federal law is enacted that specifically prohibits the expenditure of federal funds to prevent the interstate transfer… (3) [DOJ] issues an opinion or memorandum allowing or tolerating the interstate transfer… (4) The Attorney General issues a written opinion [Gov. Code §12519 process] that state law authorization… will not result in significant legal risk to the State of California under the federal Controlled Substances Act…” §26308(b): DCC shall notify Governor + policy committees and post on its website upon a trigger event.

Why dormant: trigger (4) tested and failed (AG 19 December 2023, above); triggers (1)–(3) have not occurred for marijuana. Schedule III medical order does NOT by itself satisfy any trigger on the evidence gathered (it is a DEA scheduling order, AG Order No. 6754-2026 per CDTFA, not a federal-law amendment, expenditure prohibition, or DOJ toleration memo) — but whether a future DOJ memo/AG re-opinion could treat medical-only transfer differently is Verify; do not state Schedule III definitively triggers or definitively does not trigger for medical without an AG/DOJ source. Adult-use: no trigger from rescheduling (still Schedule I).

10.7 Interstate commerce, Dormant Commerce Clause, and the tribal Schedule III question

  • Moving marijuana across state lines is a federal crime even between legal states (secondary summaries consistent with CSA).
  • Peridot Tree + circuit split: see Part 9, §9.6. Oregon Jefferson Packing House interstate challenge voluntarily dismissed without prejudice 17 June 2026 (secondary; reasons including November 2025 federal hemp redefinition and Peridot Tree) — Verify dismissal docket. SHIP Act is introduced, not law — do not present as a route; its second trigger was truncated in extract, do not complete it.
  • Hemp through-shipment ≠ California sale: 2018 Farm Bill §§10113–10114: no state/tribe shall prohibit transportation/shipment through its territory of hemp produced in accordance with federal law; states may regulate production more stringently. California sale rules (CDPH/AB 8) are stricter and govern products sold in California. Post-11 December 2026 federal redefinition narrows compliant hemp — California response Verify. Big Sky Scientific (2019 Idaho seizure) secondary summary is outdated on appeal/Idaho law — do not present as pending in 2026.
  • Transport through California: BPC §26090(f) public-roads protection is NOT an interstate-commerce authorisation. No general right to transport out-of-state marijuana through California located — do not invent one. DCC delivery regulation 4 CCR §15418 text Verify.
  • Tribal Schedule III question — remains Verify, with nuance to print: (a) on-reservation tribal-law operation — not shown to be “state-licensed”; (b) a DCC licence held by a tribal entity for off-reservation/state-market commerce — clearly a state licence, but that is not a tribal-government licence recognised as state-licensed; (c) a hypothetical tribal medical programme seeking Schedule III treatment — Verify, no official source located. Tribal dispensaries found are described as recreational/adult-use, not state-licensed medical programmes. Only (b) is clearly “state-licensed,” and (b) does not answer (c).

10.8 Reciprocity

California does NOT recognise out-of-state medical cards as MMIC (NORML, Leafwell, MMJNow, MMJHealth, LegalClarity — all secondary; cite DCC/CDPH guidance in a future update if it can be opened). Visitors 21+ may buy adult-use within Run 1 limits (28.5g/8g) for use in California; transporting it across state lines remains a federal crime. Home-state card gives NO MMIC sales/use-tax exemption (requires California MMIC + government ID, RTC §6369.6), NO medical-only products, NO medical purchase treatment. Under-21 out-of-state patient exception: none located — Verify. No residency requirement for adult-use purchase located — Verify DCC retail rules if a purchase-residency sentence is needed.

10.9 Federal lands, mail and shipping

  • Federal lands: No California-specific federal-lands cannabis rule located — mark Unavailable/Verify against NPS/DoD/VA/TSA policies; do not list specific parks/bases as prohibited without a source; do not cite the rescinded Cole Memo as current policy without Verify.
  • Mail/shipping (all secondary summaries of carrier terms — Verify against current USPS Publication 52, UPS, FedEx terms; do not cite LegalClarity as carrier authority): mailing marijuana is a federal crime regardless of state legality; USPS lists marijuana as prohibited in domestic mail; UPS prohibits “under any circumstances”; FedEx prohibits cannabis/THC. Hemp/CBD exception under USPS Pub 52 §453.37 (secondary) — record-retention conflict: 2 years vs 3 years in secondaries, Verify Pub 52. Hemp prohibited in international mail including APO/FPO/DPO.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

Date accessed: 1 November 2026. Method limit, stated plainly: no live browser visit was made in the Parts 11–12 research session. Material came from public-web text searches and two Cornell page text fetches (§15417 and §15409). Several regulation texts below are Cornell LII / Rogoway reproductions, not the official Westlaw CCR text — DCC's own “Text of Regulations” page says its document covers regulations in effect as of 1 October 2026 and that Westlaw hosts the official version. Re-check every section and figure against that text before publication-grade use.

11.1 Cultivation operations

Canopy, definitions, premises diagram

  • “Canopy” = the designated area(s) at a licensed premises that will contain mature plants at any point in time, including mature plants used for seed production or research and development (4 CCR §15000(m), Cornell reproduction).
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15000 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • “Cannabis waste” = any material intended for disposal that contains cannabis but is not otherwise hazardous waste; waste consisting solely of plant material is organic waste under Public Resources Code §42649.8(d) (§15000).
  • “Indoor cultivation” = cultivation within a permanent structure using exclusively artificial light, or within any type of structure using artificial light above 25 watts per square foot (§15000).
  • Premises diagram: licensees must maintain at all times an accurate, complete, detailed diagram on record with DCC; no modification without prior DCC approval; the designated responsible party submits Form 27 (Notifications and Requests to Modify a License) with the diagram. For all licence types except temporary cannabis events it must identify/label limited-access areas and all security cameras with assigned numbers (unless exempt under §15315 or §15044).
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/resources/premises-diagram-guidance/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Cultivation-specific diagram items (from the 2024 DCC regulation text): water sources labelled by beneficial use (irrigation, domestic, fire protection, power, fish/wildlife); diversion/groundwater-well/rain-catchment locations with geographic coordinates; maximum wattage/wattage-equivalent of each light; nursery items including designated pesticide/agricultural-chemical storage, composting area if on-site composting, secured cannabis-waste area if different, and canopy areas with dimensions in feet and aggregate square footage if non-contiguous.
  • Source: https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2024/09/20240919_Regulation-Text.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Cultivation plan: §16309 requires licensed cultivators to establish and maintain a cultivation plan. Full §16309 contents were not opened in this session — Verify before listing its required elements.
  • Source (proposed-text extract citing §16309): https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2025/07/2025-01-r_dcc_cultivation_sanitation_15-day_text_7.8.2025.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Pesticides — DPR + county agricultural commissioners

  • No pesticide product is federally registered for use on cannabis. A product can legally be used on cannabis in California only if its active ingredients meet DPR criteria. As of Dec 2024 DPR's list includes about 100 products meeting the criteria and more than 200 vetted products that did not. Products on the list were scientifically reviewed by US EPA/DPR for human health/environmental impacts and registered for use on similar crops, not specifically for cannabis. The list is not a recommendation, endorsement, or exhaustive list.
  • FIFRA §25(b) / 3 CCR §6147 minimum-risk (25-B) products are not scientifically reviewed by DPR and are not on the list, though they may meet the criteria.
  • All cannabis/cannabis products must undergo residual pesticide testing by a DCC-licensed laboratory.
  • Sources: https://www.cdpr.ca.gov/cannabis-cultivation/ and https://www.cdpr.ca.gov/cac-letter/list-of-products-legal-to-use-on-cannabis/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Cannot-be-used criteria (secondary DPR-derived summary, Verify against the current DPR document before publishing): not registered for a food use in California; California Restricted Material including federal Restricted Use Pesticides (3 CCR §6400); on the groundwater protection list. Representative examples listed: aldicarb, carbofuran, chlordane, chlorfenapyr, coumaphos, daminozide, DDVP (dichlorvos), etofenprox, fenoxycarb, imazalil, mevinphos, methyl parathion, paclobutrazol, propoxur, spiroxamine, thiacloprid. Absence from the list does not authorise use.
  • Source: https://overgrow.com/uploads/short-url/AvIt1oTa9K3ZiBz7C9F5eBwa9nc.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary reproduction).
  • County Agricultural Commissioners (CACs) enforce pesticide use laws under Food and Agricultural Code Divisions 6 and 7 and Title 3 CCR: inspections, compliance monitoring/assistance focused on applicators/workers, priority incident/illness investigation, CEQA-compliant pesticide permits, label enforcement. A slide summary states licence applications require a pest-management plan including a signed attestation to contact the appropriate CAC before using pesticides listed in the plan, citing “Sections 8106(a)(3)(C)” — Verify that citation; it was not opened as regulation text.
  • Source: https://slidetodoc.com/pesticides-and-cannabis-an-overview-of-pesticides-as/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary).
  • Pesticide use reporting: DPR determined cannabis meets the definition of “agricultural commodity” in 3 CCR §6000; Food and Agricultural Code Divisions 6 and 7 apply, including reporting under 3 CCR §6626 (not §6627). Site code 26010 is the only site code for cannabis (Hemp/Cannabis); old codes 21003 (Hemp, Fiber Crop) and 70 (Marijuana) were deleted/changed. Forms DPR-PML-025, DPR-PML-017c, DPR-PML-183.
  • Source: https://www.cdpr.ca.gov/cac-letter/site-code-changes-in-pesticide-use-reporting-for-cannabis/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Water, LSA, CEQA (Run 1 anchors — repeat, do not re-derive)

  • Water Boards General Order WQ 2023-0102-DWQ: setbacks Class I 150 ft / Class II 100 ft / Class III 50 ft; Tier 1 >2,000 sq ft and <1 acre.
  • SIUR: up to 6.6 acre-feet/year; diversion season 1 Nov–31 Mar; dry-season forbearance; riparian rights cannot be used for dry-season irrigation; SDURs may not be used for a commercial cultivation licence; Fully Appropriated Stream year-round sources not eligible for SIUR.
  • CDFW LSA: Fish & Game Code §1602 notification before substantially changing bed/channel/bank or diverting/obstructing flow; LSA Agreement or written verification that none is required for an annual licence.
  • CEQA lead agency: local if discretionary, DCC if ministerial/exempt.

Energy / greenhouse-gas reporting — FORMER REQUIREMENTS, REPEAL EFFECTIVE 1 JULY 2026 [AUDIT CORRECTION]

  • Current text as reproduced on Cornell: §16305(a) — from 1 Jan 2023, all holders of indoor, tier 2 mixed-light licence types of any size, and nursery licensees using indoor or tier 2 mixed-light techniques, shall ensure electrical power used for commercial cannabis activity meets the average electricity greenhouse-gas emissions intensity required by their local utility provider under the California Renewables Portfolio Standard Program (Pub. Utilities Code Div. 1, Pt. 1, Ch. 2.3, Art. 16, commencing §399.11). §16305(b) — if the cultivator's average weighted GHG intensity, as calculated/reported on renewal under §15020, exceeds the local utility provider's intensity, the licensee shall obtain carbon offsets for the excess from the previous annual licensed period, from: (1) American Carbon Registry; (2) Climate Action Reserve; or (3) Verified Carbon Standard.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-16305 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Renewal electricity reporting, §15020(e) (from 1 Jan 2022): for each power source on the licensure application for the previous annual period — total electricity supplied by the local utility provider, provider name, and GHG intensity per kWh reported under Pub. Utilities Code §398.4(c) for the most recent calendar year available; total from a zero-net-energy renewable source under §398.4(h)(5) not part of net metering/utility benefit; total from other unspecified sources (§398.2(e)) and other on-site generation (e.g. generators, fuel cells); and average weighted GHG intensity across all sources.
  • PENDING REPEAL — do not present §16305/§15020(e) as settled future law without checking the DCC-2025-01-R outcome: DCC's March 2025 Initial Statement of Reasons for “Commercial Cannabis Cultivation Updates; Minimum Sanitation Standards” (DCC-2025-01-R) proposes repealing §16305 because it “inaccurately relies” on the Renewables Portfolio Standard programme, which applies to energy providers rather than individual operators, and proposes addressing GHG impacts project-by-project at annual licence review under CEQA instead, removing the §15020(e) renewal GHG data collection. The January 2026 fourth 15-day text still reproduced §16305 text, so adoption status at 1 Nov 2026 is Verify against the DCC rulemaking page / OAL register.
  • Sources: https://api.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/03/dcc_cultivation_updates_isor.pdf ; https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2026/01/2025-01-r_cultivation_sanitation_fourth_15-day_text_01.23.2026.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • §16306 (generator requirements, cross-referencing CARB Title 17 standards) was also proposed for repeal in the same package — Verify outcome.
  • DCC's 1 July 2026 regulation update (per DCC 14 Sep 2026 licensee newsletter) “remove[d] redundant/duplicative provisions, streamline[d] operational and administrative burdens, and establish[ed] minimum sanitation standards” — Verify which package this was and whether it changed cultivation section numbers.
  • Source: https://mailchi.mp/cannabis/licenseenewsletter09142026 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Waste, odour, lighting, security (cultivation)

  • Waste, §17223 (Cornell text opened via search): (a) dispose of all waste under the Public Resources Code and other applicable state/local laws; licensee must evaluate waste for hazardous-waste designation under Public Resources Code §40141. (b) Establish and implement a written cannabis waste management plan describing the disposal method(s); disposal only by: (1) on-premises composting; (2) collection/processing by a local agency, a franchised/contracted waste hauler, or a locally permitted private hauler on a regular organic-waste route; (3) self-haul to a staffed, fully permitted solid-waste landfill/transformation facility, composting facility/operation, in-vessel digestion facility/operation, transfer/processing facility/operation, chip-and-grind operation/facility, or a Title 14 §17402.5(d) recycling centre meeting the 90%-inorganic / recycling / organic-to-(A)–(E) conditions; (4) reintroduction into agricultural operation via on-premises organic-waste recycling (e.g. tilling into land, no-till farming). (c) Keep cannabis waste in a secured receptacle/area, access restricted to the licensee, its employees, the local agency, or the permitted hauler. (e) Self-haul only by the licensee/employees and subject to further conditions (truncated in search — Verify §17223(d)–(e) in full).
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-17223 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Rendering rule — narrower than the old “unusable and unrecognizable” slogan: CalRecycle Q&A states the 29 Sep 2021 consolidated regulations narrowed rendering: for cannabis/products disposed of because the batch failed quality standards, the material must be rendered “unusable” before disposal, under video surveillance (except a premises authorised exclusively for cultivation, or the cultivation area of a microbusiness). Pure plant-material cultivation waste is organic waste and is not subject to that rendering requirement; documentation is still required. Do not state a 50/50 grinding/mixing ratio as a DCC rule — that figure appears only in secondary waste guides and was not found in §17223 text opened.
  • Source: https://www2.calrecycle.ca.gov/Docs/Web/121932 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Odour and lighting: no statewide DCC odour standard or lighting standard beyond the premises-diagram wattage disclosure and the 25 W/sq ft indoor definition was located in this session. Odour control is imposed locally (use permits, CEQA mitigation, local ordinances) — state as Unavailable at state-regulation level / Verify locally, do not invent a setback or filtration rule.

11.2 Manufacturing operations

Licence types (DCC manufacturing page)

  • Type 6: non-volatile solvent extraction or mechanical extraction (pressure, heat, cold — no chemicals).
  • Type 7: volatile and non-volatile solvents, mechanical extraction, infusion.
  • Type N: infusion, packaging, labelling (no extraction — see Run 1 Part 4 for full N/P distinction; P = packaging/labelling only in Run 1 usage).
  • Type S: manufacturers in a shared-use facility — extract only with butter/food-grade oils, water, glycerin, vegetable oil, animal fat, or mechanical methods; infusion; packaging/labelling. Shared-use facilities rotate multiple Type S manufacturers on a schedule sharing space/equipment. A Type 6, 7 or N licensee can register all or part of its premises as a shared-use facility.
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/applicants/license-types/manufacturing-license-types/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Type S application extras (DCC checklist): licence number and address of the registered shared-use facility; signed use agreement between primary licensee and Type S licensee; designated area for the Type S licensee and its cannabis storage shown on the premises diagram. Closed-loop applicants must upload a signed closed-loop system certification.
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/applicants/application-resources/application-checklist/application-checklist-manufacturers/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Premises diagram for closed-loop extraction must show the system's location and serial number (§15006(k) in the 2024 draft text).
  • Extraction safety beyond that: no statewide DCC closed-loop/ventilation/fire-code section was opened in this session. Local fire-marshal / California Building and Fire Code approval is the operative safety gate in local examples (e.g. Oxnard §11-484: professional-grade closed-loop system, commercially manufactured with permanently affixed visible serial number, engineer certification to ASME/good engineering practice, fire-department approval, ≥99% purity solvents) — that is a local example, not a state rule. Verify §§17124–17128 (shared-use) and any §172xx extraction-safety section on the current text before publishing state extraction-safety rules. Do not assert federal FDA GMP (21 CFR Part 117/111) incorporation — no citation located.

GMP via PQP / MMP / BPR

  • DCC manufacturing page: manufacturers must follow good manufacturing practices (GMPs) so products are free of contaminants, safe, and produced consistently, demonstrated through a robust quality control programme. The product quality plan (PQP), master manufacturing protocol (MMP), and batch production record (BPR) are all part of that programme: PQP = foundational document per product type identifying/preventing/monitoring/correcting risks that may cause adulteration, misbranding, or testing/QA failure; MMP = organising document of written instructions for each point/step/stage; BPR = separate document per distinct production batch.
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/licensees/manufacturing/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • §17214 PQP (Cornell reproduction, via search): manufacturer shall establish/implement a written PQP for each type of product manufactured at the premises; it shall address hazards that, if not mitigated, may cause adulteration, misbranding, or testing/QA failure; assessment must identify each step from component intake through transfer off the premises, identify preventive measures, and evaluate risks: (1) biological hazards incl. microbiological; (2) chemical hazards incl. radiological, pesticide contamination, solvent/other residue, natural toxins, decomposition, allergens; (3) physical hazards (stone, glass, metal fragments, hair, insects); (4) process failures leading to contamination, allergen cross-contact, packaging/labelling errors, or other quality errors. Preventive examples: cleaning/sanitising equipment/utensils; in-house testing of raw cannabis for pesticides; allergen control programme; homogeneity procedures. Monitoring example: holding temperature-sensitive raw materials below 41°F. Corrective actions must be product-type/risk-specific.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-17214 — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Verify §17214(f)–(h) (corrective-action, verification, records) — truncated in search.
  • Consequences of PQP/GMP failure (DCC newsletters 2026): notice to comply, citation and fine, product embargo or recall, other compliance/licensing actions.
  • §17212 product components (Cornell): manufacturers shall establish/implement written policies/procedures to ensure/maintain component quality; raw materials/components shall not contain microorganism levels rendering the product injurious, or shall be pasteurised/treated so they no longer do; materials susceptible to aflatoxin/natural toxins/pests/extraneous material shall not exceed FDA Defect Levels Handbook (Rev. Feb 2005, incorporated by reference) limits before incorporation; holding/storage must meet HSC §§114047(a)–(b), 114049, 114051.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-17212 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

11.3 Testing operations — Type 8

Required panel (§15714)
