Every representative sample is tested for: (1) cannabinoids; (2) foreign material; (3) heavy metals; (4) microbial impurities; (5) mycotoxins; (6) moisture content and water activity; (7) residual pesticides; (8) residual solvents and processing chemicals; and (9) if applicable, terpenoids. All sample increments must be homogenised before analysis, except foreign-material testing. Results of each analysis are reported on the COA. - Source: https://www.rogowaylaw.com/dcc_regulations/chapter-6-testing-laboratories/article-5-laboratory-testing-and-reporting/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (reproduction of Chapter 6, Article 5).

Action levels — table (all Cornell reproductions, accessed 1 Nov 2026)

Test Section Sample minimum Pass rule
Moisture / water activity (dried flower incl. pre-rolls) §15717 0.5 g Water activity ≤0.65 Aw; report % moisture + Aw + pass/fail on COA
Microbial impurities §15720 1.0 g Inhalable: STEC not detected in 1 g and Salmonella spp. not detected in 1 g and pathogenic Aspergillus (A. fumigatus, A. flavus, A. niger, A. terreus) not detected in 1 g. Non-inhalable: STEC + Salmonella only, same 1 g non-detection. No CFU count — presence/absence.
Mycotoxins §15721 0.5 g Total aflatoxins B1+B2+G1+G2 ≤20 µg/kg and ochratoxin A ≤20 µg/kg; report µg/kg + pass/fail
Heavy metals §15723 0.5 g Inhalable / non-inhalable (µg/g): cadmium 0.2 / 0.5; lead 0.5 / 0.5; arsenic 0.2 / 1.5; mercury 0.1 / 3.0
Residual solvents §15718 0.25 g (product or pre-rolls) Category I (µg/g): 1,2-dichloroethane 1.0; benzene 1.0; chloroform 1.0; ethylene oxide 1.0; methylene chloride 1.0; trichloroethylene 1.0 — LOQ 1.0 µg/g or lower for Category I. Category II (µg/g): acetone 5000; acetonitrile 410; butane 5000; ethanol 5000 (exempt for tinctures; ethanol/isopropyl exempt for topicals); ethyl acetate 5000; ethyl ether 5000; heptane 5000; hexane 290; isopropyl alcohol 5000; methanol 3000; pentane 5000; propane 5000; toluene 890; total xylenes 2170. Note: an older (pre-2021) text split Category II inhalable vs other limits (e.g. hexane 70/290, toluene 30/890) — the current Cornell §15718 text as returned shows a single product/pre-roll column; Verify whether the inhalable split survives in the current official text before republishing split figures.
Residual pesticides §15719 0.5 g 66 pesticides. Category I: not detected above LOD; LOQ 0.10 µg/g or lower for all Category I. Category II: report µg/g on COA, must not exceed specified levels; COA shows pass/fail. Full Category I/II numeric tables were NOT retrieved — Verify final tables. Proposed modified text DCC 2025-03-R (2nd 15-day, 13 Apr 2026) restates the same 0.5 g / µg/g / pass-fail framework — Verify adoption. Source: https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2026/04/2025-03-r-pesticide-testing-04.13.2026-2nd-text.pdf
Cannabinoids §15724 0.5 g Profile must include at minimum THC, THCA, CBD, CBDA, CBG, CBN; LOQ 1.0 mg/g or lower; report % for THC/THCA/CBD/CBDA (dry-weight formula: wet-weight % / (1 − % moisture/100)) and all other cannabinoids as % and mg/g (or mg/mL); below-LOQ reported as “<1 mg/g” (dry) / “<1 mg/mL” (volume). Label-accuracy tolerance: Verify §15724(h) in full. An AB 1027 bill-text summary states edible THC per serving must not deviate from 10 mg by more than 10% after 1 Jan 2022 (±12% before) — bill text, not a confirmed current §15724 quote. Do NOT republish the old BCC-era “15% tolerance / three significant figures” summary as current.
Foreign material §15722 Total representative sample, before homogenisation Examine exterior and interior of dried flower. Pass if: ≤1/4 of total sample area covered by sand/soil/cinders/dirt; ≤1/4 covered by mold; plus (from older text, Verify (e)(3)–(4)) 1 insect fragment, 1 hair, or 1 count mammalian excreta per 3.0 g; ≤1/4 covered by embedded foreign material. Report pass/fail on COA.
Terpenoids §15725 — If applicable / if labelled — Verify §15725 scope (whether terpenoid testing is mandatory only when terpenoids are claimed on the label).

Sources: §15717/§15721/§15722/§15724 via Rogoway Chapter 6 Article 5 (above); §15723 https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15723 ; §15718 https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15718 ; §15720 https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15720 — all accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Independence, ISO, COA, lab shopping

  • Independence: testing laboratory licensees must be independent and third-party-owned; a person holding a testing laboratory licence cannot hold any other cannabis business licence, and lab staff cannot be employed by or hold ownership in any other cannabis licence type (multiple secondary guides agree; BPC §26100 framework). Verify the current Chapter 6 independence section number on Cornell/official CCR before citing a section.
  • ISO/IEC 17025: labs must obtain and maintain ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation, use standard operating procedures, develop a laboratory quality assurance programme, and participate in a proficiency testing programme. AB 1027 (2025–26, pending — see §11.9) would also authorise DCC blind proficiency/performance testing subject to appropriation.
  • Sources: https://californiacannabis.org/testing-lab ; https://californiacannabis.org/licensing/testing ; https://www.distru.com/cannabis-blog/cannabis-testing — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary).
  • Standardised methods: SB 544 (Laird), Ch. 547, Stats. 2021, required DCC to establish standardised cannabinoid test methods for all testing laboratories by 1 Jan 2023 (per AB 1027 analysis). A secondary summary states DCC mandated new standardised methods for dried flower and non-infused pre-rolls effective 1 Jan 2024 and that only 18 of 38 labs were compliant at that point — Verify both; do not use 18/38 as a current lab count.
  • COA (from AB 1027 analysis summaries — Verify against current §§157xx): a laboratory may issue a COA only after completion of all required tests and may not alter a COA after issuance (AB 1027 text summary allows amendment only to correct minor errors as defined by DCC); the lab must upload COAs to CCTT and email a copy directly to DCC within one business day of finishing testing (secondary guide gives the mailbox as testinglabs@cannabis.ca.gov — Verify mailbox; it is a programme mailbox, not a personal contact).
  • Lab shopping / re-sampling ban — DCC distributor checklist (strong finding): once a sample has been obtained from a batch for regulatory compliance testing, a distributor may not arrange for or allow another laboratory to sample or re-sample the same batch unless DCC has given written approval to re-sample and test. The distributor must store the entire batch on its licensed premises until it passes, or fails and a corrective action plan for remediation has been approved by DCC. Cited sections: §§15304, 15305, 15305.1, 15705, 15707, 15708, 17305, 17401.
  • Source: https://cannabis.ca.gov/2025/08/sampling-checklist/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Failed batches, remediation, retest

  • A batch fails when its sample does not meet regulatory specifications. Batches that fail must be destroyed within 60 days of failure unless remediable, per the DCC distributor checklist.
  • Remediation: distributor/manufacturer prepares a corrective action plan and submits it to remediation@cannabis.ca.gov (programme mailbox); DCC approval is required before transport/remediation. If approved, the failed batch must be transported to the approved manufacturer/microbusiness within 30 calendar days of approval. If DCC determines the batch cannot be remediated, the distributor must destroy it within 30 days of that determination (checklist). Verify the 60-day / 30-day deadlines against current §§15306/17305 — checklist and Rogoway reproduction may reflect different amendments.
  • §17305 (Rogoway reproduction): a finished product batch failing any regulatory compliance test shall be destroyed unless remediable by relabelling under §17305(d) or an approved corrective action plan under §17305(e). The manufacturer submitting the plan must be the manufacturer that remediates it and must not receive the batch until DCC approves the plan. Edibles: except relabelling under (d) and repackaging under (f), failing edibles shall not be remediated and shall be destroyed; remediating a failed edible by mixing it with another batch renders the final product adulterated regardless of defect level. Relabelling route (d): a batch failing only for cannabinoid/terpenoid content may be relabelled with correct COA information, provided §17304 THC limits are met. Repackaging route (f): edibles failing because the per-package THC limit was exceeded may be repackaged only under a DCC-approved corrective action plan (conditions not fully retrieved — Verify).
  • Source: https://www.rogowaylaw.com/dcc_regulations/chapter-10-cannabis-and-cannabis-products/article-3-failed-product-batches/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • DCC Form LIC-017, “Corrective Action Plan For Remediation” (rev. 21 Apr 2025): plan must be approved before any remediation/reprocessing/repackaging/relabelling/reformulation/modification; if the product is adulterated, the original manufacturer must conduct a root cause analysis and incorporate preventive measures into its PQP under §17214.
  • Source: https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2025/04/Form-DCC-LIC-017-Corrective-Action-Plan-for-Remediation.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

11.4 Distribution / transport (licensee-to-licensee, not retail delivery)

  • Only persons holding a distributor licence (Type 11, or microbusiness authorised for distribution, or Type 13 transport-only distributor — see below) or their employees may transport between licensees/premises (BPC §26070(b)–(c)). For-hire vehicles require a motor carrier permit (Vehicle Code Ch. 2, Div. 14.85, commencing §34620); CHP has safe-operation authority including the Basic Inspection of Terminals (BIT) programme (VC §34501.12). Distributor licensees shall be bonded and insured at a minimum level established by DCC (§26070(a)).
  • Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/california/code-bpc/division-10/chapter-7/section-26070/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Transport rules (old Title 16 §5311 numbering, reproduced in secondary guides — Verify current Title 4 §15311 numbering/content): transport only inside a vehicle/trailer, not visible/identifiable from outside; locked in a box/container/cage secured inside the vehicle/trailer (trunk counts as inside); no aircraft, watercraft, drone, rail, human-powered or unmanned vehicle; vehicle alarm system at minimum (motion detectors, pressure switches, duress/panic/hold-up alarms also permitted); no leaving a vehicle/trailer with goods unattended in a residential area or parked overnight in a residential area; travel only between licensees shipping/receiving goods and the distributor's own premises when engaged in transport; multiple shipments at once permitted; no one under 21 in the transport vehicle; only the licensee, its employees, or qualified security personnel in the vehicle; no non-cannabis goods except cannabis accessories and licensee branded merchandise/promotional materials; A- and M-goods may travel together only if clearly identified/marked “A”/“M” on physical packaging and on the shipping manifest.
  • Source (secondary guide reproducing the rules): https://www.distru.com/cannabis-blog/cannabis-transportation — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Shipping manifest, §15314 + BPC §26070(e)–(g): before transport, the distributor shall generate a shipping manifest through CCTT; transmit it to DCC and to the receiving licensee before transporting; verify goods taken into possession match the manifest; a manifest shall accompany every transport (physical copy maintained during transport, available on request to DCC/law enforcement); the receiving licensee shall maintain each electronic manifest and make it available on request. A 5 Feb 2025 DCC provisional-licence revocation notice (Clone Wizards / Mendocino Magic Farms) cites failure to generate a CCTT manifest, failure to transmit it to DCC, and failure to have a completed sales invoice/receipt (§15311(b)) and transport from an unlicensed location (§15311(l)) as violations — useful as an enforcement example, not a rule list.
  • Source: https://ca-times.brightspotcdn.com/2f/71/ad4c9a23422aa2e4b3fe3cf37ac8/clone-wizard-mendocino-magic-farms-notice-of-provisional-license-revocation.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Transport vehicle information (§15312) and transport personnel (§15313) and distributor transport-only licence (§15315) were listed by title only in the April 2025 DCC regulations table of contents — contents not opened; Verify. The distributor-transport-only premises exception noted in the Vampa decision (premises on the same parcel as cultivation premises) was truncated — Verify §15315.
  • Distributor QA, §15307 (Cornell, via search): on receiving a COA for regulatory compliance testing, before transporting goods to retailers/microbusinesses authorised for retail, the distributor must ensure: the COA corresponds to the batch; the COA date is less than 12 months old; labelled cannabinoid/terpenoid/Total THC/Total CBD amounts are accurate under §15307.1 if labelled before receiving the COA; goods have not exceeded any expiration/sell-by date provided; all events before receipt of the COA have been entered into CCTT. If goods are not fit for sale, the distributor may arrange a corrective action plan under §17305, with relabelling vs manufacturer-remediation routes distinguished.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15307 — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Check against current text — a 2024 draft CCTT text proposed renumbering §§15306–15307.
  • Type 13 note: a secondary guide states Type 13 is a transport-only distributor that cannot transport to a retailer except immature plants/seeds from a licensed nursery to a licensed retailer/microbusiness. Verify Type 13 scope — Run 1 flagged a Type 12 vs Type 13 microbusiness numbering conflict; SB 128 (2023–24) added “Type 13 — Cannabis event organizer” to codified classifications in one secondary summary, which conflicts with the transport-only usage. Do not assign a Type number to either function until BPC §26050 is checked. This conflict is unresolved.

11.5 Retail operations — RESOLVED items

Access, ID, hours

  • Access, §15400: A-only premises limited to individuals ≥21; M-only premises limited to individuals ≥18 with a valid physician's recommendation, plus individuals ≥21; A+M premises may include both groups.
  • Retail area access, §15402(a): access to purchase only after the retailer/employee has confirmed age and identity under §15404. All sales must take place within the retail area, except delivery, a drive-in/drive-through window authorised by §15025(e), or curbside delivery under §15402(d).
  • ID, §15404 (Cornell text): (a) adult-use sales only to individuals ≥21 after confirming age and identity by inspecting a valid form of identification under (c). (b) medicinal sales only to individuals ≥18 possessing a valid physician's recommendation after confirming age, identity, and recommendation under (c). (c) Acceptable identification: (1) a document issued by a federal, state, county, or municipal government (or political subdivision/agency), including but not limited to a valid motor vehicle operator's licence, containing name, date of birth, height, gender, and photo; (2) a valid Armed Forces identification card with name, date of birth, and photo; (3) a valid passport issued by the United States or a foreign government. No ID-retention or ID-scanning rule was found in §15404 as returned — do not invent one. Expired-ID treatment is not addressed in the section as returned — Verify if needed.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15404 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • MMIC is voluntary for purchase; it is required (with government ID) only for the RTC §6369.6 sales-tax exemption (Run 1).
  • Hours, §15403: retail sales/delivery 6 a.m.–10 p.m. (Run 1 + multiple guides; delivery employees may only receive deliveries during §15403 hours, §15415 summary).

Daily limits — CONFLICT RESOLVED on the regulation text

  • §15409 (Cornell page opened 1 Nov 2026, crawled 19:50 UTC): (a) a retailer shall not sell more than the following to a single adult-use customer in a single day: (1) 28.5 grams of non-concentrated cannabis; (2) 8 grams of cannabis concentrate as defined in BPC §26001, including concentrate contained in cannabis products; (3) 6 immature cannabis plants. (b) A retailer shall not sell more than the following to a single medicinal patient, or a primary caregiver purchasing on the patient's behalf, in a single day: (1) 8 ounces of medicinal cannabis in the form of dried mature flowers or the plant conversion as provided in HSC §11362.77; (2) 12 immature cannabis plants. (c) Notwithstanding (b), if the patient's valid physician's recommendation contains a different amount, the patient may purchase an amount consistent with the patient's needs as recommended by a physician and documented in the recommendation. (d) Adult-use and medicinal limits shall not be combined to allow purchase in excess of any limit in the section. (e) The retailer is responsible for determining that concentrate amounts in manufactured products sold comply with the section.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15409 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • This resolves the prior conflict: the Cova formulation (“8 oz flower AND 8 oz concentrates”) is rejected — §15409(b)(1) allows 8 oz of dried mature flowers or the HSC §11362.77 plant conversion, not a separate 8 oz concentrate allowance. The Cannavine/Flowhub formulation (8 oz flower + 12 plants, recommendation override) is confirmed, with the plant-conversion alternative added. The prior Run 1 note citing “§§15410 et seq.” for purchase limits was wrong — purchase limits are §15409; returns are §15410 (below). Do not cite §15410 for limits.
  • Note: §15409 limits are per retailer per day as written (“shall not sell … in a single day”) — the regulation text as returned does not state a statewide cross-retailer aggregation rule. Do not assert one without a further citation.

Returns, free goods, packaging/exit

  • Returns, §15410 (Rogoway Chapter 3 reproduction): (a) “customer return” = a customer's return of cannabis goods purchased from a licensed retailer, back to that retailer. (b) A retailer may accept customer returns of goods previously sold to a customer. (c) A retailer shall not resell goods that have been returned. (d) Goods abandoned on the retailer's premises shall be treated as a customer return. (e) Defective manufactured products returned by customers may be destroyed or returned to the licensed distributor from whom the goods were obtained.
  • Source: https://www.rogowaylaw.com/dcc_regulations/chapter-3-retailers/all-sections-chapter-3/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Free goods, §15411 (partial): free goods only to a medicinal patient/primary caregiver possessing a valid physician's recommendation or a valid HSC §11362.71 identification card; before providing free goods to a medicinal patient without a §11362.71 card, the retailer must verify the attending physician's licence in good standing with the Medical Board / Osteopathic Medical Board / Board of Podiatric Medicine and keep a copy of the patient's/caregiver's driver's licence or other government-issued identification (extract truncated — Verify §15411 in full).
  • Exit packaging, §15413: all packaging tamper-evident and child-resistant, placed in opaque exit bags (per retailer operating-plan summaries; Verify §15413 text). Retailers may not package/label goods themselves (§15412 prohibition on packaging, per operating-plan summaries).

Delivery — §§15414–15418

  • §15414 non-storefront retailer: authorised to conduct retail sales exclusively by delivery (BPC §26001(o) in the older extract — Verify current BPC lettering); must comply with all retailer requirements except those related to public access and retail area; licensed premises closed to the public.
  • §15415 delivery employees: all deliveries by a delivery employee directly employed by a licensed retailer; each ≥21; all deliveries in person, not through an unstaffed vehicle; deliveries received by customers only during §15403 hours; employee shall return to the licensed premises after the last delivery of the day if any unsold goods remain; during delivery, only delivery and necessary rest/fuel/vehicle-repair stops; during deliveries the employee shall carry a copy of the retailer's licence and government photo ID (extract truncated — Verify full §15415(f)). Retailer shall maintain an accurate list of delivery employees and provide it to DCC on request (§15413 in the same extract — section number to verify).
  • §15415.1 deliveries facilitated by technology platforms: exists as a separate section; all other delivery/marketing/advertising requirements must be complied with (full platform conditions not retrieved — Verify). A Feb 2026 DCC emergency decision (LACC, LLC) cites §15415.1(b)(3) for failure to ensure a third-party technology service provider did not use the licensee's licence number.
  • §15416 delivery to a physical address: only to a physical address in California; shall not leave California while possessing goods for delivery; shall not deliver to an address on publicly owned land or on land/in a building leased by a public agency; older text: “may deliver to any jurisdiction within the State of California provided that such delivery is conducted in compliance with all delivery provisions of this division.” Conflict preserved: read with BPC §26090(f) (public-roads rule, Run 1) and SB 1186 medicinal-delivery protection (BPC §§26320–26325, §12.8 below). Do not present §15416 alone as settling adult-use delivery into a banning jurisdiction. The extract was truncated at “shall not deliver to a school …” — Verify the full school/sensitive-use delivery prohibition.
  • Source for §§15414–15416 extract: https://gardengrove.novusagenda.com/AgendaPublic/AttachmentViewer.ashx?AttachmentID=7560&ItemID=4954 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • §15417 delivery vehicle (Cornell page opened 1 Nov 2026, 19:46 UTC): enclosed motor vehicle operated by a delivery employee of the licensee; no exterior marking/indication the employee is carrying cannabis goods; only the licensee or its retail employees in the vehicle; goods not visible to the public; stored in a secure, fully enclosed trunk inaccessible from inside the vehicle, or in a secured area/compartment within the interior (solid or locking metal partitions, cages, or high-strength shatterproof acrylic; may be comprised on three sides by vehicle body parts if shatterproof and not glass); goods shall not be left in an unattended vehicle unless locked, equipped with an active vehicle alarm, and stored as required; vehicle outfitted with a dedicated GPS device owned by the licensee, for delivery only, permanently or temporarily affixed, active and inside the vehicle at all times during delivery; retailer shall at all times be able to identify geographic location of all delivery vehicles and document location history, maintained ≥90 days and provided to DCC on request; on request, retailer shall provide DCC vehicle make, model, color, VIN, licence plate, DMV registration; any delivery vehicle is subject to DCC inspection and may be stopped/inspected at a licensed premises or during delivery. History: former Title 16 §5417 renumbered to Title 4 §15417, filed 14 Jul 2021 (Register 2021, No. 29); (b)–(c) amended 7 Nov 2022 (Register 2022, No. 45). Authority BPC §26013; Reference BPC §§26070, 26090.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15417 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • §15418 goods carried during delivery (Cornell reproduction, via search): employee shall not carry goods with a value >$10,000 at any time (value = current retail price of all goods carried/in the vehicle); employee may only carry cannabis goods, cannabis accessories, branded merchandise of any licensee, or promotional materials, and may only perform deliveries for one licensed retailer at a time, departing and returning to the same licensed premises before taking possession from another licensee; before leaving, employee must have a delivery inventory ledger of all goods provided, complying with §15049.3; during the trip the ledger must be maintained in hard copy or electronically (including in CCTT), updated after each completed delivery to reflect remaining inventory, updated under §15049.3, and entered into CCTT no later than the end of the calendar day of the trip; on request by DCC or any law enforcement officer, employee must immediately provide: delivery inventory ledgers from departure to request time; all delivery request receipts for goods carried/in the vehicle/already delivered; and the log of all stops from departure to request time; if the employee has no delivery requests to perform for a 30-minute period, the employee shall not make additional deliveries and shall return to the licensed premises.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15418 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Still to retrieve: §15420 delivery request receipt contents (§15418 references §15420 except date/time and customer signature for pre-departure information) and full §15049.3 delivery inventory ledger fields.

Security, video, inventory, records

  • Video surveillance, §15044 (via DCC decisions + manufacturer checklist): digital video system, minimum camera resolution 1280×720 pixels. Does NOT apply to a premises authorised exclusively for cultivation, or to the cultivation area of a microbusiness premises. System shall at all times effectively/clearly record the area under surveillance. Areas: where cannabis/products are weighed, packed, stored, loaded/unloaded for transportation, prepared, or moved within the premises; limited-access areas; security rooms; areas storing a surveillance storage device, with ≥1 camera recording access points to the secured recording area; entrances/exits from both indoor and outdoor vantage points clearly recording activity within 20 feet. Retailers/microbusinesses authorised for retail shall also record point-of-sale areas and display areas; at each point-of-sale location, camera placement must allow recording of facial features of any person purchasing/selling goods, or any person in the retail area, with sufficient clarity to determine identity. Cameras record continuously 24 hours/day at ≥15 frames per second. Recordings shall clearly/accurately display time and date and be kept ≥90 days; licensee must demonstrate ability to easily retrieve/playback footage up to 90 days before inspection.
  • Sources: https://api.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/fd-dcc24-0002149-inv.pdf (Emerald City Distribution, eff. 21 Jul 2025); https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2026/06/2026-05-12-Vampa-Oil-LLC-Final-Decision.pdf (12 May 2026); https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/licensees/manufacturing/cannabis-manufacturer-self-inspection-checklist/ — all accessed 1 Nov 2026. Verify current §15044 text, including the distributor-transport-only self-distribution exception noted in Vampa (premises on the same parcel as cultivation premises).
  • Other security (checklist + secondary inspection guide — Verify each section's subject): alarm system, commercial-grade locks, sign-in/sign-out, perimeter security, designated limited-access areas; checklist cites §§15042, 15042.1, 15044, 15046, 15047, §§15047.1–15052.1 (inventory/track-and-trace), §17218 (manufacturing inventory). Visitors must be identified/logged on entering; limited-access areas where cannabis is stored must be closed except to authorised personnel (secondary guide — use only for the general checklist, not agency structure; it incorrectly describes DCC/CDFA/CDPH pre-2021 roles).
  • Source: https://manzurilaw.com/what-to-expect-from-california-cannabis-agency-inspections/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary).
  • Employee badges: retail/delivery staff wear an employer-issued photo ID badge with business name, licence number, first name, and employee number (4 CCR §15043, per a secondary summary — Verify §15043).
  • Record retention — three clocks, do not conflate: MAUCRSA commercial records 7 years, securely stored, easily accessible on inspector request (contracts with other licensed businesses, sales invoices, environmental compliance, track-and-trace documentation, employee personnel records — Verify the 7-year rule's regulation section; Run 1 cited BPC, not a specific 4 CCR section). Tax records ≥4 years (CDTFA, Run 1). Video ≥90 days (§15044). Delivery GPS history ≥90 days (§15417). Do not cite old §§5037/5044 as current.

11.6 Track-and-trace in detail (CCTT / Metrc)

  • Anchors: §15049 — all cannabis/products assigned a plant or package tag and recorded; activities recorded within 24 hours. §15051 — review at least every 30 days, reconcile, notify discrepancies. §15049.2 — transfers/manifests. New material adds §15047.2 (account creation/recording duties, cited in the Feb 2026 LACC emergency decision), §15049.3 (delivery inventory ledger), and §§15047.1–15052.1 as the current track-and-trace article range in the 2026 manufacturer checklist. Verify whether §§15047–15052 (Run 1) vs §§15047.1–15052.1 (2026 checklist) reflects a renumbering, and quote the current article range.
  • CCTT failures cited as violations in the Feb 2026 LACC emergency decision (examples of what DCC enforces, not a rule list): falsifying business records by recording delivery sales as in-person sales; failing to record receipt of cannabis/products in CCTT within 24 hours; failing to record sales/donations in CCTT within 24 hours; failing to create delivery inventory ledgers before delivery employees leave the premises; failing to record delivery information in CCTT by the end of the calendar day of delivery. Cited: §§15047.2(b) and (d), 15049(b)(1) and (b)(8), 15049.3(a)–(c).
  • Source: https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2026/06/2026-02-23-EDO-LACC-LLC.pdf (LACC, LLC dba Weedyy and CCG Delivery, Licence No. C9-0000683-LIC, emergency decision effective 23 Feb 2026) — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Cultivation recording nuance — flag, do not smooth: a DCC-2025-01-R proposed modified text for §15049.1 states cultivation activities shall be recorded “within three (3) calendar days of occurrence” and harvest-batch information (wet weight immediately after harvest; weight of cannabis waste per harvested plant batch; unique harvest-batch name; initiating date of harvest) shall be reported per harvest batch. That 3-day figure is from proposed text; the general §15049 24-hour rule remains the anchor. Verify whether §15049.1 as adopted uses 3 days for cultivation activities.
  • Source: https://cdn.cannabis.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2025/07/2025-01-r_dcc_cultivation_sanitation_15-day_text_7.8.2025.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Other CCTT points (proposal summaries — Verify whether they became law/regulation): AB 1027 proposal summary states distributors must, beginning 1 Mar 2026, record QA review of each batch in CCTT. Commingled harvest batches are prohibited when the final product is packaged flower (DCC 20 Jan 2026 licensee update summary — Verify regulation section). Before retail sale, distributors/microbusinesses authorised for distribution must arrange representative sampling and regulatory compliance testing under BPC and 4 CCR §§15000–17905 (same update).
  • Still missing: plant/package tag rules (including group-tagging rulemaking DCC-2026-03-R, public comment through 27 Jul 2026 per a June 2026 DCC update — Verify status), harvest-batch rules, waste/destruction recording, transfer manifest fields beyond §15314 (above), distributor storage rules.

11.7 Events

  • Two licences required: (1) cannabis event organiser licence; (2) temporary cannabis event licence per event. Organiser licence alone does not authorise cultivation, distribution, manufacturing, or retail (§15600(c)). Organiser licensees comply with Chapter 1 except §§15006, 15007, 15010, 15019, 15025, 15027, 15034, 15038, 15042, 15044, and 15046–15052.1 (§15600(b)).
  • Source: https://www.rogowaylaw.com/dcc_regulations/chapter-5-cannabis-events/all-sections-chapter-5/ ; https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/licensees/events/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Temporary event licence, §15601: authorises the organiser to hold an event where on-site sale and consumption are authorised at the licensed location on the licensed dates. Single day or up to 30 days [AUDIT CORRECTION: DCC-2025-01-R, effective 1 July 2026]; no licence for more than 4 days. Application submitted no less than 60 calendar days before the first day. If the participant list/diagram changes after application or issuance, the applicant shall submit an updated list and updated diagram (Form DCC-LIC-028, Notifications and Requests Regarding Regulatory Compliance) no less than 72 hours before the event. All employees of participating licensees shall comply with employee badge requirements in §15403 at all times during the event and provide badges to DCC representatives on request (note: the extract cites §15403 for badges — Verify; §15403 is hours of operation elsewhere in this report, so this citation may be an extract error for §15043). All cannabis waste at the event shall be collected/disposed of under §17223. Organiser and all participating licensees shall comply with §15037 and all other applicable record-keeping requirements. Violations may result in disciplinary action against the organiser licence or any participating licensee's licence.
  • Event sales, §15602: only persons ≥21 may purchase and consume at a temporary cannabis event; before selling, the selling licensee shall confirm age and identity using valid identification as specified in §15404. Sales only in the retail area designated in the premises diagram (§15002.1(b)(5)); each sale performed by a licensed retailer, non-storefront retailer, or microbusiness authorised for retail; sales only on the licensed dates at the licensed location and within §15403 hours; goods for sale must comply with retail-sale requirements and §15406; display goods must comply with §15405; goods sold must comply with §15413 (packaging/exit); returns must comply with §15410; daily limits under §15409 apply to all event sales; free goods only if §15411 is complied with. Shipments to the event must be transported by a licensed distributor/microbusiness in compliance with the Act/division; organiser staff must check shipments to prevent prohibited items (alcohol, tobacco) from entering.
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-15602 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Venue/local authorisation: events only at county fairs, district agricultural association events, or venues a local jurisdiction has expressly approved for temporary cannabis events; DCC issues a state temporary event licence only in jurisdictions that authorise such events (BPC §26200(e)(1)(D), Run 1). Alcohol/tobacco sales prohibited at these events (§15601 summaries).
  • Fees (Run 1, confirmed on DCC events page): organiser annual licence — application $1,000; licence fee by planned events: 0–5 events $3,000; 6–10 $5,000; 11–20 $9,000; >20 $20,000 (§15014(d)). Temporary event application $1,000 per event (§15014(a); §15601).
  • Informational/educational events with no sales and no consumption are not required to be licensed by DCC (§15604(a)).
  • Organiser duties (DCC events page): coordinate all aspects — maintaining the event space, hiring security, posting required signs, providing DCC the participant list and a diagram showing layout and participant set-up.
  • Type-number conflict (unresolved): one secondary summary states SB 128 (2023–24) added “Type 13 — Cannabis event organizer” to codified classifications. That conflicts with the transport-only Type 13 usage in §11.4 and with Run 1's Type 12 microbusiness numbering. Do not assign a Type number to the organiser licence until BPC §26050 is checked.
  • Still missing: temporary event premises-diagram full checklist, event consumption-area controls beyond §15602, event sales-tax treatment, and whether organiser duties include on-site consumption-area supervision beyond security/signs/diagram.

11.8 Recalls, embargo, inspections, enforcement, appeals

Voluntary recalls — §17226

  • Licensees shall establish/implement written procedures for recalling goods determined to be misbranded or adulterated; implemented on discovery, or on DCC notification, that one or more batches are adulterated/misbranded.
  • Notification protocols must include: a mechanism to notify all customers that have or could have obtained the goods, including communication/outreach via media as necessary and appropriate; a mechanism to notify any licensees that supplied or received the recalled goods; instructions to the general public and other licensees for return or destruction.
  • Collection/destruction: recalled goods intended for destruction shall be quarantined ≥72 hours unless DCC requests a longer holding time; following quarantine, render unusable and dispose of under §17223.
  • Licensee shall notify DCC of any recall within 24 hours of initiating the recall.
  • A licensed manufacturer may submit a corrective action plan under §17305; if not approved, goods shall be destroyed under §17226(a)(4).
  • History: new section refiled 28 Mar 2022 as emergency, operative 28 Mar 2022 (Register 2022, No. 13); amendment filed 7 Nov 2022, operative 7 Nov 2022 (Register 2022, No. 45).
  • Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/california/4-CCR-17226 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

DCC-initiated recall / embargo — current vs pending

  • Current framework (via AB 1826 analysis summary): when DCC has evidence a product is adulterated or misbranded, DCC shall notify the licensee; on notification, the licensee may voluntarily recall and either remediate or destroy under DCC supervision (BPC §26039.1(a) in the analysis summary). Verify BPC §§26039.1, 26039.3 on leginfo.
  • AB 1826 (Lackey, 2025–26, introduced 11 Feb 2026, amended 19 Mar 2026) is PENDING, not law — do not present its procedures as current. It would: create a third category “administrative error” alongside adulterated/misbranded; require DCC notifications to include supporting documentation/evidence; require a meet-and-confer within 5 business days (rather than current informal proceedings); require DCC to work diligently toward a final decision and remove embargo tags within 24 hours if the product is found compliant; require condemnation proceedings within 10 days of rejecting a corrective action plan, or within 10 days of an embargo if no plan is submitted; for perishable agricultural products, require an ALJ hearing ≤5 business days after the condemnation petition on licensee request and an ALJ decision within 48 hours of hearing conclusion; prohibit DCC from requiring a voluntary recall, liability waiver, or waiver of informal-meeting/hearing/appeal rights as a condition of approving a voluntary recall, authorising remediation, supervising destruction, removing an embargo tag, approving a corrective plan, or permitting destruction.
  • Sources: https://billtexts.s3.amazonaws.com/ca/ca-analysishttps-leginfo-legislature-ca-gov-faces-billAnalysisClient-xhtml-bill-id-202520260AB1826-ca-analysis-395618.pdf ; https://ct3k1.capitoltrack.com/Bills/25Bills\asm\ab_1801-1850\ab_1826_98_A_bill.pdf (backslashes as returned — verify before reusing) — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Consumer-facing recall example (2026, before cut-off)

  • DCC voluntary recall notice, 15 Sep 2026, Cali Lean tinctures: if experiencing symptoms/adverse reactions, contact a physician immediately; check the package for UID and batch numbers in the recall; if they match, dispose of the product or return it to the retailer for proper disposal. Licensees: DCC and/or the distributor will send email notices to all licensees with recalled product in inventory, with instructions. Questions: recalls@cannabis.ca.gov (programme mailbox). Reason given: cannabis used to make the products was produced under unsanitary conditions and methods/facilities/controls did not conform with pesticide statutes and regulations.
  • Source: https://mailchi.mp/cannabis/consumer-advisory-voluntary-product-recall-bjuuy6io4w-17010843 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Recall scale claim — secondary, Verify: a LinkedIn post citing DCC Feb 2025 states DCC's 2024 investigations produced 63 recalls affecting 259 products and removed almost 25,000 units from legal retail shelves. Do not present as official until the DCC Feb 2025 consumer-protection results page is opened.
  • Source: https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/every-cannabis-complaint-public-health-data-point-davidson-ii-mph-casxe — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Inspections, citations, fines — fine-unit conflict PARTLY resolved

  • DCC compliance page (via search text; Run 1 noted this page failed to open in that track): DCC conducts routine inspections of licensed premises; a citation may include a fine — up to $5,000 per violation (licensed person) or $30,000 per violation (unlicensed person); the page directs readers to “chapter 12 of the DCC regulations” for compliance/enforcement actions and to Compliance Action Records and Final Decisions pages.
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/compliance-with-state-law/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (search text only — flag).
  • Secondary summaries (to be verified against §17802): DCC may issue citations containing orders of abatement and fines against a licensee or unlicensed person for any act/omission violating MAUCRSA or regulations (§17802(a)); each citation may contain orders of abatement, monetary fines, or both (§17802(c)); violations classified Minor / Moderate / Serious; Serious violations can result in suspension or revocation; each day of violation is a separate and new violation (Rogoway summary of BPC §26031.5 / §17802); citation must be in writing and describe with particularity a basis for the citation (BPC §26031.5(a)).
  • Sources: https://shaygilmorelaw.com/administrative-law/ ; https://www.rogowaylaw.com/california-state-cannabis-enforcement-actions-and-discipline/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Fine-unit finding: the parent task and a Green Ark secondary summary state “up to $30,000 per violation, per day to an unlicensed person” citing §17802(c)(3), while the DCC page extract says “$30,000 per violation (unlicensed person)” without “per day.” The “per day” element is consistent with the separate-violation-per-day rule in the Rogoway summary, but §17802(c)(3) itself was not opened in this session — Verify the exact unit before publishing “per day.” Present as: up to $5,000/violation (licensed) and up to $30,000/violation (unlicensed), with per-day treatment Verify.
  • Source for the per-day formulation: https://green-ark.vercel.app/articles/california-reports-230m-of-illicit-cannabis-seized-in-2026-with-14-arrests-in-q3 — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary; also reports UCETF seizures to 29 Oct 2026 — a pre-cut-off enforcement example, not a rule).
  • Notice to comply — old-era, Verify current: a McElfresh Law guide (BCC era) states a notice to comply may be given before inspectors leave or mailed within 15 calendar days of inspection, describing violations and giving 20 calendar days to correct and return the signed notice. Do not present 15-day/20-day figures as current DCC rules without opening the current §17801 / notice-to-comply regulation.
  • Source: https://www.criminallawyersandiego.com/blog/has-the-ca-bureau-of-cannabis-control-issued-you-a-citation-what-you-need-to-know/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

Appeals — CCAP

  • DCC FAQ (via search): eligible to appeal — citations (order of abatement and/or administrative fine, BPC §26031.5); disciplinary actions including annual licence suspension, annual licence revocation, and fines (BPC §26031, Gov. Code §11506); emergency decisions and orders (4 CCR §17815, Gov. Code §§11460.10–11460.80). Process: OAH administrative hearing; if the Director/designee adopts the proposed decision (or makes changes not requiring rejection), it becomes a final decision; the appellant may then appeal the final decision to the Cannabis Control Appeals Panel (CCAP). CCAP only accepts appeals from annual commercial cannabis licensees/applicants issued a final decision by DCC (BPC §26043). CCAP is separate from DCC, five appointed members: three by the Governor, one by the Senate Committee on Rules, one by the Speaker of the Assembly. Notice of appeal must be received by CCAP within 30 days after the final day on which DCC may reconsider its decision. Provisional licence revocations/suspensions cannot be appealed to CCAP. Filing a notice of appeal with CCAP triggers an automatic stay — the decision is not enforced while the appeal is pending.
  • Source: https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/cannabis-laws/compliance-action-records/appealing-a-compliance-or-licensing-action-faq/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Secondary additions — Verify on the CCAP site: informal conference under §17803 does not start/extend the formal appeals clock; CCAP must issue its decision within 90 days after the hearing concludes; after CCAP, judicial review is by writ of mandate. Do not publish the 90-day CCAP deadline until verified.
  • Emergency decisions: §17815 and Gov. Code §11460.40 are cited in the Feb 2026 LACC notice for a suspension effective 23 Feb 2026 at 5:00 p.m. — an example of an EDO, not a general rule summary.

11.9 Tax compliance operations

  • Run 1 anchors (carry forward, no new detail asserted): retailer collects excise from the purchaser, separately states it, files a cannabis retailer excise tax return, and includes excise in gross receipts subject to sales tax. Gross receipts include price after discounts, local business tax passed on, own-vehicle delivery, service/payment fees, mandatory packaging/device charges; exclude sales tax itself. Each DCC licence authorising retail sales is registered as a separate location under the excise-tax permit, including a separate medicinal-only licence at the same location as an adult-use licence (CDTFA, opened in Run 1). Separate A or M licences issued to separate entities at the same location each need their own seller's permit and cannabis retailer excise tax permit (CDTFA, opened in Run 1). Retailers must record wholesale cost and retail selling price in CCTT, plus other DCC requirements (CDTFA, opened in Run 1). Records: ≥4 years for tax, 7 years for MAUCRSA (CDTFA page, Run 1).
  • Still missing: CDTFA permit application/renewal steps, filing frequency, late-payment penalties, audit triggers, distributor-to-retailer transition credits for pre-2023 sales, vendor-compensation wind-down (programme ended 31 Dec 2025). Do not fill from secondary tax guides without a CDTFA citation.
  • Pending bills affecting operations — all pending/proposed in summaries found, none established as enacted in this session; check leginfo status before citing:
  • AB 1027 (2025–26): testing/COA/QA-record proposals (see §§11.3, 11.6); would require licensees to provide the COA on request and authorise DCC off-the-shelf testing (see §12.1). Sources: https://ctweb.capitoltrack.com/Bills/25Bills\asm\ab_1001-1050/ab_1027_cfa_385213_asm_comm.html ; https://ctweb.capitoltrack.com/Bills/25Bills\asm\ab_1001-1050/ab_1027_cfa_386655_asm_comm.html (backslashes as returned) — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • AB 1965 (2025–26): testing-panel analysis source: https://billtexts.s3.amazonaws.com/ca/ca-analysishttps-leginfo-legislature-ca-gov-faces-billAnalysisClient-xhtml-bill-id-202520260AB1965-ca-analysis-398914.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • AB 1826: see §11.8.

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

Date accessed: 1 November 2026. No legal advice. Consumer topics in this Part, especially housing, insurance, custody and immigration, rest on secondary guides, not opened statute text, and are labelled as such. Outcomes in custody, housing and immigration matters are fact-specific.

Method limit: same as Part 11 — search extracts and secondary sources, not live official page opens, except where a Cornell page fetch is stated.

12.1 Consumer verification, complaints, recalls, adverse events

  • Verify a licence: DCC licence search explainer (opened in Run 1 Part 5): https://www.cannabis.ca.gov/resources/search-for-licensed-business/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Search tool address cited in secondary material: https://search.cannabis.ca.gov/ — Run 1 flagged live resolution/redirect as Verify. DCC 22 Dec 2025 licensee update advises vetting new businesses by requesting a copy of the DCC licence and verifying it via the Cannabis Unified License Search.
  • DCC public contacts (programme contacts, not personal): info@cannabis.ca.gov and 1-844-61-CA-DCC (1-844-612-2322), from multiple DCC pages.
  • Complaint categories (secondary guide summary — Verify on the current DCC “Submit a complaint” page): a complaint about a business operating without a licence; a licensed business; cannabis goods including packaging/labelling; goods that made you sick. Best contact per the guide: DCC complaint portal, or phone 1-844-61-CA-DCC. Programme mailboxes in DCC materials: recalls@cannabis.ca.gov; remediation@cannabis.ca.gov; licence changes licensechange@cannabis.ca.gov (Run 1, Form LIC-027).
  • Source: https://www.420-packaging.com/Blogs/California-Cannabis-License-Search-A-Comprehensive-Guide.html — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Warning: that source still lists pre-2021 three-agency portals (BCC online.bcc.ca.gov, CDFA CalCannabis Accela, CDPH MCSB) as current. Those are superseded — do not cite them as current portals.
  • Recalled product — consumer steps (DCC 15 Sep 2026 notice, §11.8): if experiencing symptoms/adverse reactions, contact a physician immediately; check UID and batch numbers against the recall; if they match, dispose of the product or return it to the retailer for proper disposal. (Do not use the product, give it away, or attempt home remediation — common-sense safety inferences, not quoted DCC text; label as such if included.)
  • Adverse-event reporting — GAP: no California-specific mandatory consumer adverse-event reporting system distinct from a DCC complaint about goods that made you sick was located. Do not invent a CDPH adverse-event form or a MedWatch-equivalent for cannabis. State as Unavailable/Verify; the DCC complaint route is the located route.
  • Reading a label / COA: label must include GOVERNMENT WARNING (Schedule I wording Verify after Schedule III), product weight/type, active-ingredient list, appellation where applicable, allergen warnings, THC/CBD mg per serving and per package, universal symbol, child-resistant and resealable multi-serving packaging (Run 1, §§17403–17412 numbering Verify). THC limits §17304 (anchors above). COA on request: AB 1027 / AB 1965 would require licensees to provide the COA on request and authorise DCC off-the-shelf testing — proposals, not current law as established in this session. Do not present “retailer must give you the COA on request” as current law until a current regulation or enacted bill is opened.
  • Potency-inflation litigation example — secondary, do not use as a legal finding: Centeno/Wilson v. DreamFields Brands / Med for America (Jeeter), filed 20 Oct 2022, LA Superior Court Case No. 22STCV33980, alleging pre-rolls contained far less THC than labelled. A 30 Jan 2025 DCC voluntary recall for Jeeter/Baby Jeeter pre-rolls was cited in the same secondary summary, reason truncated. Verify the recall notice; do not state the lawsuit's outcome (proposed class action in the summary, not a judgment).

12.2 Driving, impairment, open container — open-container rule RESOLVED

  • No per se THC limit — confirmed in substance, leginfo text still to be opened: California has never set a numeric per se limit for THC or any other drug for driving, unlike alcohol's 0.08% BAC per se standard. Vehicle Code §23152(f) makes it unlawful to drive while under the influence of any drug; §23152(e) prohibits driving under the combined influence of alcohol and a drug; §23153 addresses DUI causing bodily injury. Prosecution must prove actual impairment — that cannabis impaired the ability to drive with the caution of an ordinarily prudent, sober driver — not merely presence of THC/metabolites. Evidence typically relied on: officer observations (driving pattern, speech, coordination, bloodshot eyes, smell, admissions), field sobriety tests, Drug Recognition Expert (DRE) evaluations, blood/urine toxicology. THC and non-psychoactive metabolites can remain detectable for days/weeks after impairing effects wear off, especially in regular users; presence alone does not establish impairment at the time of driving. HSC §11362.45 preserves laws making it unlawful to drive/operate while smoking/ingesting cannabis or while impaired (Run 1 anchor).
  • Sources (secondary guides): https://www.thehfirm.com/penal-codes/vc-23152-f--dui-of-drugs-duid ; https://dui.guru/dui-library/marijuana-dui-california-vc-23152f/ ; https://www.foxlawfresno.com/blog/california-marijuana-dui/ ; https://manshoorylaw.com/blog/california-recreational-marijuana-laws/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Verify VC §§23152(e)/(f), 23153 and VC §312 “drug” definition on leginfo before quoting, and confirm no 2025–26 bill created a THC per se limit. Do not invent any ng/ml figure.
  • Open container — RESOLVED on leginfo text (via search): Vehicle Code §23222(b)(1) — except as authorised by law, a person who has in their possession on their person, while driving a motor vehicle on a highway or on lands described in VC §23220(c), a receptacle containing cannabis/cannabis products (HSC §11018.1) which has been opened, has a seal broken, or loose cannabis flower not in a container, is guilty of an infraction punishable by a fine of not more than $100. §23222(b)(2) — paragraph (1) does not apply if the receptacle or loose flower is in the trunk of the vehicle. §23222(c) — subdivision (b) does not apply to a qualified patient or identification-card holder (HSC §11362.7) if both: (1) the person is carrying a current identification card or a physician's recommendation; and (2) the cannabis/product is in a container/receptacle that is sealed, resealed, or closed. Amended by Stats. 2019, Ch. 610, Sec. 2 (AB 397), effective 1 Jan 2020. Companion rule: VC §23221 — a driver shall not smoke/ingest marijuana or a marijuana product while driving on a highway; a passenger shall not do so while in a vehicle being driven on a highway (both infractions, per secondary summary).
  • Source: leginfo Vehicle Code Division 11, Chapter 12, Article 2 (via search) — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Open the leginfo §23222 page directly before quoting in the final dossier; the search return is the leginfo text but was not a full page open in this session.
  • DUI penalties — secondary only: guides state cannabis DUI carries the same penalties as alcohol DUI (fines, licence suspension, mandatory education, probation, possible jail). Verify penalty provisions (VC §§23536 et seq. for first offences, etc.) on leginfo; do not publish a penalty table from secondary guides.

12.3 Minors, schools, youth protections, packaging, advertising to minors

  • Furnishing to minors — HSC §11361 (leginfo page returned via search): §11361(a) — a person 18 or over who hires/employs/uses a minor in unlawfully transporting, carrying, selling, giving away, preparing for sale, or peddling any cannabis/cannabis product; who unlawfully sells or offers to sell any cannabis/cannabis product to a minor; or who furnishes/administers/gives, or offers to furnish/administer/give, any cannabis/cannabis product to a minor under 14; or who induces a minor to use cannabis/cannabis product in violation of law — imprisonment in state prison for three, five, or seven years. §11361(b) — a person 18 or over who furnishes/administers/gives, or offers to do so, any cannabis/cannabis product to a minor 14 or older in violation of law — three, four, or five years. Amendment note as returned: Amended by Stats. 2025, Ch. 248, Sec. 28 (AB 8), effective 1 Jan 2026. Secondary summaries agree §11361 is a straight felony (not reducible to a misdemeanour in the usual way) and no monetary transaction is required.
  • Source: https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displaySection.xhtml?lawCode=HSC&sectionNum=11361 — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • Medical use by minors — Verify, do not assert: a secondary summary states an exception for minors who use medical marijuana with parental consent. Verify the medical-minor rule (physician recommendation, parental consent, caregiver, MMIC) on leginfo/CDPH before stating it. Do not use it to soften §11361.
  • Public-place and school-zone use — now substantially confirmed by statute summaries (open leginfo §§11362.3–11362.4 before quoting verbatim): HSC §11362.3 does not permit: smoking/ingesting in a public place (except under BPC §26200 locally authorised consumption); smoking where tobacco smoking is prohibited; smoking within 1,000 feet of a school, day care centre, or youth centre while children are present, except in/upon the grounds of a private residence and only if such smoking is not detectable by others on the grounds of the school/day care/youth centre while children are present; possessing/smoking/ingesting in or upon the grounds of a school/day care/youth centre while children are present; manufacturing concentrated cannabis using a volatile solvent (personal-use ban, separate from licensed manufacturing). HSC §11362.4 penalties: smoking in a public place — infraction, fine ≤$100; smoking where tobacco is prohibited, or within 1,000 feet of a school/day care/youth centre while children are present — infraction, fine ≤$250; persons under 18 instead: 4 hours drug education/counselling and up to 10 hours community service (public-place) or up to 20 hours (school-zone/tobacco-ban), over ≤90 days once the programme/opportunity is made available. Consuming on the actual grounds of a school/day care/youth centre while children are present is stated in secondary summaries as a misdemeanour, not an infraction — Verify that sentence in §11362.4 before publishing.
  • Sources: https://legalclarity.org/is-it-illegal-to-smoke-weed-in-your-backyard-in-california/ ; https://oercommons.s3.amazonaws.com/media/editor/92375/Cannabis_regulation_handout_pRR4P0l.pdf — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary summaries of leginfo text).
  • Child-resistant packaging / advertising to minors (Run 1 anchors): BPC §26120(b) — “Packages and labels shall not be made to be attractive to children.” Packaging must be child-resistant and, for multi-serving packages, resealable (Run 1 secondary summaries of §§17403–17412 — numbering Verify). Advertising: BPC §26152(a) prohibits false/misleading advertising; DCC advertising rules prohibit advertising attractive to children/persons under 21 and impose audience/proximity restrictions in secondary summaries — Verify each restriction against current §26152 and 4 CCR §§17400 et seq. before publishing a list. Billboard ban: BPC §26152(d) + Farmer 2021 (highways crossing the California border).

12.4 Public use and consumption lounges / cafés

  • Run 1 anchors (repeat, not re-proved): HSC §11362.3 — §11362.1 does not permit smoking/ingesting in a public place, except under BPC §26200 (locally authorised consumption). AB 1775 (Haney), signed 30 Sep 2024, effective 1 Jan 2025, amends BPC §26200(g): local jurisdictions may authorise retailers/microbusinesses with consumption areas to prepare/sell non-cannabis food, non-alcoholic beverages, host live performances, sell tickets. Permissive only — no state mandate, no separate DCC lounge licence in sources opened; lounges operate under a Type 10 retailer or Type 12 microbusiness with local on-site-consumption authorisation (Type 12 vs Type 13 microbusiness numbering conflict from Run 1 remains unresolved).
  • San Francisco: Board voted 7–4 on 14 July 2026, Mayor signed 23 July 2026, effective 23 Aug 2026, distinct café permit, first year existing licensed retailers/equity partners only, no alcohol/tobacco, on-site only — all SECONDARY in Run 1, Verify on sfgov.org / SF Office of Cannabis ordinance.
  • Palm Springs: lounges already permitted under Municipal Code Ch. 5.55; staff report 8 July 2026 says six operational lounges (Run 1).
  • Still to research: SF café worker protections (N95/respirator choice in secondary SF summary — Verify BPC §26200(g) text), whether café cannabis leaving the premises / delivery bans are state or SF-local, and current SF/Palm Springs café counts as of 1 Nov 2026. Do not update the SF café position from post-1 Nov 2026 sources.

12.5 Workplace — cross-reference, with exemptions now grounded

  • Gov. Code §12954 (Justia reproduction of the 2022 code, via search): (a) unlawful for an employer to discriminate in hiring, termination, or any term/condition of employment, or otherwise penalise, based on: (1) the person's use of cannabis off the job and away from the workplace — this does not prohibit discrimination based on scientifically valid pre-employment drug screening conducted through methods that do not screen for non-psychoactive cannabis metabolites; (2) (per summaries) an employer-required drug screening test that has found non-psychoactive cannabis metabolites in hair, blood, urine, or other bodily fluids. (b) Nothing in the section permits an employee to possess, be impaired by, or use cannabis on the job, or affects an employer's rights/obligations to maintain a drug- and alcohol-free workplace under HSC §11362.45 or any other rights/obligations specified by federal law/regulation. (c) Does not apply to an employee in the building and construction trades. (d) Does not apply to applicants/employees hired for positions requiring a federal government background investigation or security clearance under US Department of Defense regulations (32 CFR Part 117) or equivalent regulations for other agencies. (e) Does not preempt state/federal laws requiring applicants/employees to be tested for controlled substances, including as a condition of employment, receiving federal funding or federal licensing-related benefits, or entering into a federal contract. (f) Operative 1 Jan 2024 (added by Stats. 2022, Ch. 392, Sec. 2 (AB 2188); effective 1 Jan 2023).
  • Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/california/2022/code-gov/title-2/division-3/part-2-8/chapter-6/article-1/section-12954/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • SB 700 (signed 7 Oct 2023, effective 1 Jan 2024): prohibits requesting applicant information on prior cannabis use, and using criminal history on prior use unless the Fair Chance Act (§12952) or other law permits (Run 1).
  • Safety-sensitive clarification (secondary, consistent with the statute text): AB 2188 contains no general “safety-sensitive” exception and no express peace-officer/law-enforcement exemption by name. Law enforcement is NOT expressly exempt — Verify, but do not invent one. The operative carve-outs are: building/construction trades (absolute in the statute); federal background/security-clearance positions; and non-preemption of state/federal testing mandates — which in practice covers DOT safety-sensitive positions under 49 CFR Part 40 (FMCSA CDL/school-bus/hazmat, FAA, FRA, FTA, PHMSA) and federal-contract/funding/licensing testing requirements. FEHA coverage threshold: employers with 5+ employees; employers with ≤4 employees are not covered (secondary — Verify §12940 threshold). A nonprofit religious corporation/association organised primarily for religious purposes is exempt from FEHA generally (secondary — Verify).
  • Sources: https://www.lcwlegal.com/news/ab-2188-and-public-safety-agencies/ ; https://workrightlaw.com/blog/industry-exemptions-california-ab-2188-workers/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary).
  • Schedule III workplace effect: no source opened in this session addresses whether Schedule III changes any §12954 protection — Verify, do not assert a change. Note the tension for Part 12: HSC §11362.45 preserves employer rights to prohibit use, and §12954(b) preserves drug-free-workplace rights — the two provisions must be read together, not as a repeal of either.

12.6 Housing / tenancy

  • State permission is not a tenancy right: Prop 64 legalised adult personal use, but HSC §11362.3 expressly preserves a property owner's right to ban cannabis use on privately owned property, and DCC's consumer guidance states the same (“Property owners can ban the use of cannabis on privately-owned property. If you rent, read your rental agreement”). If a building/city already bans tobacco smoking in residential units, cannabis smoking is automatically prohibited there too via the §11362.3 tobacco-ban linkage.
  • Landlords may prohibit smoking: Civil Code §1947.5 allows landlords to ban smoking on rental premises, including individual units and common areas (secondary landlord guide — Verify §1947.5 text). Guides agree landlords can prohibit cannabis smoking; whether a landlord can ban all consumption methods (edibles, vaping, tinctures) for a medical patient is contested in secondary material and has no settled California appellate answer located in this session — Verify, do not state a ban on non-smoked medical use as lawful or unlawful.
  • No California “cardholder anti-discrimination” housing provision was located: a Tenants Together summary states, in contrast to Arizona's express rule, “No such provisions exist in California,” and that California courts have not spoken clearly on whether eviction of a medical patient for lease-compliant use is discriminatory; tenants who are caught tend to give in to eviction because landlords rely on no-drug/no-smoking clauses and federal-law clauses.
  • Sources: https://legalclarity.org/is-it-illegal-to-smoke-weed-in-your-apartment-in-california/ ; https://www.tenantstogether.org/es/updates/medical-marijuana-what-are-renters-rights ; https://samlaw.net/resources/blog/protecting-your-rental-property-from-cannabis-related-damage-and-liability/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (all secondary; the Tenants Together piece is dated material — treat its “courts haven't spoken” statement as dated, Verify for any post-2020 appellate decision).
  • Federal housing: because marijuana remains federally illegal (adult-use Schedule I; medical Schedule III only for state-licensed medical activity — and HUD's position as summarised pre-dates Schedule III), HUD-assisted housing treats marijuana use as “illegal use of a controlled substance” for federal purposes; HUD has stated it prohibits admission of marijuana users to assisted housing, including medical users, absent a change in federal law, and cannot make reasonable-accommodation exceptions for medical marijuana. Verify HUD's post-Schedule-III position — do not assume the pre-2026 HUD statement still stands unchanged for state-licensed medical patients. Private landlords with FHA-insured mortgages/federal financing may enforce cannabis-free policies to avoid federal-loan conflicts (secondary — Verify).
  • Eviction procedure itself (if a landlord proceeds): California eviction procedure under Code of Civil Procedure §1161 et seq. must be followed precisely; a cannabis-related lease violation without proper notice/justification can support a wrongful-eviction claim (secondary landlord guide — general procedure, not a cannabis-specific rule).

12.7 Insurance (consumer)

  • No California consumer-insurance mandate or cannabis-specific consumer rule was located. Present the following as market/secondary observations, not California law, and do not present any insurer's practice as a rule:
  • Health insurance: typically no coverage for medical marijuana — no federal mandate (including under the Affordable Care Act) requires insurers to cover it, and no state currently mandates private-insurer coverage (secondary 2025 guide). ACA premium factors do not include marijuana use (unlike tobacco smoking, which can raise premiums).
  • Life insurance: underwriting turns on frequency/amount/method and the underlying condition treated; some insurers treat recreational smokers at smoker's rates, others do not treat medical users the same as tobacco smokers if otherwise healthy; honesty on applications matters (misrepresentation is the recurring warning). No California rule fixing a cannabis life-insurance rate was located.
  • Homeowners: whether stolen cannabis, damaged plants, or grow-related damage is covered depends on the individual policy's personal-property limits and exclusions; a 2014 Allstate (Colorado) statement treating marijuana “just like any other personal property not subject to a specific exclusion” is not a California rule and is dated — do not import it as California practice.
  • Auto: a cannabis DUI is penalised like an alcohol DUI (see §12.2); a personal auto policy's “compensated carrying” exclusion can bar coverage for a delivery driver using a personal vehicle on the job — Murphy v. AAA Auto Insurance of Southern California (Cal. Ct. App., decided before the 14 Mar 2025 Carlton Fields summary), where the court held the exclusion barred coverage for a Grassdoor delivery driver's collision, was neither ambiguous nor contrary to public policy on those facts, and noted the employer's Labor Code §2802(a) indemnity obligation. That is a coverage/exclusion case about a delivery employee, not a rule that consumer cannabis use voids personal auto cover.
  • Sources: https://www.insurancequotes.com/insurance-tips/420-cannabis-business-insurance-2025 ; https://www.carltonfields.com/insights/blogs/propertycasualtyfocus/migrated/california-appellate-court-agrees-marijuana-delivery-driver-accident-not-covered-under-personal-auto — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (secondary).

12.8 Parental / custody / child-welfare

  • No legal advice. Present law as published; outcomes are fact-specific.
  • Family Code framework (secondary summaries, Verify §§3011, 3020 on leginfo): custody/visitation are governed by the child's best interest (Fam. Code §3020); §3011 directs courts to consider “the habitual or continual illegal use of controlled substances, the habitual or continual abuse of alcohol, or the habitual or continual abuse of prescribed controlled substances by either parent.” Because adult cannabis use is legal in California, courts as summarised focus not on mere use but on whether use creates a substantial risk of harm — excessive use, use in front of children, use while having physical custody without a sober adult supervising, driving under the influence, second-hand smoke exposure, and insecure storage (especially edibles attractive to children) are the factors repeatedly cited. Mere lawful use, without harm/risk, is treated in these summaries like alcohol use, not as automatic “substance abuse.”
  • Child-welfare investigations: AB 2595 (signed 2022) requires the California Department of Social Services to ensure that when a social worker investigates alleged child abuse/neglect, a parent's/guardian's use or possession of cannabis is treated in the same manner as use/possession of alcohol and legally prescribed medication (secondary summary — Verify AB 2595 codification). Secondary child-welfare commentary states drug use alone, even if illicit, is not supposed to be enough to prove a child is at risk, but that social workers have in practice scrutinised medical-marijuana parents and courts have ordered reductions/cessation, drug tests, meetings, parenting classes, and therapy before restoring custody in individual cases (anecdotal LA County reporting — do not generalise to a rule).
  • Sources: https://shaygilmorelaw.com/intersection-cannabis-law-family-law-california-legal-standards-new-questions/ ; https://www.thegorskifirm.com/blog/2021/june/how-does-recreational-and-medicinal-marijuana-us/ ; https://www.420magazine.com/community/threads/ca-parents-who-use-medicinal-marijuana-face-scrutiny-by-child-protective-services.318230/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (all secondary).
  • Unavailable: a California statute stating that lawful cannabis use alone can never support a custody or dependency finding, and any current CDSS cannabis-specific investigation protocol text. State both as Unavailable/Verify.

12.9 Immigration / federal consequences for consumers

  • No legal advice. Federal law controls immigration consequences even where California use is lawful.
  • General federal framework (secondary/ILRC summaries — Verify INA text before quoting): a conviction for an offence “relating to” a federally defined controlled substance is a ground of inadmissibility and deportability (INA §212(a)(6)(G) / §237(a)(2)(B)(i), 8 USC §§1182, 1227), with a limited exception/waiver for a single incident involving simple possession of 30 grams or less of marijuana. A person is inadmissible (but not deportable on that ground alone) if they make a qualifying admission to an immigration official that they committed a controlled-substance offence. A noncitizen who is or has been a drug abuser/addict after admission is removable under §237(a)(2)(B)(ii) without a conviction, subject to the same 30-gram simple-possession exception. California courts must give the Penal Code §1016.5 plea warning (conviction may result in deportation, exclusion, or denial of naturalisation).
  • ILRC February 2026 chart adds an argument (not a settled holding) that a California cannabis conviction entered on or after 9 Nov 2016 may not meet the federal definition of cannabis and therefore may not be a removal ground — present as an advocate argument, Verify case law, do not present as a safe harbour.
  • Schedule III caveat: the federal Schedule III move covers state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products only. Whether and how it changes any immigration analysis for a state-licensed medical patient — as opposed to adult-use use, unlicensed activity, or industry employment — was not addressed in any source opened in this session. Verify; do not tell any noncitizen that medical use is now immigration-safe.
  • Practical secondary warnings (not rules): noncitizens are routinely advised by immigration practitioners not to admit use to immigration officials, not to obtain a medical card, work in the industry, or publicly promote use until federal law changes — present as practitioner advice, not as California law.
  • Sources: http://ilrc.org/sites/default/files/2026-02/OVERVIEW-Cal-Chart-Internal-Links.pdf ; https://legalclarity.org/california-penal-code-and-immigration-key-laws-and-consequences/ ; https://www.thelawyersforyou.com/blog/is-marijuana-legal-in-california-laws-for-immigrants-and-residents/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026.

12.10 Expungement / record relief

Proposition 64 — HSC §11361.8 (petition route)

  • Prop 64 (2016) created a path for persons with pre-Prop 64 cannabis convictions to have convictions redesignated, dismissed, or sealed, depending on the underlying offence. Offences affected per a law-firm summary: HSC §11357 (possession), §11358 (cultivation), §11359 (possession for sale), §11360 (sale/transport) — each reclassified or dismissed depending on amount, circumstances, and age at the time. Practical first step in that summary: obtain a current California DOJ criminal history record (via live-scan) to see whether automatic relief has already been applied; if not applied to a qualifying conviction, petition under §11361.8.
  • Source: https://www.cocislaw.com/how-to-get-a-drug-charge-expunged-from-your-criminal-record-murrieta-temecula-menifee-lake-elsinore-criminal-defense-law-office-of-nic-cocis/ — accessed 1 Nov 2026. Verify §11361.8 text, eligibility, and redesignation vs dismissal vs sealing distinctions on leginfo before publishing.

AB 1793 (2018) — automatic shift

  • AB 1793, signed by Governor Jerry Brown in 2018, shifted Prop 64 relief from petition-based to largely automatic: DOJ was directed to identify eligible cannabis convictions and apply appropriate relief without individual petitions; DOJ had seven months to review cases and file petitions for reduction/expungement; district attorneys had one year to challenge or grant; persons currently serving time received highest priority; possession with attempt to sell could be reduced from felony to misdemeanour.
  • Conflicting benefit counts — preserve both, pick neither: Judicial Council estimate cited in one summary: 218,000 residents would benefit. A separate secondary summary states nearly 200,000 convictions were dismissed through the state-initiated process. An academic summary states only about 3% of eligible people navigated the original petition process (motivating AB 1793) and that DAs were required to seal records by July 2020, with several counties using Code for America's “Clear My Record” tool.
  • Sources: https://indicaonline.com/blog/california-expungement-bill-passes-for-cannabis-convictions/ ; https://cannigma.com/wp-json/wp/v2/posts/57712 ; https://www.thefreelibrary.com/CHASING+A+CLEAN+SLATE%3a+THE+SHIFTING+ROLES+OF+PRIVACY+AND+TECHNOLOGY...-a0830427597 — accessed 1 Nov 2026 (all secondary). Verify against DOJ/Judicial Council reporting if a total is needed.

Clean Slate — AB 1076 + SB 731 (broader than cannabis — do not conflate)

  • AB 1076 (signed 8 Oct 2019; operative 1 Jan 2021 in one summary, automatic relief implemented July/Aug 2022 in others — date conflict, see below) added Penal Code §851.93 (automatic arrest-record relief) and §1203.425 (automatic conviction-record relief). Initially: people who completed probation without revocation, and misdemeanour/infraction convictions.
  • SB 731 (signed 2022) expanded automatic relief to eligible felony convictions (except those requiring sex-offence registration, with varying waiting periods of one to four years in one summary; serious/violent and registerable offences excluded in another). SB 731 was made effective 1 July 2023 but subject to Budget Act appropriation; AB 134 (10 July 2023) delayed implementation to 1 July 2024; AB 168 (2 July 2024) pushed the operative date to 1 October 2024 for §§1203.425 and 851.93. One secondary source still says “effective July 1, 2024” — the October 2024 date is the better-supported operative date; flag the July/October conflict and Verify on leginfo.
  • Operation (secondary guides, Oct 2026 update): DOJ reviews statewide criminal-justice datasets monthly, identifies eligible records, and grants relief without a petition; eligibility is restricted to records in DOJ's database and to convictions on/after 1 Jan 1973 (per one guide); relief adds a notation and restricts public disclosure — it does not erase the record for criminal-justice agencies (police, DOJ, FBI can still see full history). A person may still petition under §§1203.4, 1203.4a, 1203.41, 1203.42, or 1203.425 if automatic relief was missed in error. Court records of convictions set aside under those sections are automatically sealed (from 1 Aug 2022 per one guide).
  • Broader Clean Slate totals — do NOT present as cannabis totals: a Social Impact Center white paper states California began automatically sealing old misdemeanour records in 2022 under AB 1076 and that in the first six months DOJ data showed 11 million cases sealed, benefiting 6 million individuals. Those are all-offence Clean Slate figures.
